Partial mitigations the auditor undercounts: the sync apply is already two-phase (store_sync_runs/plans stored by runId, schema.ts:72-95, :263-290; apply requires syncRunApplicable and re-reads each product with liveCheck before writing, sync.ts:255-266, apply.ts:609-645), every Hub price write goes through price_ledger (schema.ts:570-600), and rebuild links dry run to apply by auditId (sync.ts:222). What is genuinely missing is per-value undo and via/actor on apply_audit, which matters only if these are brought into scope.

Corrected reading: the gap is real but scoped. If (and only if) sync apply / publish / rebuild are chosen as LLM tools, they must be registered as actions with via, proposal and (where feasible) undo; the existing two-phase run→apply and ledger make this a wiring job, not a design gap. Severity for the exposure question: moderate, conditional on scope, not major. The "would exist equally in a fresh repo" part is reasonable and stands.
  - Corrected claim: The high-blast-radius Hub-side writers (sync apply, publish, rebuild, repair, trash-duplicate, full pull, pricing/taxonomy reset) are server actions outside the vetrina_operations log: they have dry-run, a stored two-phase run→apply with live re-check and a price ledger, but no via/actor on apply_audit, no proposal object and no per-value undo, and they are reachable only through the Authelia session, not the API token. This is a deliberate boundary (docs/operations.md, docs/api.md: the token API exposes only the registered, undoable Vetrina actions), not an accident. It becomes structural debt for LLM exposure only if those operator tools are put in the LLM's scope; the owner's stated examples (menus, categories) fall in the family already covered. If they are put in scope, registering them as actions with via/proposal/undo is a wiring task on existing primitives. Severity for the exposure question: moderate, conditional, rather than major.

#### S5 [contested · major] Every write to WooCommerce re-reads the live store immediately before writing and homepage writes use compare-and-swap, and the single-runner serializes catalog operations; but there is no rate limiting, no cap on open proposals or queue depth, and each proposal performs live Woo reads.

- **Evidence**: Live re-reads: writes.ts:116,168,224,319,590,633,676; remove.ts:70; order.ts:316,335; live-check.ts:345-381 (decideLiveWrites for sync apply, apply.ts:648). Homepage CAS: page.ts:93-96 (checkExpected throws 'stale' 409) and wp-source.ts:160-205 (expected_modified_gmt/expected_attrs_hash/dry_run to the WP plugin). Serialization: runner.ts:269-286 (one promise chain, FIFO) with no length check. propose previews with `{ live: true }` (proposals.ts:43). grep for rate-limit/throttle/429 in src finds only outbound retry handling (adapters/http.ts:32-49, woo/media.ts:262).
- **Implication**: Write correctness under concurrency is already good enough for unattended use. Backpressure is the gap: an LLM in a retry loop can create unlimited proposals (each hitting Woo) and queue unlimited operations behind a 2000-product sale (writes.ts:63-68 notes a 2000-product sale takes an hour or two). Needed additions: per-principal rate limit at `serve`, a cap on open proposals, queue-depth visibility/refusal, and dedupe of identical pending actions. All are additive at one chokepoint.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Checked every cited location plus the whole write surface.

Live re-reads (confirmed): src/server/vetrina/operations/writes.ts:116,168,224,319,590,633,676 each call `readFresh(client, chunk)` right before the write; remove.ts:70 same; order.ts:284/313-323 `staleWrites` re-reads and re-plans (up to REPLANS rounds) before `batchUpdateProducts` at :295, and undoOrder re-reads at :335. apply.ts:645-651 reads `client.getAllVariations` and runs `decideLiveWrites` when `liveCheck` is set, and every non-dry-run caller sets it (actions/sync.ts:272 `liveCheck: !data.dryRun`, scheduler.ts:312, vetrina-prices.ts:74, margin.ts:248/543, store-edits.ts:95). collections/runner.ts:334-346 and :619-626 also read the live index page before `batchUpdateProducts`.

Homepage CAS (confirmed): page.ts:93-96 `checkExpected` throws VetrinaError("stale", ..., 409); wp-source.ts:155-205 send expected_modified_gmt / expected_attrs_hash / dry_run on homepage/block and homepage/layout.

Serialization (confirmed, but mis-cited): the single FIFO promise chain is src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts:28-42 and :74-91 (`s.chain = s.chain.then(...)`, `queued: string[]`), not :269-286 (file is 191 lines; collections/runner.ts:269-286 is unrelated incremental-read code). `grep queued.length` over src/server/vetrina returns nothing: no depth check. live-check.ts:345-381 is also a bad cite (file is 143 lines; `decideLiveWrites` is at :72), but the function exists and does what is claimed.

No rate limiting (confirmed): src/server/api/v1.ts:57-71 `serve` only checks HUB_API_TOKEN and runs the handler; no counter, no per-principal state. Narrow grep for rateLimit|throttle|429 across src finds only outbound handling (adapters/http.ts:32-36, woo/media.ts:262) and comments.

No cap on open proposals / no dedupe (confirmed): proposals.ts:104-109 `propose` parses, previews with `assess(..., { live: true })` (:43) and inserts (:72-93) with no count query and no lookup of an existing open proposal with the same input/digest; `digest` is used only at :157 for staleness on confirm, not for dedupe. POST /api/v1/proposals (src/app/api/v1/proposals/route.ts:14-27) exposes this directly. order-sources apply and catalog-order (src/server/api/order-sources.ts:66, catalog-order.ts:113) call `startOperation` directly, also with no queue check.

Only overstatement: "Every write to WooCommerce re-reads" is literally false for two legacy UI server actions outside the API surface: src/server/actions/store-edit.ts:45 (`batchVariations` price/stock from UI input after only `assertSnapshotIsThisStore`, no live read) and src/server/actions/duplicates.ts:37 (`deleteProduct` decided from the stored snapshot, not a live read); woo/publish.ts:600-610 also replaces the variation set wholesale rather than comparing. None of these are reachable via /api/v1 (routes: actions, catalog reads, homepage read, operations, order-sources, proposals), so the claim holds for the surface an LLM caller would use; the correction is a scope qualifier. Implication and severity stand.
  - Corrected claim: Every write WooCommerce receives through the Vetrina operations path and the /api/v1 surface (hide/show, term moves, sales, remove, reorder, price sync with liveCheck, collections) re-reads the live store immediately before writing (writes.ts:116,168,224,319,590,633,676; remove.ts:70; order.ts:284,316,335; apply.ts:645-651 via decideLiveWrites at live-check.ts:72; collections/runner.ts:334,619), and homepage writes use compare-and-swap (page.ts:93-96; wp-source.ts:155-205). A few legacy UI-only server actions do not (actions/store-edit.ts:45, actions/duplicates.ts:37, publish.ts:600-610 replaces variations wholesale), but they are not exposed via /api/v1. A single FIFO promise chain serializes catalog operations (vetrina/operations/runner.ts:74-91) with no queue-length check. There is no rate limiting at `serve` (api/v1.ts:57-71), no cap on open proposals and no dedupe of identical pending proposals (proposals.ts:72-93,104-109; digest is only used for staleness at :157), and each proposal performs live Woo reads (proposals.ts:43 `{ live: true }`). The only 429/rate-limit handling in src is outbound retry (adapters/http.ts:32-36, woo/media.ts:262).
- **Skeptic** (refuted, medium): The facts hold (no rate limiter at serve, no proposal cap, FIFO chain with no length check: src/server/api/v1.ts:57-62, src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts enqueue, proposals.ts propose/insert). The implication and severity do not survive the surrounding code:

1. A proposal is near-inert and bounded. `propose` writes nothing to Woo (proposals.ts:100-105); its live reads are a sample of at most SAMPLE=20 products, LIVE_READS=4 at a time, and only sale-type previews read live at all — every other kind previews from the store index "the fast way" (preview.ts:41-52, uses of ctx.live at :172, :199, :216). Open proposals are DB rows that expire after PROPOSAL_MINUTES=15 (src/lib/vetrina/proposals.ts:12; proposals.ts insert expiresAt) and are trimmed at 30 days. So "an LLM in a retry loop can create unlimited proposals (each hitting Woo)" costs ~20 reads per call, not writes; a cap on open proposals buys almost nothing for store safety.

2. Writes need a second, separately guarded step. confirmProposal (proposals.ts:122-175) claims the row by CAS (open->confirming, :144-150), is idempotent on re-confirm (:127-128; docs/api.md "a retry never runs it twice"), re-previews and refuses as `stale`/`refused` when the shop changed. The run itself re-reads live and files unchanged products as `already` (writes.ts:116-125, 212-235), so even two identical queued actions do not double-write — the auditor's own "live re-reads" evidence already covers "dedupe of identical pending actions" at the write level; what remains is wasted reads, not incorrect writes.

3. The 2000-product-sale evidence is misread. writes.ts:63-68 says that one-after-another a 2,000-product sale "would take an hour or two" — the counterfactual the code avoids with SALE_AT_ONCE = 4. The comment documents a mitigation, not a current hour-long block.

4. "Per-principal rate limit" does not fit the design: there is exactly one principal, HUB_API_TOKEN (v1.ts:57-61, proxy.ts:17-21). Any abuse is self-inflicted by the operator's own single token holder. A global limit at `serve` is a 10-line addition, which the auditor itself concedes ("additive at one chokepoint") — inconsistent with "major".

5. Queue visibility partly exists: each operation view carries `queued` (service.ts:64 via runner.isQueued) and GET /api/v1/operations lists running/queued actions, so a caller can see the backlog.

Residual truth: there is no backpressure, and a flood of confirms can queue many operations behind each other; the Woo client has outbound 429/backoff (adapters/http.ts:32-49) but no inbound throttle. That is a minor hardening item, not a readiness blocker, and it says nothing about keep-vs-rewrite: the chokepoint already exists. Severity should be minor.
  - Corrected claim: Writes re-read the live store and skip products already at the target state, homepage writes use compare-and-swap, proposals require a separate CAS-claimed idempotent confirm, and the runner serializes catalog operations (sales 4 products at a time, writes.ts:63-68). There is no inbound rate limit at `serve` and no cap on queue depth or open proposals, but proposals write nothing, expire in 15 minutes and read at most a 20-product sample (sale kinds only), and duplicate queued actions cost reads rather than double writes; queue position is already visible via the `queued` flag. A single global rate limit (there is one principal, HUB_API_TOKEN) and an optional queue-depth refusal at `serve` are minor additive hardening, not a structural gap or a reason to extract the core into a new repo.

#### S6 [refuted · strength] Input validation is comprehensive on the API surface: every /api/v1 body and query is parsed with Zod, action inputs come from the same Zod unions that generate the published JSON Schema, and ids are UUID-checked; bounds cap a single action at 2000 products, sale 1-90%, margin 0-200%.

- **Evidence**: registry.ts:225-232, 248-266 (JSON Schema via z.toJSONSchema from OperationInputSchema/PageActionInputSchema), :269-273 (parseAction). v1.ts:42-54 (UUID guards), :67 (ZodError -> 400). Routes: catalog-order.ts:36-40,77-87; order-sources.ts:51-54; operations/route.ts:13; catalog/terms/route.ts:14. Bounds: input.ts:68-70 (MAX_OPERATION_PRODUCTS, catalog-ops.ts:20 = 2000), :90 (Percent 1-90), :96-97 (margin), :137,142,153.
- **Implication**: Fit for an LLM caller as-is: tool schemas and server validation share one source of truth, so a model cannot be handed a shape the server will not check. This is a strong argument to extract the registry + input schemas as the core of any package rather than re-specifying them in a fresh repo.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): Confirmed parts: registry.ts:225-232 builds per-kind schemas from OperationInputSchema/PageActionInputSchema options and :248-266 emits JSON Schema with z.toJSONSchema; :269-273 parseAction dispatches to the same Zod parsers; proposals.ts:105-107 uses parseAction. v1.ts:42-54 UUID-guards operation/proposal ids (order-sources/[id]/route.ts:8-13 has its own copy; apply/route.ts reuses operationId), v1.ts:67 maps ZodError to 400. Zod on queries/bodies: catalog-order.ts:36-41 (TermQuery), :77-89 (OrderBody); order-sources.ts:51-55 (ApplyBody); operations/route.ts:13; catalog/terms/route.ts:14; reads.ts:82-96 (ProductsQuery). Bounds: input.ts:90 Percent 1-90, :93-98 margin via catalog-ops.ts:144-145 (0-200), :66-71 ids <= MAX_OPERATION_PRODUCTS (catalog-ops.ts:20 = 2000).

Counterexamples that make the claim literally false: (1) "every /api/v1 body is parsed with Zod" — POST /api/v1/order-sources is not: src/server/api/order-sources.ts:21-49 (uploadFrom/upload) does hand-rolled JSON.parse + typeof checks and falls back to passing the raw request text straight to putSource (service.ts:135-146); its bounds are ad-hoc slices/clips (MAX_SOURCE_NAME 80, origin slice 500, format.ts:33-34 clip SKU 64/name 300, MAX_SOURCE_ITEMS 20000 at format.ts:356), not a schema. Also OrderBody.ids/skus (catalog-order.ts:82-83) carry no array length cap; idsOfSkus (:92-98) resolves an unbounded SKU list before the 2000 cap applies at :105 (Next bodySizeLimit is 50mb, next.config.ts:16). (2) "bounds cap a single action at 2000 products" — only for ids targets and non-whole-term query targets. A whole-term hide/sale/unsale (termKey set) is capped at MAX_TERM_PRODUCTS = 50,000 (context.ts:33-36, :41-43; catalog-ops.ts:28), and for query targets the 2000 cap is enforced at resolve time (context.ts:34-36), not at parse time, so nothing in the published JSON Schema reflects it. (3) registry.ts:260 uses unrepresentable:"any", so refinements (wholeTerm, endsOn not in the past, ids tooMany superRefine) are dropped from the published schema; the server is stricter than the schema, which supports "the server checks everything the schema offers", but the schema is not a full statement of what is accepted. The JSON Schema also only covers the action union (/actions, /proposals); OrderBody, ApplyBody and the query schemas are Zod-validated but not published.

The implication (one source of truth for action schemas, worth extracting) stands for the /proposals path; the claim as written overstates coverage and the product cap, which matter for an autonomous caller's safety model (one call can hide or discount up to 50,000 products via a whole-term action).
  - Corrected claim: Input validation on /api/v1 is strong but not universal: every query string and all action-taking bodies (proposals via parseAction, catalog/order via OrderBody, order-sources/{id}/apply via ApplyBody) are parsed with Zod, action inputs come from the same Zod unions that generate the published JSON Schema (refinements such as whole-term consistency, future endsOn and the ids cap are not representable and are dropped from that schema, so the server is stricter than what it publishes), and operation/proposal/source ids are UUID-checked. The exception is POST /api/v1/order-sources, whose body is hand-parsed (raw text accepted) and bounded only by ad-hoc clips (name 80, origin 500, 20,000 items). Bounds: sale 1-90%, margin 0-200%; a single action is capped at 2000 products for id lists and ordinary query targets (the query cap applied at resolve time, not in the schema), but a whole-term hide/sale/unsale may act on up to 50,000 products, and OrderBody's ids/skus arrays have no length cap before SKU resolution.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, medium): The factual core mostly holds, but two facts in the claim are wrong and the implication ("fit for an LLM caller as-is", "one source of truth so a model cannot be handed a shape the server will not check") overreaches.

Confirmed: every /api/v1 route either parses with Zod or funnels into parseOperationInput/parseAction (src/app/api/v1/*/route.ts; src/server/api/catalog-order.ts:77-89 OrderBody, :111-116 re-parsed through parseOperationInput; src/server/api/order-sources.ts:51-54 ApplyBody). UUID guards at src/server/api/v1.ts:42-54; ZodError->400 at :67. Percent 1-90 at src/server/vetrina/operations/input.ts:90; margin 0-200 at :93-97 with src/lib/vetrina/catalog-ops.ts:144-145. JSON Schema is generated from the same unions at src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.ts:225-266.

Wrong fact 1 — the 2000 cap is not universal. src/server/vetrina/operations/context.ts:33-36 uses MAX_TERM_PRODUCTS for whole-term hide/sale/unsale (wholeTermAction, :41-43), and src/lib/vetrina/catalog-ops.ts:28 sets MAX_TERM_PRODUCTS = 50_000. A single `{kind:"hide", termKey:"category:N", target:{kind:"query",...}}` action can touch 50k products. The Zod-level 2000 check (input.ts:68-70) applies only to `ids` targets; `query` targets are capped only at runtime.

Wrong fact 2 — the published JSON Schema is NOT the shape the server checks. z.toJSONSchema silently drops every .refine/.superRefine. I ran zod 4.4.3 from node_modules on an equivalent schema: the output kept minItems/pattern but lost the superRefine ids cap and the object-level refine entirely. So the exported tool schema lacks: the 2000-ids cap (input.ts:68-70), termId-requires-taxonomy (:57), wholeTerm/termKey consistency (:109-121), EndsOn's valid-day/not-past checks (:80-88), OrderBody's "ids or skus" (catalog-order.ts:89). Direction is safe (server checks more than the schema), but "share one source of truth" is false in the sense that matters for an LLM tool: a model following the schema faithfully will still get 400s, and the tool definition must re-state those rules in prose (only partially done via .describe at input.ts:75).

Implication overreach — shape validation is not a safety model. For an autonomous caller the relevant properties are the proposal/confirm two-step, dryRun, undo and caps, not Zod. Yet POST /api/v1/catalog/order (catalog-order route :26-40) and POST /order-sources/{id}/apply write immediately (202) without the proposal step, and the `delete` action (registry.ts:141-154, WordPress trash) is reachable with the same single flat HUB_API_TOKEN (v1.ts:57-61) with no scopes or per-action gating. So "fit for an LLM caller as-is" does not follow from comprehensive input validation.

What survives: the recommendation to extract registry + input schemas as the core rather than re-specify them is sound — the Zod unions are a real asset and the registry already produces an MCP-shaped listing. Severity should be "strength, with gaps to close" rather than an unqualified strength.
  - Corrected claim: Input validation on /api/v1 is thorough: every body/query is Zod-parsed or routed through parseOperationInput/parseAction, ids are UUID-guarded, sale is 1-90% and margin 0-200%. However, the product cap is 2000 only for id-list targets and non-term query targets; whole-term hide/sale/unsale actions are capped at 50,000 (context.ts:33-36, catalog-ops.ts:28). The published JSON Schema (z.toJSONSchema) drops all refine/superRefine rules (ids cap, termKey/target consistency, termId-needs-taxonomy, endsOn date rules, "ids or skus"), so it is a lossy superset of what the server checks, not a single source of truth; an LLM tool definition must restate those rules. Validation alone does not make the API fit for an autonomous caller as-is: /catalog/order and /order-sources/apply write without the proposal/confirm step, and `delete` is reachable with the same unscoped bearer token. The Zod unions + registry are still the right core to extract, with the refinements and the write-path gating added around them.

#### S7 [contested · major] The codebase is strictly single-tenant: no table carries a shop identifier, shop identity is the process's env (WOO_BASE_URL/keys, one HUB_API_TOKEN, one DATABASE_URL), and the site-guard exists precisely because two shops sharing a database would corrupt each other.

- **Evidence**: site-guard.ts:289-296 ('Every table here is one shop's state with no shop column ... two installs sharing a Postgres plan one shop against the other's snapshot'); schema.ts grep for shop_id/shopId/tenant: 0 hits; env.ts:44-46,53,154; docs/auth.md:8-11 (per-shop separation done by Authelia groups and hostnames); wp-source.ts:35-38 (WP plugin auth reuses the Woo consumer key).
- **Implication**: A single LLM-facing middleware serving several shops is not a configuration change here; it requires a tenant dimension across ~25 tables, per-tenant Woo credentials, per-tenant tokens, and tenant-scoped runner/scheduler state (currently globalThis, runner.ts:236). If the target is one-Hub-per-shop with the LLM pointing at the shop's own Hub, the current model is adequate and nothing blocks exposure. If multi-shop is required, this is the single largest argument for 'extract the core and rebuild the persistence/runtime shell' over 'keep', because tenancy is cross-cutting rather than additive.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Checked each cited location in /home/user/woo-hub. (1) src/server/woo/site-guard.ts is 32 lines; the quoted comment is real but at lines 18-20, not 289-296: "Every table here is one shop's state with no shop column, so two installs sharing a Postgres plan one shop against the other's snapshot and write with the other shop's variation ids." assertSnapshotIsThisStore (l.23-31) throws "Two shops are sharing one database — nothing was written" on mismatch, confirming the guard's purpose. (2) src/server/db/schema.ts has 24 pgTable definitions; grep for shop_id/shopId/tenant/store_id/site_id over src returns no column hits (only TopBar.tsx:104 ShopIdentity UI component and a local Map name in preview.ts:192). The one shop-identifying column is store_snapshot.site_url (schema.ts:181) on a single-row table whose id defaults to "current" — it records which shop the whole database belongs to (used by the guard via getSnapshotInfo, site-guard.ts:12; written by pull.ts:198), not a per-row tenant discriminator. So "no table carries a shop identifier" is literally slightly off, but the correction is trivial and does not change the implication. (3) src/lib/env.ts:44-46 WOO_BASE_URL/WOO_CONSUMER_KEY/WOO_CONSUMER_SECRET, l.53 HUB_API_TOKEN (single optional string), l.154 DATABASE_URL — all single process-level values; VETRINA_HOST at l.136 is also a single hostname. (4) docs/auth.md:8-11 confirms per-shop separation is done by Authelia groups and per-shop HUB_HOST/VETRINA_HOST, i.e., one Hub install per shop. (5) src/server/vetrina/wp-source.ts:35-38 builds Basic auth from WOO_CONSUMER_KEY/SECRET for the wc-gh/v1 WP plugin API. (6) runner.ts is 191 lines; the globalThis runner state is at l.39 (comment l.19-22), not l.236; catalog.ts:542,756 and words.ts:22 also keep process-global state. Substance of the claim stands; only the line numbers for site-guard.ts and runner.ts are wrong, and store_snapshot.site_url should be acknowledged as a whole-database shop marker rather than a tenant column.
  - Corrected claim: The codebase is single-tenant: none of the 24 tables in src/server/db/schema.ts has a tenant/shop column (the only shop identifier is store_snapshot.site_url on the single-row "current" snapshot, which marks which shop the whole database belongs to); shop identity is the process's env (WOO_BASE_URL/keys at env.ts:44-46, one HUB_API_TOKEN at env.ts:53, one DATABASE_URL at env.ts:154, one VETRINA_HOST at env.ts:136); per-shop separation is done outside the app by Authelia groups and per-shop hostnames (docs/auth.md:8-11); and the site-guard (site-guard.ts:18-31) exists precisely because two shops sharing a database would corrupt each other. Runner/scheduler state is process-global on globalThis (runner.ts:39, catalog.ts:542).
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The facts hold (src/server/woo/site-guard.ts:17-31 says every table is one shop's state with no shop column; src/lib/env.ts:44,53,154 are one WOO_BASE_URL, one HUB_API_TOKEN, one DATABASE_URL; src/server/collections/runner.ts:36,95 keep runner state on globalThis). But the implication and severity do not follow. (1) Single-tenancy is a documented, deliberate deployment model, not an oversight: docs/deploy.md:309 ('Run a single app container per shop'), docs/deploy.md:361-382 ('A second shop on the same server' — a second stack with its own env, database, scheduler and COMPOSE_PROJECT_NAME, routed by hostname behind one Caddy and one Authelia), docs/hub-and-plugin.md:90 ('One Hub per shop'), docs/auth.md:8-11 (per-shop groups). Multi-shop is already supported today as process-per-shop; it is a configuration/deployment change, not a schema change. (2) For LLM exposure the natural multi-shop shape is the LLM-side client (MCP server/tool router) holding N {hubUrl, token} pairs, one per shop Hub — exactly what the existing /api/v1 is built for: docs/api.md:3 describes it as 'for a script, another service or an assistant', bearer-token gated per install (src/server/api/v1.ts:58-61). The auditor's own text concedes 'if the target is one-Hub-per-shop ... nothing blocks exposure'; that IS the repo's stated target, and nothing in the user's request asks for a single multi-tenant process. (3) Hence 'the single largest argument for extract-and-rebuild' is built on an unstated requirement; a fresh repo would face the same choice and process-per-tenant is a legitimate answer for a handful of shops (docs/auth.md:27-31 sizes the system at one VPS and a few shops). The site-guard is a mitigation that makes misconfiguration fail safe, not a sign of a design flaw. Severity 'major' is wrong; this is at most an informational note that in-process multi-tenancy is out of scope by design.
  - Corrected claim: The codebase is single-tenant per process by explicit design: no table carries a shop id, shop identity is the process env (WOO_BASE_URL/keys, HUB_API_TOKEN, DATABASE_URL), and the site-guard (site-guard.ts:17-31) makes a misconfigured shared database fail safe. Multiple shops are already supported as one stack per shop behind shared Caddy/Authelia (docs/deploy.md:309,361-382; hub-and-plugin.md:90), so an LLM-facing layer serves several shops by holding one {hub URL, HUB_API_TOKEN} per shop and calling that shop's /api/v1 — a client-side configuration, not a schema or runtime rewrite. In-process multi-tenancy would be a cross-cutting change, but it is not required for LLM exposure and is not an argument for rebuilding the repo. Severity: informational/minor.


<a id="state"></a>
## State, runtime and operational coupling

### Summary

The persistent state is in good shape for lifting out: all 23 tables in src/server/db/schema.ts are plain Postgres, keyed by stable ids, with cursors for long jobs (store_pull_runs, store_sync_runs), a claim column on media_jobs, and an advisory-locked single-row store snapshot; none of it is tied to Next. The runtime is not: the whole background engine (daily sync, 15-min feed cycle, orders pull, 5-min store-index check, photo queue) is an in-process scheduler started from src/instrumentation.ts inside the Next server, and its state plus six more coordinators live on globalThis with no DB lease or queue. Specifically, the Vetrina operations runner (the thing POST /api/v1/proposals/confirm hands work to) is a per-process promise chain: a confirm filed by the API only runs if the same process that accepted it stays alive, an operation whose process dies is marked "interrupted" and never resumed, and two processes would serialize nothing against each other. The collections runner, apply-job map, taxonomy cache, publishing Set, homepage-presence cache and store-terms cache are the same pattern; the docs explicitly require "a single app container per shop". Only the media queue (FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED + locked_until) and the snapshot writes (pg_advisory_xact_lock) are multi-process safe. What an LLM caller would read has two layers with different freshness: the store_index (one row per product, light taxonomy facts; refreshed every 5 min incrementally by date_modified, fully once a day, and patched from each write's answer) backs every /api/v1/catalog read and the operations' plans, while the store_snapshot (one jsonb blob of the whole store, 9.5 MB for 3 000 products, ~140 MB for 20 000) is the pricing/sync truth and is only re-read whole by the 04:30 daily pull. The index is explicitly documented as "may be minutes behind" and the snapshot as "up to a day old", which an API caller cannot currently see except via snapshot uploadedAt and store_index seen_at. Memory is a known operating hazard: the README measures the dev server climbing to the 4 GB heap limit, and the repo carries a heap watch, a heap-snapshot route and several SQL-side rewrites that exist only to avoid deserializing the snapshot blob. The practical implication: the domain code can run as a separate long-lived Node service sharing Postgres, but only if that service is the one and only process running the scheduler, the operations runner and the collections runner, and the Next UI stops importing them; alternatively the coordination must be moved from globalThis into Postgres rows (a lease/claim per job as media_jobs already does). The four /api/cron routes and scripts/trigger-cron.sh already prove the pull/feed/refresh/media steps can be driven from outside the process, but no cron route exists for the Vetrina operations runner, the collections check or the orders pull.

## In-process scheduler (src/server/scheduler.ts, 870 lines)

Started from `src/instrumentation.ts:7-13` (`register()` when `NEXT_RUNTIME === "nodejs"`), i.e. inside the Next server process. Enabled when `SCHEDULER=on` or NODE_ENV=production (`scheduler.ts:842-843`). Guard against double start is the in-memory `s.started` flag only (`:847`).

| Job | Cadence | Steps / notes | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| Daily run | `SCHEDULER_TIMES` default 04:30 Europe/Rome | pull, gs, kicksdb, storeSync, collections (full read), repair, backfill, recategorize | `:97`, `:363-463`, `:690-706` |
| Catch-up | 60 s after boot if last slot not `ok` in `scheduler_runs` | `loadHistory` marks `running` rows `interrupted` on boot | `:517-558`, `:698-701` |
| Retry | +1 h, max 2 | failed steps only | `:77-78`, `:673-683` |
| Feed cycle | `SCHEDULER_FEEDS_MINUTES` (15 if GS configured) | gs, feedSync; skips turn if `s.running` | `:98`, `:712-726`, `:593-596` |
| Orders pull | `SCHEDULER_ORDERS_MINUTES` default 15 | setTimeout chain | `:729-747` |
| Collections check | `SCHEDULER_COLLECTIONS_MINUTES` default 5 | `runCheck()` then `queueSweep()` | `:767-785` |
| Photo queue | every 60 s idle, 1 s while `more`; 4 min budget, concurrency 2 | `drainMedia`, paused during pull | `:91-95`, `:791-822` |
| Heartbeats | after a fully successful run/cycle | `SCHEDULER_HEARTBEAT_URL`, `SCHEDULER_FEEDS_HEARTBEAT_URL` | `:577-585`, `:648` |
| Retention | after each daily run | `history.prune()` (7 d feed cycles), `pruneRunLogs()` (7 d sync runs, 90 d logs) | `:495-509`, `:561-574`; `src/server/retention.ts:22-24` |

Mutual exclusion between daily run and feed cycle is the in-memory boolean `s.running` (`:593-607`). No DB lease.

| Route | maxDuration | Does |

| `POST /api/cron/pull-store` | 800 s | `runFullPull()` (`src/app/api/cron/pull-store/route.ts:24`) |
| `POST /api/cron/refresh-catalog` | 800 s | up to 50 rounds x 100 SKUs KicksDB (`refresh-catalog/route.ts:9,27`) |
