Not covered by any cron route: store sync writes (`syncStore`), collections check/full, orders pull, repair/backfill/recategorize, the Vetrina operations runner / sweep. `src/proxy.ts:117-118` exempts `/api/cron/*` and `/api/v1/*` from the Authelia header check.

## In-memory singletons that assume one process (all `globalThis`, non-test)

| # | Singleton | File:line | What it holds | Multi-process consequence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `__storeHubScheduler` | `src/server/scheduler.ts:170-206` | enabled/running flags, next/last run times, step counters | two processes = two schedulers, every sync runs twice (README.md:385, docs/deploy.md:309-310) |
| 2 | `__vetrinaOperations` | `src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts:39-43` | `chain: Promise`, `queued: string[]`, `running`, `recovered`, `sweep` | operations serialized only within one process; a restart marks running rows `interrupted`, "never resumed" (`:19-22`, `:57-69`); `isQueued()` (`:46-50`) is read by `service.ts:64` to label API status |
| 3 | `__storeHubCollections` | `src/server/collections/runner.ts:95-111` | `chain`, `running`, `queued`, `confirm: Set`, `refused: Map`, `readFrom`, `incremental`, `lastFullAt` | `readChanges` falls back to a full store read whenever `lastFullAt == null` in this process (`:251`); Vetrina writes queue through `queueProductWrite` (`:173-181`) to avoid racing the decision |
| 4 | `__storeHubApplyJobs` | `src/server/sync/apply-job.ts:40-41` | Map of running/finished live applies, 1 h retention | "a restart loses the job" (`:16-17`); `startApplyJob` returns null if another is running in this process only (`:74-76`) |
| 5 | `__storeHubTaxonomyCache` | `src/server/woo/taxonomy-cache.ts:21-22` | attributes/brands/categories/tags/terms promises, 5 min TTL | "one app container per shop" (`:16`); a term created by another process is invisible for up to 5 min |
| 6 | `__storeHubPublishing` | `src/server/woo/publishing.ts:14-15` | Set of SKUs mid-publish | duplicate-parent guard; "this process is the whole picture" (`:10`); media `claim()` excludes these SKUs (`media.ts:168-178`) |
| 7 | `__vetrinaCatalogPresence` | `src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts:542-583` | homepage sections cache, 60 s TTL, 2.5 s wait | `forgetHomepagePresence()` is called after each runner job (`runner.ts:86`) only in that process |
| 8 | `__vetrinaCatalogFilling` | `src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts:756-763` | boolean "first full read under way" | catalog overview reports 0 products until this process's read finishes |
| 9 | `__vetrinaSiteFeatures` | `src/server/vetrina/powers.ts:59-65` | WP plugin capabilities, 5 min TTL | benign |
| 10 | `__storeHubStoreTermsV2` | `src/server/pricing/store-terms.ts:480-481` (TTL `:129` 60 s) | store index terms view for pricing rules | benign staleness |
| 11 | `__vetrinaOpsLanguage` | `src/server/vetrina/operations/words.ts:22-23` | AsyncLocalStorage locale | per-request, fine |
| 12 | `__redis` / `cacheSingleton` | `src/server/cache/redis.ts:7-20, 48-53` | ioredis client; falls back to `MemoryCache` on error (`:27-46`) | only consumer is `src/server/actions/preview.ts:357` (KicksDB answers); Redis is optional, not a coordination primitive |
| 13 | `__pgPool` | `src/server/db/client.ts:11-14` | pg Pool (globalThis only in dev) | fine |
| 14 | demo-mode state `__vetrinaDemo`, `__vetrinaDemoTerms`, `__vetrinaDemoLogSince` | `fixture-source.ts:80`, `collections/fixture-client.ts:28`, `operations/standing.ts:28` | in-memory fake shop | demo only |
| 15 | module-level `started` | `src/server/debug/heap-watch.ts:35` | heap watch interval | benign |

Multi-process-safe primitives already present:
- `media_jobs` claim: `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` + `locked_until = now + 10 min` (`src/server/woo/media.ts:31, 166-189`).
- Snapshot writes: `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('store-hub:store_snapshot'))` in a transaction (`src/server/store-json/repo.ts:34-39`).
- Pull cursor in `store_pull_runs` (`pull.ts:76-88, 113-180`); `startPull` resumes an existing `running` row, but the scheduler refuses to drive a pull updated within 2 min (`scheduler.ts:80, 369-373`) and `pullInFlight` uses a 10 min staleness window (`pull.ts:68-70`) — heuristics, not a lease.
- `vetrina_operations.status` is in Postgres, so a second process can *read* progress; it just cannot pick up the work.

## Store snapshot vs store index (what an LLM caller would read)

| | `store_snapshot` | `store_index` |
|---|---|---|
| Shape | 1 row, whole store as one jsonb `data` (format `woo_rest_pull`), `product_count`, `uploaded_at` (`schema.ts:179-187`; `pull.ts:194-202`) | 1 row per product, light columns + taxonomy jsonb (`schema.ts:503-536`) |
| Refreshed whole by | daily pull at 04:30 (`scheduler.ts:364-380`), `/api/cron/pull-store`, Sync tab | daily `runFull` (`collections/runner.ts:485-495`) |
| Refreshed incrementally by | `patch*` after every Hub write (publish, apply, Vetrina), under advisory lock, products stamped `hub_patched_at` / `as_of` (`store-json/repo.ts:41-59, 92-101`) | 5-min `runCheck` via `modified_after` with 10 min overlap, max 20 pages then full read (`runner.ts:44-47, 248-287`); every write's answer upserted (`:336, 384`); `freshen()` on catalog read triggers a check if >2 min since last (`collections/service.ts:171-181`) |
| Staleness documented | "up to a day old between two daily pulls" (`scheduler.ts:261-262`); `saveSnapshotKeepingPatches` keeps products patched after pull start (`repo.ts:179-237`) | "the index may be minutes behind" (`catalog.ts` line 19 of the 700-745 window); stores that ignore `modified_after` fall back to daily only (`runner.ts:263-270`) |
| Freshness signal available | `getSnapshotInfo().uploadedAt` (`repo.ts:326-344`) | `indexInfo()` -> `newestModified`, `lastSeen` (`collections/repo.ts:273-281`) |
| Consumers | pricing/sync plans, publish, rebuild, repair, export, duplicates (`repair.ts:84`, `actions/export.ts:54`, `actions/duplicates.ts:30`, `actions/debug.ts:37,98,189` load it whole; most other paths were rewritten to SQL-side jsonb queries, `repo.ts:250-358`) | Vetrina catalog reads (`catalog.ts:40-55`), `/api/v1/catalog/*` via `src/server/api/reads.ts:9-10`, operations plans, pricing store-terms (`store-terms.ts:442-456`) |
| Liveness at write time | sync re-reads each product live before writing (`scheduler.ts:258-263`, `src/server/woo/live-check.ts`) | decisions re-read live in chunks of 25 before writing (`runner.ts:315-338`) |

Deployment: `Dockerfile:40-55` builds Next standalone, runs migrations then `node server.js`; `deploy/docker-compose.yml:116-171` one `app` service + postgres + redis (256 MB LRU, no persistence, `:191-195`); `docs/deploy.md:3` "One VPS runs the whole Store Hub", `:309-310` "Run a single app container per shop". Redis is used only as a KicksDB answer cache.

## Memory / heap

- README.md:45-66: dev server climbs ~14 MB/round to the default 4 GB heap limit ("Ineffective mark-compacts near heap limit"); production build flat at ~56 MB on a 400-product store.
- `src/server/debug/heap-watch.ts` (73 lines, started from `instrumentation.ts:11-12`), on in dev, `HEAP_WATCH=on` in prod; `src/app/api/debug/heap/route.ts` GET stats / POST v8 heap snapshot (dev only).
- `store-json/repo.ts:250-263`: deserializing the snapshot was "9.5 MB of JSON for a 3000-product shop, several times that as a live object graph" per render; `:350-358`: "140 MB of JSON for a 20 000-product shop"; `schema.ts:64-71` plans paginated by run_id for the same reason. Remaining whole-blob readers listed above (repair, export, duplicates, debug).
- `buildReimport` still `structuredClone`s the whole model (`store-json/patch.ts:54`).

## Counts
- 23 tables; 28 migrations; 26 server-action modules in `src/server/actions/`; 4 modules in `src/server/api/` (reads, catalog-order, order-sources, v1); 22 API route files (4 cron, 14 under /api/v1, health, dock, publish, debug/heap).
- 15 distinct `globalThis` singletons (table above), 2 of them (`media_jobs`, snapshot lock) backed by Postgres-level coordination.

### Claims and verdicts

#### S1 [contested · blocker] The operations runner that executes confirmed proposals is a per-process in-memory promise chain with no persisted queue, so an API-driven action only runs if the process that accepted it stays alive, and two processes would not serialize against each other.

- **Evidence**: src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts:19-22 ('A restart loses the queue: what it left running is marked interrupted, never resumed'), :39-43 (state on globalThis: chain, queued, running), :72-89 (enqueue chains promises), :57-69 (recover() marks rows interrupted). src/server/vetrina/operations/service.ts:79-105 (startOperation inserts the row then calls queueRun in-process). service.ts:64 derives the API's 'queued' flag from in-memory isQueued().
- **Implication**: Blocker for running the domain as a separate service alongside the Next UI unless exactly one process hosts the runner and the UI/API stop calling queueRun directly; for keep/extract the fix is the same (a DB-leased worker like media_jobs), for a fresh repo it is a design input: operations need a claimable job table, not a promise chain.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Every cited location is literally true. src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts:19-22 (doc comment: state on globalThis, "A restart loses the queue: what it left running is marked interrupted (recover()), never resumed"); :28-43 (RunnerState {chain, queued, running, recovered, sweep} stored on globalThis.__vetrinaOperations); :72-89 (enqueue pushes to s.queued and chains s.chain = s.chain.then(...), pure in-memory promise chain, no DB write of a queue position); :57-69 recover() calls repo.markInterrupted. repo.ts:424-442 markInterrupted does `update vetrina_operations set status='failed' where status='running'` (and likewise for 'undoing') with no process/worker identity or lease column, so a second process calling recover() would mark the first process's genuinely in-flight rows as failed — not just "no serialization" but active interference. service.ts:79-105 startOperation inserts the row (default status 'running', schema.ts line with default("running")) then calls queueRun(row.id) in-process at :104; service.ts:64 derives `queued` from in-memory isQueued(). The API paths do reach this: src/server/vetrina/operations/proposals.ts:168 (confirmProposal -> startOperation), src/server/api/order-sources.ts:66 and src/server/api/catalog-order.ts:113 (startOperation(..., "api")), and src/app/api/v1/operations/[id]/undo/route.ts -> undoOperation -> queueUndo (service.ts:173). Counterexample search: the only cross-process serialization in the repo is pg_advisory_xact_lock in config/repo.ts:132, overrides/repo.ts:54, store-json/repo.ts:36 (unrelated tables), and the media_jobs claim/lease in woo/media.ts:30,165-194 and schema.ts:451 — confirming the implication's reference point exists but is not used for vetrina_operations. No lease, FOR UPDATE, or SKIP LOCKED on vetrina_operations anywhere. Claim stands as written; the only refinement is that recover() in a second process would actually fail the first process's running rows, which strengthens rather than weakens it.
  - Corrected claim: The operations runner that executes confirmed proposals (and API-started operations and undos) is a per-process in-memory promise chain on globalThis with no persisted queue or lease: an API-driven action only runs if the process that accepted it stays alive, two processes would not serialize against each other, and worse, recover() in any newly started process marks every row with status 'running'/'undoing' as failed regardless of which process owns it (repo.ts markInterrupted has no worker identity), so a second process would actively fail the first's in-flight operations.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, medium): Facts confirmed, and slightly worse than stated. runner.ts:39-43 keeps chain/queued/running on globalThis; :72-89 serializes via promise chaining; :57-69 recover() calls repo.markInterrupted, which at repo.ts:424-441 flips EVERY row with status "running"/"undoing" to "failed" with no owner, lease or lockedUntil column. So two processes would not merely fail to serialize: the second process's first startOperation/listOperations (service.ts:79 'await recover()') would mark the first process's in-flight job as interrupted while it is still writing to Woo. The API already routes through this runner today (src/app/api/v1/operations/[id]/undo/route.ts -> undoOperation -> queueUndo; src/app/api/v1/proposals/route.ts POST), and operationView's 'queued' flag at service.ts:64 is in-memory isQueued().

The implication and severity are where the claim overreaches. (1) The constraint is not specific to operations nor newly introduced by LLM exposure: the whole app is already documented and deployed as exactly one process per shop (docs/deploy.md:309 'Run a single app container per shop: the syncs run inside it'; Dockerfile standalone server; scheduler.ts:168-170 uses the same globalThis pattern). Any LLM/MCP exposure that hosts the tool surface inside that one Next process, or as a thin client of the existing /api/v1 routes, inherits the already-accepted single-runner guarantee and hits no new blocker; the auditor's own wording concedes this ('unless exactly one process hosts the runner'), which is precisely today's deployment. (2) The 'separate service alongside the Next UI' scenario the blocker rests on is one architecture option the auditor assumed, not something the middleware question requires. (3) The remedy pattern already exists in-repo: media.ts:166-187 claims jobs with FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED + lockedUntil, so moving operations onto a leased job table is a bounded, known change, not an architectural unknown; for a fresh repo it is an ordinary design input. (4) Durability of accepted work ('only runs if the process stays alive') is partly mitigated: the row is inserted first (service.ts:93-102), recover() records the interruption with how far it got and what was filed, and the row stays undoable (runner.ts:52-55, repo.ts:432) — a restart loses progress visibly rather than silently, which an LLM caller can read back via GET /api/v1/operations/{id}.

A competent engineer would therefore classify this as a hard single-instance constraint to carry into the design (and a must-fix before any multi-process topology), not as a blocker for exposing the middleware to an LLM. Severity should be 'major / design constraint', with the implication scoped to multi-process deployments.
  - Corrected claim: The operations runner is a per-process in-memory promise chain on globalThis (runner.ts:39-89) with no persisted queue or lease; recover() (runner.ts:57-69, repo.ts:424-441) marks every 'running'/'undoing' row failed on process start, so a second process would not only fail to serialize but would also mark the first process's in-flight actions interrupted. This is a hard single-instance constraint, already documented and accepted for the whole app (docs/deploy.md:309; the scheduler uses the same pattern), not a blocker for LLM exposure: tool/MCP access hosted in the same Next process or as a client of the existing /api/v1 operations and proposals routes inherits today's single-runner guarantee. It becomes a blocker only if the domain is split into a separate service alongside the UI or scaled to several processes; the fix (a claimable job table with lockedUntil + FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, as media_jobs already does in src/server/woo/media.ts:166-187) is a bounded change for keep/extract and a design input for a fresh repo. Severity: major design constraint, not blocker.

#### S2 [contested · major] The background engine (daily sync, feed cycle, orders pull, collections check, photo queue) runs inside the Next.js process via instrumentation.ts, and its mutual exclusion is an in-memory boolean; the docs require a single container per shop.

- **Evidence**: src/instrumentation.ts:7-13 starts startScheduler(); src/server/scheduler.ts:170-206 (globalThis state), :593-607 (s.running gate), :847 (s.started guard), :824-870 (five timers armed). README.md:385 'every sync runs once per instance'; docs/deploy.md:309-310 'Run a single app container per shop'.
- **Implication**: The UI and the domain cannot be scaled or deployed independently today. Extracting the core means moving the scheduler out with it (it is a thin orchestrator over server modules, so this is mechanical) and persisting the running/started gate in scheduler_runs, which already records slots and interrupted runs.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Every cited location checks out literally. src/instrumentation.ts:7-13 — register() dynamically imports "@/server/scheduler" and calls startScheduler() when NEXT_RUNTIME === "nodejs", so the engine boots inside the Next.js server process. src/server/scheduler.ts:169-206 — state lives on globalThis.__storeHubScheduler (comment at 167-168 explains instrumentation and the server-action bundle each get a module copy, so globalThis is the shared store); SchedulerState includes `started: boolean` and `running: boolean`. :593-607 — run() checks `if (s.running)` (feed cycle skips, daily waits up to WAIT_FOR_IDLE_MS then gives up), then sets `s.running = true` at 607 and back to false at 623; this is a plain in-memory boolean, not a DB lock. :847 — `if (s.started) return; s.started = true;` guards double-arming. :824-870 — startScheduler arms five timers: boot(s) → arm() daily (659), armFeeds (716), pullOrders (867, rescheduling itself at 745), workMedia (868/820), checkCollections (869/783). README.md:384-385: "Run a single instance, or every sync runs once per instance." docs/deploy.md:309-310: "Run a single app container per shop: the syncs run inside it, so a second copy would run every sync twice." scheduler_runs (schema.ts:395-411, status enum includes "interrupted") is written by history.open/close (scheduler.ts:470-491) and loadHistory marks leftover "running" rows "interrupted" at boot (:517-520), as the implication states.

Counterexamples looked for and found only as nuances, not refutations: (a) the `s.running` boolean gates only the daily run and feed cycle; orders pull, collections check and photo queue run on their own timers with no mutual exclusion against each other or against run() (only workMedia defers while a store pull runs, and the store pull itself is tracked in a DB row store_pull_runs, woo/pull.ts:66-84). (b) The in-process scheduler is optional: SCHEDULER=off plus CRON_SECRET exposes /api/cron/{pull-store,sync-goldensneakers,refresh-catalog,media} routes for an external scheduler (README.md:387-390, src/app/api/cron/*/route.ts), so the single-instance requirement is a consequence of the default mode, not an absolute. (c) Some write paths already use Postgres advisory locks (config/repo.ts:132, overrides/repo.ts:54, store-json/repo.ts:36), so cross-instance safety is partly present at the repo layer. None of these contradict the claim's literal statements; they only qualify the implication, so the claim stands with those qualifications.
  - Corrected claim: The background engine (daily sync, feed cycle, orders pull, collections check, photo queue) runs inside the Next.js process via instrumentation.ts (startScheduler() on NEXT_RUNTIME=nodejs), and the mutual exclusion between the daily run and the feed cycle is an in-memory boolean (s.running on a globalThis store); the orders pull, collections check and photo queue have no shared gate at all. The docs require a single container per shop in the default mode (README.md:384-385, docs/deploy.md:309-310). Mitigations already present: SCHEDULER=off plus CRON_SECRET lets an external scheduler drive the jobs through /api/cron/* routes (still served by the Next.js process), the store pull is tracked in a DB row (store_pull_runs), and config/overrides/snapshot writes take Postgres advisory locks; scheduler_runs already records slots and marks interrupted runs, so persisting the running gate there is a natural extension.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The facts hold: src/instrumentation.ts:7-13 calls startScheduler(); src/server/scheduler.ts:170-206 keeps state on globalThis, :592-607 gates on an in-memory `s.running`, :847 guards double-start with `s.started`, :824-870 arms the timers; README.md:382-385 and docs/deploy.md:309-310 say to run one instance. The implication ("UI and domain cannot be scaled or deployed independently today; extraction requires moving the scheduler and persisting the gate in scheduler_runs") and the "major" severity do not follow.

1. Separation already exists by configuration, not by refactor. README.md:382-395 documents `SCHEDULER=off` + `CRON_SECRET` and four authenticated endpoints (src/app/api/cron/{pull-store,refresh-catalog,sync-goldensneakers,media}/route.ts), each of which calls the domain module directly (e.g. pull-store/route.ts imports runFullPull from @/server/woo/pull; media/route.ts calls drainMedia). startScheduler() returns before arming anything when SCHEDULER is not on (scheduler.ts:845-847). So today you can run N UI/API replicas with SCHEDULER=off and one scheduler instance (or none, driven by external cron). The "single container" rule in the docs is the consequence of the default being on, not an architectural inability to split.

2. The in-memory `running` flag is not the system's mutex over the domain, so persisting it is not a precondition for exposing the core. UI server actions (src/server/actions/publish.ts:49, feeds.ts:47) and the cron routes never consult `s.running`; they only read status for display. The real cross-driver guards are already in the database: store_pull_runs with `pullInFlight` (src/server/woo/pull.ts:68-87, resume-or-reuse of the single running pull), the scheduler itself checks the DB for a Sync-tab pull before pulling (scheduler.ts:369-373) and syncStore defers to an in-flight pull (scheduler.ts:275-279), and the media drain pauses on the same DB check (api/cron/media/route.ts:30-31). An LLM/MCP tool layer would call the same server modules the UI and cron routes call and inherit these DB-level guards exactly as the UI does now.

3. For the middleware/LLM question the scheduler is orthogonal: the auditor concedes it is a thin orchestrator over server modules. Whether it stays in the Next.js process or moves to a worker does not change the surface an LLM would call (the domain modules / the /api/cron-style entry points). The only genuine residual gap is scheduler-vs-scheduler overlap when two instances both have SCHEDULER=on, which is already mitigated by documented configuration and by the DB pull guard; it is a minor operational note, not a major blocker.

Correct framing: minor. The `s.running`/`s.started` flags only serialize the scheduler's own jobs within one process; cross-process coordination for the heavy operation (the store pull) is already DB-backed, and the UI/API tier is already separable from the scheduler via SCHEDULER=off + /api/cron.
  - Corrected claim: The background engine runs inside the Next.js process via instrumentation.ts, and the scheduler's own mutual exclusion is an in-memory boolean on globalThis. However, the UI/API tier is already separable from it today: with SCHEDULER=off the scheduler arms nothing, and the same steps are exposed as authenticated /api/cron endpoints that call the domain modules directly (README.md:382-395), so extra replicas can run alongside one scheduler instance or an external cron. The in-memory gate only serializes scheduler jobs within one process; cross-driver coordination for the store pull is already persisted in store_pull_runs (pull.ts:68-87, scheduler.ts:369-373, 275-279), and UI actions and cron routes never depend on the gate. Exposing the core to an LLM would reuse the same module surface and DB guards; moving the scheduler out is optional and mechanical. Severity: minor.

#### S3 [refuted · major] Fifteen globalThis singletons exist; only two coordination points (media_jobs claim and snapshot writes) are Postgres-backed and therefore safe for more than one process.

- **Evidence**: grep of globalThis: scheduler.ts:170, operations/runner.ts:39, collections/runner.ts:95, sync/apply-job.ts:40, woo/taxonomy-cache.ts:21, woo/publishing.ts:14, vetrina/catalog.ts:542 and :756, vetrina/powers.ts:60, pricing/store-terms.ts:480, operations/words.ts:22, cache/redis.ts:7, db/client.ts:11, plus three demo-mode ones. Safe ones: src/server/woo/media.ts:166-189 (FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, locked_until), src/server/store-json/repo.ts:34-39 (pg_advisory_xact_lock).
- **Implication**: The pattern for a multi-process-safe core already exists in the codebase (media_jobs); applying it to the operations runner, collections runner and apply-job is the concrete work either option (a) or (b) must do. The caches (taxonomy, presence, store-terms, site features) are TTL-bounded and only cause seconds-to-minutes of staleness across processes.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): Checked every cited line and grepped for counterexamples.

Singleton count: `grep -rn globalThis src` yields 16 distinct globalThis-held stores, not 15: the 13 the evidence lists (scheduler.ts:170, vetrina/operations/runner.ts:39, collections/runner.ts:95, sync/apply-job.ts:40, woo/taxonomy-cache.ts:21, woo/publishing.ts:14, vetrina/catalog.ts:542 and :756, vetrina/powers.ts:60, pricing/store-terms.ts:480, vetrina/operations/words.ts:22, cache/redis.ts:7, db/client.ts:11) plus three demo-mode ones (vetrina/fixture-source.ts:80, vetrina/operations/standing.ts:28, collections/fixture-client.ts:28) = 16. The claim's own arithmetic (13 + 3) does not equal 15. Also, three of those are not coordination state at all: db/client.ts:11 is a pg Pool, cache/redis.ts:7 is an ioredis client, and words.ts:22 is an AsyncLocalStorage (request-scoped locale), so lumping them as singletons that are "unsafe" for multi-process is misleading — they are harmless per-process handles.

"Only two coordination points are Postgres-backed": false. Beyond the two cited (src/server/woo/media.ts:171-188 `.for("update", { skipLocked: true })` + lockedUntil; src/server/store-json/repo.ts:36 `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('store-hub:store_snapshot'))`), there are two more transaction-scoped advisory locks: src/server/config/repo.ts:132 `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('store-hub:config'))` (used by updateActiveConfig, repo.ts:119-129, explicitly to stop two editors dropping each other's rule changes) and src/server/overrides/repo.ts:54 `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('store-hub:overrides'))` (updateOverrides). Both are cross-process-safe read-modify-write serializers on shared documents, i.e. exactly the kind of coordination point the claim counts. Additionally, src/server/cache/redis.ts implements a Redis-backed shared cache (RedisCache, used by src/server/actions/preview.ts:18,357), which is a cross-process cache rather than a per-process one (it only falls back to MemoryCache on Redis errors).

What does hold: the operations runner (runner.ts:15-41, "A restart loses the queue"), collections runner (collections/runner.ts:34-38, 95), apply-job (apply-job.ts:38-40), scheduler (scheduler.ts:168-170) and the publishing in-flight Set (publishing.ts:9-14, "One app container per shop (docs/deploy.md): this process is the whole picture"; docs/deploy.md:309 "Run a single app container per shop") are genuinely in-process-only coordination, and the implication (reuse the media_jobs claim pattern) is sound. But the numeric core of the claim — fifteen singletons, only two Postgres-backed points — is wrong on both numbers, and the correction (4 advisory/row locks, not 2; plus Redis) is not trivial because it changes how much of the multi-process-safe pattern already exists.
  - Corrected claim: Sixteen globalThis-held stores exist (13 production + 3 demo-mode), but three of them are mere per-process handles (pg Pool at db/client.ts:11, ioredis client at cache/redis.ts:7, AsyncLocalStorage at operations/words.ts:22), not coordination state. The in-process-only coordination points that are unsafe for more than one process are the scheduler (scheduler.ts:170), operations runner queue (vetrina/operations/runner.ts:39), collections runner chain (collections/runner.ts:95), apply-job map (sync/apply-job.ts:40) and the publishing in-flight SKU set (woo/publishing.ts:14), with the rest being TTL/presence caches. Four coordination points are already Postgres-backed and multi-process-safe: the media_jobs claim (woo/media.ts:171-188, FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED + locked_until), snapshot writes (store-json/repo.ts:36), config writes (config/repo.ts:132) and overrides writes (overrides/repo.ts:54), all via pg_advisory_xact_lock; the product preview cache is additionally Redis-backed (cache/redis.ts). The deployment doc (docs/deploy.md:309) explicitly assumes a single app container per shop.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The factual inventory is accurate (globalThis at src/server/scheduler.ts:170, src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts:39, src/server/collections/runner.ts:95, src/server/sync/apply-job.ts:40, src/server/woo/publishing.ts:14, etc.; SKIP LOCKED at src/server/woo/media.ts:166-189). The implication and severity do not follow from it, for three reasons.

1. Multi-process safety is not a prerequisite of LLM exposure. The deployment contract is explicitly one app container per shop (docs/deploy.md:309 "Run a single app container per shop: the syncs run inside it, so a second copy would run every sync twice"), and the code states the same invariant (src/server/woo/publishing.ts:10 "One app container per shop (docs/deploy.md): this process is the whole picture"). Replacing the UI caller with an LLM caller changes who calls, not how many processes run. The repo already exposes exactly this path: docs/api.md:1-20 describes `/api/v1` "for a script, another service or an assistant", running "the same code the Vetrina's screens run (src/server/vetrina/operations/)", and docs/operations.md:87 already files such actions with `via: "assistant"`. So an MCP/tool layer that calls the single Hub process over HTTP inherits the single-process invariant unchanged; nothing in the claim shows that invariant would break.

2. The system of record for the operations runner is already Postgres, not globalThis. The runner persists every action's status and progress to `vetrina_operations` rows (src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts:11-22 "writes its progress to its row as it goes"); status transitions are compare-and-set at the DB (src/server/vetrina/operations/repo.ts:210-225 `moveStatus` with `where status in (from)`, documented as preventing "two undo requests at once, or one racing the run's end"); and a restart is handled by `markInterrupted` (repo.ts:420-439) called via `recover()` before every service entry point (src/server/vetrina/operations/service.ts:80,127,133,143,185,222). What lives on globalThis is only the in-process FIFO chain, and its stated purpose is that both the instrumentation bundle and the server-action bundle share one copy (runner.ts:19-21, scheduler.ts:168-169). The auditor's characterization that only media_jobs and snapshot writes are DB-backed understates the existing mitigation.

3. The prescribed remedy is the wrong pattern for the runners. The operations runner and collections runner deliberately serialize: "one at a time, in the order asked — two actions writing the same products at once would each file a 'before' the other is about to change" (runner.ts:12-15) and "two decisions writing the same product's categories from two different readings is the one thing this must never do" (collections/runner.ts:34-36). `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` on media_jobs (media.ts:166-189) exists to let several workers claim different jobs concurrently; applying it to these runners would permit the concurrent writes they exist to forbid. Multi-process operation there would need a global serializer (advisory lock / single leader, cf. src/server/store-json/repo.ts:34-39), not the media_jobs claim pattern. So a competent engineer would not conclude from these facts that "applying the media_jobs pattern" is "the concrete work either option must do".

Residual truth: if the new middleware were built as a separate process that imports the core modules directly (rather than calling the one Hub process over /api/v1), the in-process queue and the publishing Set would be split and the serialization guarantee lost. That is a design constraint to document ("keep one process per shop, or add a cross-process serializer"), not a major blocker, and it is independent of whether the caller is a UI or an LLM. Severity should be minor/informational.
  - Corrected claim: About fifteen globalThis singletons hold per-process state (scheduler, operations runner, collections runner, apply-job progress, publishing set, several TTL caches, db/redis pools); only the media_jobs claim (FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED) and store-json snapshot writes (advisory lock) are coordinated through Postgres. This reflects a documented, deliberate single-app-container-per-shop deployment (docs/deploy.md:309, publishing.ts:10) and does not block LLM exposure: /api/v1 already serves assistants from the same single process, the operations runner persists status/progress and uses DB compare-and-set transitions plus restart recovery (operations/repo.ts moveStatus, markInterrupted), and the in-process FIFO is intentionally serial to prevent concurrent writes to the same products. Consequence for the middleware work: keep the one-process-per-shop constraint (an MCP/tool layer should call the Hub process, not import the core into a second process), or, if horizontal scaling ever becomes a requirement, add a cross-process serializer (advisory lock / single leader) rather than the media_jobs SKIP LOCKED pattern, which would allow the concurrent writes the runners forbid. Severity: minor.

#### S4 [contested · major] The Sync tab's live apply is tracked only in a process-local Map; a restart loses the job and apply_audit is the only record.

- **Evidence**: src/server/sync/apply-job.ts:16-17 ('a restart loses the job, and the history (apply_audit) says what it wrote'), :40-41 (globalThis Map), :74-76 (single-apply guard is in-memory). Called from src/server/actions/sync.ts:317; status read at :135.
- **Implication**: If the price/stock sync is to be exposed to an LLM caller, its progress and single-writer guarantee must move to apply_audit rows (status already has 'queued'/'running' values at schema.ts:101-103), otherwise a separate caller cannot see or prevent a concurrent apply.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Every cited location checks out literally. /home/user/woo-hub/src/server/sync/apply-job.ts:15-17 says in the header comment "Kept in the process, like the scheduler's state: a restart loses the job, and the history (apply_audit) says what it wrote." Lines 40-41 define the store as `globalThis.__storeHubApplyJobs`, a plain `Map<string, ApplyJob>`; lines 74-76 (`startApplyJob`) implement the single-apply guard purely as `if (runningApplyJob()) return null` over that Map; line 63 even documents `getApplyJob` returning null "when the server has restarted since". The Sync tab's live path calls it at /home/user/woo-hub/src/server/actions/sync.ts:317 and reads the running job at :135 (`runningApply: runningApplyJob()`), and polls it via getSyncApplyJob at :330-334. Nothing else in the repo persists the job (grep for `__storeHubApplyJobs`, `startApplyJob`, `runningApplyJob` hits only apply-job.ts, its test, and actions/sync.ts). No Postgres advisory lock covers the apply: the three `pg_advisory_xact_lock` uses (config/repo.ts:132, overrides/repo.ts:54, store-json/repo.ts:36) are transaction-scoped locks on config/overrides/snapshot writes, not on the apply as a whole.

Two refinements, neither of which refutes the claim; both make it stronger. (1) apply_audit is indeed the only durable record, and it already carries a status of "running" during a live apply: applySync inserts the row with `status: options.dryRun ? "dry_run" : "running"` at /home/user/woo-hub/src/server/woo/apply.ts:544-551 and finalizes it at :827-831. So the "running" enum value at schema.ts:101-103 is in use; "queued" is never written anywhere (grep). But that row has no progress columns (done/total live only in the in-memory ApplyJob, apply-job.ts:90-93) and the guard at apply-job.ts:76 never consults it — so after a restart a second apply can start while the previous row still says "running". (2) The in-memory guard does not even cover all writers today: `applySyncPrices` at actions/sync.ts:289 and the scheduler's price sync at /home/user/woo-hub/src/server/sync/price-sync.ts:113 call `applySync` directly, bypassing `startApplyJob`, and the scheduler's own `s.running` flag (scheduler.ts:136, 593-607) is a separate in-memory flag. So the "single-writer guarantee" is weaker than a process-local guard: it only serializes Sync-tab live applies against each other. The implication (move progress + guard to apply_audit rows) stands and is if anything understated.
  - Corrected claim: The Sync tab's live apply is tracked only in a process-local globalThis Map (apply-job.ts:40-41); its single-apply guard is an in-memory check (apply-job.ts:74-76) and a restart loses the job. apply_audit is the only durable record: applySync already inserts a row with status "running" at start (apply.ts:544-551) and finalizes it (apply.ts:827-831), but that row carries no progress counters and the guard never reads it; "queued" is never written. The in-memory guard also only covers the Sync tab path (actions/sync.ts:317) — applySyncPrices (actions/sync.ts:289) and the scheduler's price sync (price-sync.ts:113) call applySync directly and bypass it, so no cross-caller single-writer guarantee exists today.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, medium): The factual part holds: src/server/sync/apply-job.ts:40-41 keeps jobs in a globalThis Map and :74-76 refuses a second startApplyJob only against that Map; a restart loses it (:16-17, :66). But the stated implication misdescribes both what a separate caller can see and where the single-writer guarantee actually lives.

1. "A separate caller cannot see a concurrent apply" is wrong. applySync itself (not the job wrapper) inserts an apply_audit row with status "running" before the first Woo write (src/server/woo/apply.ts:545-551) and flips it to applied/partial/failed with finishedAt at the end (:826-831). So a running live apply is already visible to any process via `apply_audit where status='running' and finished_at is null`; listApplyHistory (:995-999) already reads these rows. The "the Map is the only tracker, apply_audit is only history" framing is false — apply_audit is a live record, only lacking per-product progress (done/total) and never being reconciled after a crash (there is no equivalent for apply_audit of scheduler.ts:518-521, which marks stale scheduler_runs "interrupted" on boot). The 'queued' status the auditor points to (schema.ts:101-103) is unused anywhere in src (grep), so it is not evidence of a half-built design.

2. The in-memory Map is not the single-writer guarantee, so moving *it* to DB rows is not the fix. Only startSyncApply (actions/sync.ts:317) goes through startApplyJob. The scheduler's unattended store sync calls applySync directly (src/server/sync/price-sync.ts:113, via scheduler.ts:265/400-401), and the synchronous applySyncPrices (actions/sync.ts:289, still called from SyncWorkspace.tsx:532) also calls applySync directly. Today a tab apply and the 04:30 scheduler apply can already overlap unchecked, and so could a Map-backed or DB-backed job table placed in the wrapper. A competent engineer would conclude the mutual exclusion must sit inside applySync (e.g. on the existing apply_audit "running" row, or a pg advisory lock) regardless of how the UI's progress polling is stored — a different and broader finding than "persist the job Map".

3. Topology: deploy/docker-compose.yml runs one `app` service (no replicas), the scheduler is in-process (scheduler.ts:19-20). An LLM tool surface mounted in the same Next.js process shares the Map and gets the existing "Another apply is still writing" refusal (actions/sync.ts:318); the "separate caller cannot prevent" consequence only applies if the tool server is a separate process, which the auditor assumed without stating.

Severity: the restart loss is minor (the apply dies with the process; the apply_audit row remains, albeit stuck at "running"). The real exposure risk — no cross-caller lock in applySync and no stale-row reconciliation — is worth fixing before LLM exposure but is misattributed and overstated as "major" against the Map; the Map is a UI-polling convenience, not the correctness boundary.
  - Corrected claim: The Sync tab's live-apply progress (done/total) is tracked only in a process-local Map (apply-job.ts:40-41) and the Map-based single-apply guard (:74-76) covers only startSyncApply; a restart drops the job view. However, applySync already writes an apply_audit row with status "running" at start and finalises it at the end (woo/apply.ts:545-551, 826-831), so a concurrent live apply is visible to any process via the database. The actual gaps for LLM/middleware exposure are: (a) the mutual-exclusion check lives in the UI wrapper, so the scheduler's apply (price-sync.ts:113) and the synchronous applySyncPrices (actions/sync.ts:289) bypass it and can already overlap with a tab apply; and (b) stale "running" apply_audit rows are never reconciled after a crash (unlike scheduler_runs at scheduler.ts:518-521). The fix is a lock inside applySync (on the apply_audit running row or a pg advisory lock) plus boot-time reconciliation, not persisting the job Map. Severity: moderate, not major.

#### S5 [refuted · minor] An API caller reads two layers with different freshness: the store_index (minutes behind, refreshed every 5 min by date_modified plus every write's answer) backs all /api/v1/catalog reads, while the store_snapshot (one jsonb blob, re-read whole once a day at 04:30) is the pricing/sync truth; freshness signals exist in the DB but are not surfaced on the API.

- **Evidence**: schema.ts:503-536 (store_index) and :179-187 (store_snapshot). collections/runner.ts:44-47,248-287 (incremental check, 10 min overlap, 20-page cap), :485-495 (daily full). scheduler.ts:58-62 and :261-262 ('snapshot is up to a day old between two daily pulls'). collections/service.ts:171-181 (freshen on read if >2 min). store-json/repo.ts:326-344 (getSnapshotInfo uploadedAt) and collections/repo.ts:273-281 (indexInfo newestModified/lastSeen). src/server/api/reads.ts:9-10 reads via vetrina/catalog, i.e. the index.
- **Implication**: For LLM exposure the read model is already a proper denormalized index in Postgres, which is a strength; the gap is exposing as_of/last_seen on responses and deciding whether price-bearing answers come from the index or the snapshot. Both tables are plain SQL and would move unchanged into an extracted service.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, medium): Confirmed parts: store_index at src/server/db/schema.ts:503-536 (dateModified, seenAt) and store_snapshot at :179-187 (single jsonb `data`, `uploadedAt`); the incremental check in src/server/collections/runner.ts:248-287 uses OVERLAP_MS = 10 min (:47) and MAX_CHECK_PAGES = 20 (:45), and runFull at :485-495 re-reads everything; scheduler.ts:58-62 (collections check every 5 min by default, DEFAULT_COLLECTIONS_MINUTES at :83), :81 DEFAULT_TIMES "04:30", :261-262 "the snapshot is up to a day old between two daily pulls"; service.ts:171-181 freshen() requests a check when the last one is >2 min old; store-json/repo.ts:326-344 getSnapshotInfo returns uploadedAt; collections/repo.ts:273-281 indexInfo returns newestModified/lastSeen; src/server/api/reads.ts:9 imports readPage from @/server/vetrina/catalog, whose catalogProduct (catalog.ts:721-741) maps IndexRow fields including price, and catalog-order.ts:5,49,94 (order/terms routes) also reads readIndexFacts — so all three /api/v1/catalog GETs are index-backed. No freshness field is returned by readProducts (reads.ts) or added by serve() (src/server/api/v1.ts:58-70); grep of src/app/api/v1 and src/server/api for uploadedAt/lastSeen/newestModified/asOf finds nothing (only the `stale` 409 code). docs/api.md:124-125 only says the index is "kept fresh every few minutes and by every write".

Where the claim falls: it characterises the snapshot as "one jsonb blob, re-read whole once a day at 04:30" in contrast to the index which gets "every write's answer". The snapshot is equally write-through: every Hub write patches it per product — writes.ts:353,709 -> live.ts:138-146 patchSnapshotSales -> store-json/repo.ts:598 patchSnapshotProducts; actions/store-edit.ts:54 patchSnapshotProduct (:570); woo/publish.ts:706, woo/media.ts:312, woo/rebuild.ts:294, vetrina/operations/remove.ts:193 upsertSnapshotProducts (:452); woo/apply.ts:813 upsertSnapshotProductsAsOf (:496); remove.ts:116 / duplicates.ts:41 removeSnapshotProducts (:629); and the daily pull itself keeps patches made during the pull (woo/pull.ts:202 saveSnapshotKeepingPatches, repo.ts:193). The snapshot also carries a per-product freshness stamp the claim misses: snapshotCopyTime at repo.ts:559-562 (AS_OF / PATCHED_AT / savedAt), finer than table-level uploadedAt. The full pull is also not only the 04:30 slot: it runs from the Sync tab and from /api/cron/pull-store (src/app/api/cron/pull-store/route.ts:24; scheduler.ts:367-374 even defers to a Sync-tab pull). Additionally, write paths re-read the affected products live from Woo and refresh the index first (live.ts:27-35 readFresh -> upsertIndexRows), so API actions are not acting on minutes-old data; and the margin action (price-bearing) already reads the snapshot via getSnapshotProductsBySkus + ledger (margin.ts:338,444-446), so the "index vs snapshot for price answers" decision is partly already made per operation. The two-layer picture and the unsurfaced-freshness gap are right; the freshness model of the snapshot and the scope of the gap are misstated enough that the correction is not trivial.
  - Corrected claim: An API caller reads two store copies kept fresh by different mechanisms: the store_index (one row per product; brought up to date by an incremental date_modified check every 5 min with a 10-min overlap and 20-page cap, by a daily full read, by a freshen-on-open when >2 min stale, and by every write's answer) backs all three /api/v1/catalog GETs (products, terms, order) including their price field, while the store_snapshot (one jsonb blob, replaced whole by a full pull — daily at 04:30 by default, or on demand from the Sync tab / /api/cron/pull-store — and patched per product by every Hub write: sales, publish, media, rebuild, apply, remove, store edits) is what the pricing/sync and margin paths read. Write actions re-read the targeted products live from Woo before writing, so actions are not bound to index staleness. Freshness signals exist in the DB — index max(date_modified)/max(seen_at), snapshot uploaded_at, and per-product AS_OF/PATCHED_AT stamps inside the snapshot — but none is returned on /api/v1 responses; the gap is surfacing them (as_of/last_seen) and documenting per-endpoint which copy a price comes from, since margin already reads the snapshot while catalog reads use the index.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, medium): The factual half (two tables, two cadences) is accurate, but two load-bearing parts of the implication are wrong and the code already contains the mitigations the auditor says are missing.

1) "store_snapshot is the pricing/sync truth" is false. The snapshot is only the baseline a plan is made against; every price write re-reads the product live from Woo immediately before writing and keeps/overrides per the live state (src/server/woo/live-check.ts:7-31 header; src/server/scheduler.ts:258-264 "each product is read again right before its write"; src/server/actions/vetrina-prices.ts:74 `liveCheck: true`). The truth for a price write is WooCommerce itself, not the jsonb blob. Nor is the snapshot "re-read whole once a day" only: every write patches it in place with per-product as-of stamps (src/server/store-json/repo.ts:452-470 upsertSnapshotProducts, :496-557 upsertSnapshotProductsAsOf with AS_OF/PATCHED_AT and newer-copy-wins logic, :559-562 snapshotCopyTime, :570-627 patchSnapshotProduct(s), :629 removeSnapshotProducts). Per-product freshness in the snapshot is already finer than the 04:30 pull.

2) "deciding whether price-bearing answers come from the index or the snapshot" is not an open decision for the /api/v1 surface. No /api/v1 read touches the snapshot: readProducts/readHomepage go through vetrina/catalog and vetrina/service (src/server/api/reads.ts:9-10, :98-125, price at :105 comes from the index row, src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts:729). Snapshot readers are all write-side internals (apply.ts, publish.ts, rebuild.ts, price-sync, margin.ts, dashboard/data.ts). So an API caller does not read "two layers with different freshness"; it reads one (the index), and price writes are validated live.

3) "freshness signals are not surfaced on the API" is only partly true. The API already gives tool-callers what they need to act safely on possibly-stale reads: docs/api.md:124-125 states the index's freshness contract; /homepage returns modifiedGmt + attrsHash and actions accept `expected` → 409 `stale` (docs/api.md:214-215, src/server/api/v1.ts:28); proposals are re-previewed at confirm and return 409 stale with a replacement proposal if the shop changed (docs/api.md:228-235); and a catalog read itself triggers a freshen when the last check is >2 min old (src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts:775 → collections/service.ts:171-181). What is genuinely missing is a literal `asOf`/`lastSeen` field on GET /catalog/products responses (indexInfo at collections/repo.ts:273-281 exists but is not returned); that is a small additive field, not a design decision about which table answers.

Severity "minor" is right for the residual gap, but a competent engineer would not conclude from these facts that price answers face an index-vs-snapshot choice or that the snapshot is the pricing truth; the optimistic-concurrency + live-check design already resolves that for LLM exposure. The implication therefore overstates the problem.
  - Corrected claim: An API caller reads one layer: the store_index (refreshed every ~5 min by date_modified, by every write's answer, and by a freshen triggered from catalog reads older than 2 min) backs all /api/v1 reads. The store_snapshot jsonb is a write-side planning baseline only: it is pulled whole daily but patched per product with as-of stamps on every write, is never read by /api/v1, and is not the pricing truth, since every price write re-reads the product live from Woo (live-check) before writing. Freshness is already surfaced where it matters for tool use (modifiedGmt/attrsHash on /homepage, `expected` on actions, re-preview and 409 stale on proposal confirm, and docs stating the index's freshness); the only gap is an `asOf`/`lastSeen` field on catalog read responses, which indexInfo already computes. Severity: minor; both tables move unchanged into an extracted service.

#### S6 [refuted · major] Heap pressure is a documented, recurring operational hazard tied to the single-jsonb store snapshot; several modules were rewritten to query the blob in SQL, but five call sites still load the entire snapshot into the heap.

- **Evidence**: README.md:45-66 (dev server reaches 4 GB heap limit; prod flat at 56 MB); store-json/repo.ts:250-263 ('9.5 MB of JSON for a 3000-product shop'), :350-358 ('140 MB of JSON for a 20 000-product shop'); schema.ts:64-71 (plans paginated to keep the heap small). Whole-blob readers remain: woo/repair.ts:84, actions/export.ts:54, actions/duplicates.ts:30, actions/debug.ts:37,98,189; patch.ts:54 structuredClone of the model. Diagnostics: src/server/debug/heap-watch.ts, src/app/api/debug/heap/route.ts.
- **Implication**: A long-lived middleware process serving an LLM would inherit this: the snapshot's single-blob design is the main thing a fresh repo or extraction should revisit (per-product rows, as store_index already is), and until then the service needs a heap ceiling and the remaining whole-blob readers isolated from request paths.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): Checked every cited location. What holds: src/server/store-json/repo.ts:250-263 and :350-358 do document that deserializing the whole snapshot (9.5 MB / 140 MB JSON figures) caused "Ineffective mark-compacts near heap limit" and that listStoreSkus/getSnapshotProductsBySkus (and :291,:314,:369,:421 plus src/server/catalog/repo.ts:250) were rewritten to jsonb_array_elements queries in SQL. src/server/debug/heap-watch.ts and src/app/api/debug/heap/route.ts exist as heap diagnostics. src/server/db/schema.ts:64-71 does say plans are paged so rows "never have to exist in the heap at once" (though that is about the plans table, not the snapshot blob).

What fails: (1) The count. getActiveSnapshot() (repo.ts:241-247, returns rows[0].data as StoreModel, the entire blob) is called at SEVEN sites, not five: src/server/woo/repair.ts:84, src/server/actions/export.ts:54, src/server/actions/duplicates.ts:30, src/server/actions/debug.ts:37, :98, :189, and one the claim misses entirely: src/app/(admin)/duplicates/page.tsx:25, a server-component page render that loads the whole snapshot on every GET of /duplicates and then runs findDuplicateGroups over it. That omission matters for the stated implication ("isolate whole-blob readers from request paths") because it is precisely a request-path reader. The claim's own evidence list already enumerates six lines while saying "five". (2) patch.ts:54 (buildReimport) is not an independent reader; its only importer is export.ts:5, so it doubles the blob already loaded at export.ts:54 rather than adding a call site. (3) README.md:45-66 does not tie the heap hazard to the jsonb snapshot. It attributes the 4 GB-limit crash to `npm run dev` retaining compiled module graphs, states explicitly "It does not depend on how big the shop is", measured on a 400-product / 706 kB store, and shows `npm run serve` flat at 56 MB. The repo thus carries two distinct heap narratives (dev-server module retention in README/heap-watch.ts:5-9 vs. snapshot deserialization in repo.ts comments), and the README evidence contradicts rather than supports "tied to the single-jsonb store snapshot". Both narratives also locate the crashes in the dev server; the README's only production measurement shows no pressure. The claim is directionally right (whole-blob readers remain; the snapshot design is a real scaling concern) but overstated in attribution and wrong in count, with the missed site being the most relevant one.
  - Corrected claim: Heap pressure is a documented, recurring operational hazard in this repo, with two documented causes: the dev server's module-graph retention (README.md:45-66, explicitly "does not depend on how big the shop is"; production build flat at 56 MB on a 400-product store) and, separately, deserializing the single-jsonb store snapshot (store-json/repo.ts:250-263 and :350-358). Several hot paths were rewritten to query the blob in SQL (repo.ts:264-430, catalog/repo.ts:250), but seven call sites still load the entire snapshot into the heap via getActiveSnapshot(): woo/repair.ts:84, actions/export.ts:54 (which then structuredClones the whole model in patch.ts:54), actions/duplicates.ts:30, actions/debug.ts:37/98/189, and the /duplicates page render at src/app/(admin)/duplicates/page.tsx:25, which is a request-path reader. A long-lived LLM-driven middleware would inherit the snapshot-side hazard; the single-blob design (vs. per-product rows as in store_index/catalog) is worth revisiting in a fresh repo or extraction, and the remaining whole-blob readers, especially the page render, should be isolated or converted to SQL-side queries.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The factual inventory is accurate (whole-blob readers at src/server/woo/repair.ts:84, src/server/actions/export.ts:54, src/server/actions/duplicates.ts:30, src/server/actions/debug.ts:37/98/189, plus src/app/(admin)/duplicates/page.tsx:25 which the auditor missed; structuredClone at src/server/store-json/patch.ts:54). The implication and severity do not follow, for reasons the code and history make explicit:

1. The heap hazard was root-caused to the dev server, not the blob. Commit f1f45ee ("It was never the shop: next dev leaks, npm run serve does not") and README.md:45-66 state the leak "does not depend on how big the shop is": `next dev` retains compiled module graphs (+~14 MB/round), while `next start` is flat at 55-56 MB over the same pages and data. "That is every crash report in this thread." A long-lived middleware runs the production build (package.json:8 `serve`), which is exactly the configuration measured as flat. The auditor cites README:45-66 as evidence of a snapshot hazard when that passage is the retraction of that theory.

2. The 9.5 MB / 140 MB figures (repo.ts:250-263, :350-358) are extrapolations to 3000/20000-product shops; f1f45ee says the real shop is ~400 products and "the entire store snapshot is 706 kB", and that the week of scale work "was never going to be the difference between running and dying". A 706 kB jsonb deserialized a handful of times is not a heap hazard in a 56 MB process.

3. The hot paths were already moved to SQL: listStoreSkus (repo.ts:264+) for the Sync tab / store pull, getSnapshotProductsBySkus (repo.ts:357+) for publish/rebuild/apply, paginated plans (schema.ts:64-71), commits 9c4bec6, 38374ee, 65a827a. What remains is operator-initiated, low-frequency work: three debug actions, one-shot re-import export, trashing a single duplicate, a repair candidate scan, and the duplicates admin page — none is on a request path an LLM would hit repeatedly.

4. The LLM-facing surface does not touch the blob: grep for getActiveSnapshot across src/app/api/** (v1: actions, catalog, homepage, operations, order-sources, proposals) and src/server/api returns nothing. The core domain per README.md:68+ is catalog_products (per-row), and store_index (schema.ts:503) is already per-product. The snapshot is a mirror, not the model a middleware would be built around.

So "the single-blob design is the main thing a fresh repo or extraction should revisit" is not a consequence of the cited facts; a competent engineer reading f1f45ee would conclude the opposite. A heap ceiling (--max-old-space-size) is ordinary ops hygiene, not a remediation this evidence demands. Severity should be minor at most (a design-cleanliness note), not major.
  - Corrected claim: The store snapshot is stored as a single jsonb blob (store_snapshot.data, src/server/db/schema.ts:179-186) and six operator-facing call sites still deserialize it whole (woo/repair.ts:84, actions/export.ts:54, actions/duplicates.ts:30, actions/debug.ts:37/98/189, app/(admin)/duplicates/page.tsx:25; patch.ts:54 structuredClones it for export). This is a design-cleanliness point, not an operational hazard for a long-lived middleware: the recorded heap crashes were root-caused to `next dev` retaining module graphs independent of shop size (commit f1f45ee, README.md:45-66), the production server is flat at ~56 MB, the real snapshot is ~706 kB, the hot paths (sync, pull, publish, apply) already read the blob in SQL, and the API/v1 surface an LLM would call never touches getActiveSnapshot. Severity: minor. An extraction can keep catalog_products/store_index as the per-row model and leave the snapshot as an operator mirror; a heap ceiling is optional hygiene, not a prerequisite.

#### S7 [refuted · strength] The pull, feed sync, KicksDB refresh and photo queue are already drivable from outside the process through authenticated cron routes with DB cursors, proving those steps are process-independent; the Vetrina operations runner, collections check, store-sync writes and orders pull have no such external entry point.

- **Evidence**: src/app/api/cron/{pull-store,refresh-catalog,sync-goldensneakers,media}/route.ts (4 files, CRON_SECRET bearer, maxDuration 300-800); scripts/trigger-cron.sh:9-29; store_pull_runs cursor at pull.ts:113-180 and :249-265 (runFullPull resumable); scheduler.ts:67 'The /api/cron/* endpoints stay available for external schedulers'. No cron route for syncStore (scheduler.ts:265-356), runCheck/runFull (collections/runner.ts:473-495), pullRecentOrders (scheduler.ts:729-747) or queueRun/queueSweep (operations/runner.ts).
- **Implication**: Strength for option (a)/(b): roughly half the background work already has a process-agnostic, idempotent, resumable shape with Postgres cursors. The remaining half (operations runner, collections, store sync, orders) is the list of jobs that would need the same treatment before the core can live outside the Next process.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The first half of the claim checks out literally. src/app/api/cron/{pull-store,refresh-catalog,sync-goldensneakers,media}/route.ts are the only four cron routes; each gates on `req.headers.get("authorization") !== \`Bearer ${env.CRON_SECRET}\`` (e.g. pull-store/route.ts:16-21) and exports maxDuration 800/800/300/300. scripts/trigger-cron.sh:9-29 POSTs to them with the bearer and documents idempotency. The pull cursor is real: advancePull (pull.ts:113-180) writes cursorPage/productsFetched to store_pull_runs per page; startPull (pull.ts:76-88) resumes any run with status "running"; runFullPull (pull.ts:249-265) loops advancePull and says the run resumes on the next invocation. scheduler.ts:67 says exactly 'The /api/cron/* endpoints stay available for external schedulers'. Confirmed also that there is no API route calling syncStore/runPriceSync (scheduler.ts:265-356, only callers in src/app/api: none; grep of src/app/api for runPriceSync/runCheck/runFull/pullRecentOrders returns nothing), runCheck/runFull (collections/runner.ts:473-495; callers are scheduler.ts:407/771 and tests), or pullRecentOrders (orders/pull.ts:28; callers scheduler.ts:734-735 and the server action src/server/actions/orders.ts:48 only).

The second half is overstated on the Vetrina operations runner. Its file is src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts (the cited path src/server/operations/runner.ts does not exist). queueRun IS reachable from outside the process through the authenticated programmatic API: POST /api/v1/proposals/{id}/confirm (src/app/api/v1/proposals/[id]/confirm/route.ts:14-17) -> confirmProposal -> startOperation (proposals.ts:168) -> queueRun(row.id) at vetrina/operations/service.ts:104; likewise POST /api/v1/order-sources/{id}/apply -> src/server/api/order-sources.ts:66 -> startOperation. These routes are gated by `Authorization: Bearer $HUB_API_TOKEN` in src/server/api/v1.ts:62-66 and each call runs recover() first (service.ts:80). So the operations runner does have an external, authenticated entry point; what it lacks is a cron-style, resumable shape — the queue lives on globalThis and 'A restart loses the queue: what it left running is marked interrupted (recover()), never resumed' (runner.ts:19-22). Only queueSweep has no external route (reached via the server action src/server/actions/vetrina-operations.ts:135-137 from TermActionsPanel.tsx:152 and from scheduler.ts:756-757). Because the mis-classified job is the Hub's main external API surface and the implication builds a 'needs treatment' list from it, the correction is not trivial.
  - Corrected claim: The pull, feed sync, KicksDB refresh and photo queue are drivable from outside the process through authenticated cron routes (CRON_SECRET bearer, maxDuration 300-800) with a DB cursor only for the pull (store_pull_runs; runFullPull resumes a running run). The Vetrina operations runner (src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts) is also externally drivable: POST /api/v1/proposals/{id}/confirm and POST /api/v1/order-sources/{id}/apply (HUB_API_TOKEN bearer) call startOperation, which queues queueRun — but that queue is in-memory on globalThis and a restart marks the job interrupted rather than resuming it. The jobs with no external entry point at all are the collections check/full pass (runCheck/runFull), the scheduler's store-sync writes (syncStore/runPriceSync), the orders pull (pullRecentOrders, server action only), and the hidden-term sweep (queueSweep, server action only).
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The factual inventory is correct (4 CRON_SECRET routes under src/app/api/cron/*, scripts/trigger-cron.sh, store_pull_runs cursor at src/server/woo/pull.ts:73-84 and 240-265, no route for syncStore/runCheck/runFull/pullRecentOrders/queueRun). The implication drawn from it does not hold, on three counts.

1. "Process-agnostic" is overstated for three of the four routed jobs. Only the media queue has a real multi-worker lease: src/server/woo/media.ts:166-189 claims jobs with FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED + lockedUntil (and even it reads a process-local `publishing` set at :169). The pull has a resume cursor but no lease: startPull (pull.ts:77-84) simply returns the existing "running" row to any caller, and advancePull writes the cursor back unguarded; the scheduler itself says "Two drivers on one pull would race for its cursor" and protects itself with a 2-minute updatedAt heuristic (scheduler.ts:368-372, ACTIVE_PULL_MS; pullInFlight at pull.ts:68-70 is a 10-minute staleness guess). The KicksDB runId is only an accounting row (src/server/actions/feeds.ts:183-201), with no claim on the stale entries. The routes are designed as an either/or alternative to the in-app scheduler for SCHEDULER=off ("with SCHEDULER=off nothing else works it", media/route.ts:10-11; scheduler.ts:20 "no external cron needed"), not as evidence the work can run from a second process beside the Next one. Externally triggerable != process-independent.

2. "The remaining half needs the same treatment" misdescribes that half. pullRecentOrders is a stateless upsert (src/server/orders/pull.ts:46 upsertOrders) and syncStore/runPriceSync re-plans from the DB snapshot each run, with `deferred` simply recomputed next time (src/server/sync/price-sync.ts:46-57, 81, 109-129) — both are already idempotent and need only a ~30-line route, not cursors. Collections persists its change log, readFrom cursor and per-collection reports in Postgres (collections/runner.ts ~440-450 reportCollection, repo.pruneChanges at :491); only its `exclusive` serialization/status lives on globalThis (runner.ts:95-110, 134-160). The one job that is genuinely process-bound is the Vetrina operations runner, which says so itself: "A restart loses the queue: what it left running is marked interrupted (recover()), never resumed" (src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts:11-21, 57-69; repo.ts:424 markInterrupted). So the split is not half/half; it is one hard case, one easy-ish case, and two trivial ones.

3. For the LLM/middleware question the metric is the wrong one. A tool surface wants bounded request/response plus status polling. The cron routes are the opposite shape: pull-store blocks on runFullPull to completion for up to maxDuration=800s (pull-store/route.ts:7, 24) and refresh-catalog loops up to 50 rounds in one request — fine for a scheduler, unusable as a tool call. Conversely the operations service already has the right shape — startOperation files a row and queues it, loadOperation/listOperations poll progress (src/server/vetrina/operations/service.ts:79-104, 126-133), progress written to the row "which is what the screens poll" (runner.ts:13-15) — despite its in-process queue. Nor is "the core living outside the Next process" a prerequisite for LLM exposure; an MCP/tool layer can call these server functions in-process. The evidence therefore supports at most a minor convenience (four jobs can be kicked by curl), not a structural readiness strength, and the "list of jobs needing the same treatment" would mislead a planning effort.
  - Corrected claim: Four background jobs (pull, GoldenSneakers feed sync, KicksDB refresh, photo queue) can be triggered from outside via CRON_SECRET routes, but only the photo queue has a real cross-process lease (FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, media.ts:166-189); the pull's cursor is a resume point without a lock and the scheduler itself warns that two drivers race for it (scheduler.ts:368-372). The jobs without routes are not uniformly "unready": orders pull (upsert) and store sync (re-planned per run with instalments) are already idempotent and need only a route; collections keeps its log and reports in Postgres with only serialization in memory; the Vetrina operations runner alone is truly process-bound (queue lost on restart, interrupted never resumed, runner.ts:11-21). For LLM exposure the relevant shape is file-a-job-then-poll, which the operations service already has (service.ts:79-133) and the blocking 800-second cron routes do not; so this is a minor operational convenience, not a halfway-there structural strength, and it is not a prerequisite for a tool/MCP layer.


<a id="spec"></a>
## Spec quality (fresh-repo option) and codebase health (keep option): tests, CI, typecheck, docs-vs-code drift, language split, file cohesion, velocity

### Summary

The codebase is healthy by every mechanical measure I could run: `npx tsc --noEmit` returns zero errors, `eslint . --max-warnings=0` exits 0, and `npx vitest run` passes 1003 tests in 94 files in 7.6s with no database (17 files / 188 tests are Postgres-gated and skip). CI (.github/workflows/ci.yml) runs tanstack-guard, typecheck, lint, unit tests, build, and a Playwright e2e job against a real Postgres (22 e2e tests, 5 of them on /api/v1); it does not run the 17 *.db.test.ts files (they need RUN_DB_TESTS=1 and a localhost DB) and does not run scripts/guard-snapshot-blob.mjs even though that memory guard exists. Test density is good where it matters for a rewrite: the team consistently split pure "plan" modules (order-plan.ts, margin-plan.ts, publish-plan.ts, repair-plan.ts, media-plan.ts, plan.ts, proposal-plan.ts, core/*) from I/O orchestrators and tested the planners with zero vi.mock, in behavioural language with oracles (e.g. order-plan.test.ts re-sorts by the store's own rule to prove the plan), so those tests are a usable executable spec. The I/O orchestrators (apply.ts 1029 lines, scheduler.ts 870, publish.ts 761) are only covered by DB-gated tests, and src/server/api (the /api/v1 layer, 4 files) has zero unit tests and only 5 e2e tests. Docs are 11 files / 3962 lines, written as decision records with explicit "Status:" lines; all five behaviour claims I checked (woo never a price source; proposals expire 15 min; undo 7 days; operations kept 30 days; order action writes the fewest menu_order values) are true in code with named constants, and the only drift found is two stale self-descriptions (catalog-centric-redesign.md says "not yet implemented" while ProductDrawer.tsx exists; core-spine.ts header says it knows nothing of Woo yet exports WooStoreAdapter). The core/ directory (2222 lines, 12 test files) is still framed as a "KicksDB -> store repricing tool", and the Vetrina/operations layer, which is the actual middleware, lives in src/server/vetrina (36 source files, 13 unit + 7 db tests), not in core/. Language is split: identifiers mix Italian domain words (vetrina x245, taglia, ordine, classifica) with English, 68 hardcoded Italian user strings sit in server code outside the i18n dictionaries (including API error messages such as "Azione non valida."), and the i18n dictionaries total ~6400 lines across it/en plus a catalog/ subfolder. Velocity: the clone is shallow, but it shows 158 commits, 50 merge commits, PR numbers up to #95, Sept 15 to Oct 31 2026, 108 commits authored as "Claude" vs 50 by the owner.

## CI (.github/workflows/ci.yml)
- `build` job: `npm ci` -> `guard:tanstack` -> `typecheck` -> `lint` -> `npm test` -> `next build` (placeholder env).
- `e2e` job: Postgres 16 service, `next build`, Playwright chromium, `npm run test:e2e`, upload report on failure.
- Not in CI: `guard:snapshot` (scripts/guard-snapshot-blob.mjs, 85 lines, memory guard listing an ALLOWED set of whole-snapshot readers), the 17 db tests.
- Guards: scripts/guard-tanstack.mjs (40 lines, bans @tanstack/* in package.json/lockfile), scripts/guard-snapshot-blob.mjs.

## Test quality samples
