### Claims and verdicts

#### S1 [contested · strength] The repo is mechanically green: typecheck has 0 errors, lint passes at --max-warnings=0, and 1003 unit tests pass in 7.6s without a database.

- **Evidence**: Ran `npx tsc --noEmit` (no output, 12.5s), `npx eslint . --max-warnings=0` (exit 0), `npx vitest run` (94 files passed / 17 skipped, 1003 tests passed / 188 skipped). CI order in .github/workflows/ci.yml lines 20-33 matches.
- **Implication**: The keep option starts from a stable base; a programmatic/MCP layer can be added with a fast red/green loop. Nothing in the health metrics argues for a fresh repo.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Re-ran every command at HEAD 77584c1 in /home/user/woo-hub with no Postgres/Redis process running (pgrep/ss empty) and no DATABASE_URL/REDIS_URL/E2E_DATABASE_URL in env. `npx tsc --noEmit`: no output, exit 0 (2.5s warm). `npx eslint . --max-warnings=0`: exit 0. `npx vitest run`: "Test Files 94 passed | 17 skipped (111); Tests 1003 passed | 188 skipped (1191)", 6.96s, exit 0. package.json scripts map `typecheck`→`tsc --noEmit`, `lint`→`eslint . --max-warnings=0`, `test`→`vitest run`, and .github/workflows/ci.yml lines 20-33 run npm ci → guard:tanstack → typecheck → lint → test in that order (plus a build step at 34-43 and a separate e2e job at 48-99 that the claim does not mention but does not contradict). So every number in the claim is literally true. One nuance the claim omits, relevant to the implication rather than the claim: the 188 skipped tests are all the `*.db.test.ts` suites gated by `describe.skipIf(!enabled)` where `enabled = process.env.RUN_DB_TESTS === "1" && /@(localhost|127\.0\.0\.1)[:/]/.test(url)` (e.g. src/server/vetrina/catalog.db.test.ts:16). RUN_DB_TESTS is never set in .github/ or package.json (only mentioned in docs/smart-collections.md:245), so the ~16% of tests that exercise real SQL never run in CI; "green" there means the pure-logic tests only, and the SQL paths are covered only by the Playwright e2e job. That is a caveat on "stable base", not a refutation of the stated metrics.
  - Corrected claim: The repo is mechanically green: typecheck has 0 errors, lint passes at --max-warnings=0, and 1003 unit tests pass in ~7s without a database. The remaining 188 tests (17 files, all *.db.test.ts real-SQL suites) are skipped unless RUN_DB_TESTS=1 and a localhost DATABASE_URL are set, which CI never does; those paths are exercised in CI only via the separate Playwright e2e job.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The facts hold (ci.yml lines 20-33 run guard, typecheck, lint, `npm test`; vitest.config.ts includes core/** and src/** tests). The implication does not. The "17 skipped files / 188 skipped tests" the auditor glossed over are exactly the 17 `*.db.test.ts` files (src/server/vetrina/operations/{operations,margin,page,proposals,remove,demo}.db.test.ts, src/server/sync/{ledger,sizes,sync}.db.test.ts, src/server/overrides/repo.db.test.ts, src/server/config/rule-list.db.test.ts, src/server/collections/runner.db.test.ts, src/server/scheduler.db.test.ts, etc.). They are opt-in: `operations.db.test.ts:14-15` gates on `process.env.RUN_DB_TESTS === "1"` and a localhost DATABASE_URL; the only mention of RUN_DB_TESTS outside the test files is docs/smart-collections.md:245 (a manual command). The CI `build` job has no postgres service and never sets RUN_DB_TESTS; the `e2e` job provisions postgres but runs only `npm run test:e2e` (Playwright), not vitest. So CI never executes the ~2,760 lines / 104 it() of real-SQL tests that cover the operation queue, undo, restart, and the concurrency locks docs/vetrina-catalog.md:137-155 and docs/smart-collections.md:238 cite as the repo's correctness guarantees. That mutation/operations layer (src/server/vetrina/operations/*, ~10.6k lines) is precisely what an LLM/MCP tool layer would wrap; the 1003 green tests are the pure-planning/parsing slice (plan.test.ts, margin-plan.test.ts, registry.test.ts, src/lib/*, components). "Passes without a database" is therefore not a strength for the middleware question but the symptom: the DB-dependent half of the suite is invisible to CI, and a "fast red/green loop" for a programmatic layer requires a local Postgres plus RUN_DB_TESTS=1 that nothing in CI enforces. No coverage tooling is configured (no `coverage` in vitest.config.ts or package.json). Additionally all 25 entry points in src/server/actions/*.ts are `"use server"` Next.js actions, so the green suite says nothing about a non-Next invocation path. The health metrics do not argue for a fresh repo, but they also do not establish a "stable base" for the operations layer; severity should be downgraded from strength to neutral/caveat.
  - Corrected claim: The repo is mechanically green on its CI-run slice: typecheck 0 errors, lint at --max-warnings=0, and 1003 unit tests pass in 7.6s. However 17 files / 188 tests (all the real-SQL `*.db.test.ts` suites covering the operations queue, undo, restart, sync ledger and concurrency locks in src/server/) are skipped unless RUN_DB_TESTS=1 and a local Postgres are present, and CI never runs them (the build job has no database; the e2e job only runs Playwright). The green signal therefore covers the pure planning/parsing code and UI, not the mutation layer an MCP/tool interface would wrap; there is no coverage measurement, and all server entry points are Next.js "use server" actions. This is neutral for keep-vs-fresh: nothing argues for a fresh repo, but a programmatic layer cannot rely on the current CI for red/green feedback on the operations layer without first wiring the DB suites into CI.

#### S2 [confirmed · major] The pure 'plan' modules are tested as behaviour specs with zero mocks and store-rule oracles, so they are a checkable spec for a rewrite; the I/O orchestrators are not.

- **Evidence**: src/server/vetrina/operations/order-plan.test.ts:1-60 (storeOrder oracle, exact writes); plan.test.ts has 15+ rule-named `it` blocks (lines 48-213); src/server/woo/*.test.ts 7 files, 0 `vi.mock`; core/__tests__ 13 files 2343 lines. By contrast `applySync` (apply.ts:429-970) appears only in sync/*.db.test.ts, `startScheduler` only in scheduler.db.test.ts, and there is no apply.test.ts/publish.test.ts/scheduler.test.ts.
- **Implication**: For extract or fresh: the planners (order-plan, margin-plan, plan, proposal-plan, publish-plan, repair-plan, media-plan, core pricing) can be lifted with their tests as acceptance criteria. The Woo write orchestration (apply/publish/scheduler, ~2660 lines) would have to be re-specified from code and DB tests, which is where a rewrite carries the most risk.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Checked every cited item. (1) src/server/vetrina/operations/order-plan.test.ts:1-60: imports only vitest + ./order-plan; defines storeOrder() oracle (lines 11-18) sorting by written menu_order then prior rank, and each `it` asserts exact plan.writes and storeOrder(...)==plan.order. Confirmed. (2) plan.test.ts: grep shows 29 `it(` blocks, rule-named, from line 48 to 397 under describes at 47/85/199/221/233/265/344/377 — the "15+, lines 48-213" figure is an undercount, not an overstatement. (3) grep -c for vi.mock|vi.fn|vi.spyOn across src/server/woo/*.test.ts (7 files incl. media.db.test.ts) and core/__tests__/*.test.ts (12 test files + helpers.ts = 13 files; wc gives 2343 lines for the 13) all return 0; repo-wide vi.mock list contains none of them. Also 0 in order-plan/plan/proposal-plan/margin-plan tests. (4) applySync is exported at src/server/woo/apply.ts:429 (file is 1029 lines; the evidence's path "sync/apply.ts" is wrong but the claim's bare "apply.ts:429" is right). Grep shows its only test callers are sync/sizes.db.test.ts:183-195 and, via actions/sync.ts applySyncPrices, sync/sync.db.test.ts and sync/ledger.db.test.ts; sizes and ledger tests vi.mock @/server/woo/client. (5) startScheduler at src/server/scheduler.ts:824; only test caller scheduler.db.test.ts:194, which vi.mocks woo/client, kicksdb, woo/pull, goldensneakers, actions/feeds, collections/runner, catalog/enrich, woo/media (lines 37-86). (6) find confirms no apply.test.ts, publish.test.ts or scheduler.test.ts anywhere. (7) Line total apply 1029 + publish 761 + scheduler 870 = 2660, matching the claim exactly. Counterexamples sought: src/lib/schedule.test.ts (pure, no mocks) specs the scheduler's time maths, and src/server/sync/apply-job.test.ts (one vi.fn, no vi.mock) specs the background apply-job wrapper; publish-plan/live-check/sanitize-plan tests spec the rules the orchestrators call. These soften the implication ("re-specified from code") but do not contradict the claim that the orchestrators themselves lack behaviour-spec tests. Claim stands; correction is a nuance only.
  - Corrected claim: The pure 'plan' modules are tested as behaviour specs with zero mocks and store-rule oracles (e.g. order-plan.test.ts's storeOrder oracle and exact-writes assertions; plan.test.ts has 29 rule-named `it` blocks across 8 describes, lines 47-397; the 7 src/server/woo/*.test.ts and 12 core/__tests__/*.test.ts files use no vi.mock/vi.fn/vi.spyOn), so they are a checkable spec for a rewrite. The I/O orchestrators (woo/apply.ts applySync at :429, woo/publish.ts publishProducts at :381, scheduler.ts startScheduler at :824; 1029+761+870 = 2660 lines) have no unit spec: they are exercised only via DB-backed tests (sync/sizes.db.test.ts, sync/sync.db.test.ts, sync/ledger.db.test.ts, scheduler.db.test.ts) that vi.mock the Woo client and, for the scheduler, 7 further modules. Nuance for the implication: the decision rules the orchestrators call are themselves pure-tested (live-check.test.ts decideLiveWrites, store-json sanitize/missing-sizes/takeover-plan tests, publish-plan.test.ts, lib/schedule.test.ts for the scheduler's slot/retry maths, sync/apply-job.test.ts for the background-job wrapper), so what must be re-specified from code is the sequencing, audit/ledger writes and error handling of the orchestration, not the business rules it applies.
- **Skeptic** (stands, medium): Facts confirmed and actually worse than stated: the only tests touching applySync/startScheduler/the sync pipeline are the *.db.test.ts suites (src/server/sync/sizes.db.test.ts:183-195, sync.db.test.ts:96-137, scheduler.db.test.ts), and every one of them is `describe.skipIf(!enabled)` with `enabled = RUN_DB_TESTS === "1" && localhost DATABASE_URL` (sync.db.test.ts:12-13, scheduler.db.test.ts:17-18). .github/workflows/ci.yml sets neither RUN_DB_TESTS nor DATABASE_URL for the `npm test` step (only for `build`), so in CI the orchestrators have zero executed tests. `publishProducts` (publish.ts:381) has no test at all, only the pure publish-plan.test.ts. So the implication that the orchestrators are not a checkable spec stands.

Mitigation the auditor under-weighted, which narrows the magnitude but does not overturn the conclusion: applySync is mostly glue over pure, unit-tested planners. Within apply.ts:429-860 the decisions are delegated to planProductSanitize (store-json/sanitize-plan.test.ts), planFeedTakeover (takeover-plan.test.ts), planMissingSizes (missing-sizes-plan.test.ts, used at apply.ts:743-748), decideLiveWrites/liveRulesFrom (woo/live-check.test.ts, used at 609, 639-646), toStoreVariation (store-product.test.ts), withinBudget (lib/chunk), and the ledger note rules in sync/store-edit-plan.test.ts; publish.ts imports planPublish/planReimportParent/publishedPrices/publishedVariations/withoutIdentity from publish-plan (368-line test) and media-plan/repair-plan. The remaining genuinely untested orchestration behaviour is the I/O contract: audit-row lifecycle (apply.ts:541-575), per-variation merge of cleanup+price+stock+GTIN rows (673-686), per-row refusal handling (690-709), failure isolation and best-effort ledger recording on failure (716-725), snapshot patching, and the scheduler's instalment/arming logic. That is well under the quoted ~2660 lines, and it is documented in prose contracts (apply.ts:28-53, publish.ts:48-66) plus typed outcomes (ApplyOutcome, PublishOutcome). A competent engineer would still land on the same place: the write-path safety semantics (dry-run default, per-product isolation, live-check keep, audit) are exactly what an LLM-driven middleware would trigger, and they are specified only by code and by opt-in DB tests that CI never runs. Severity "major" is right for the fresh-repo option; for keep/extract it is lower, since these orchestrators can be kept and wrapped behind the existing v1 API (e2e/api.spec.ts already exercises an assistant-facing operations API) without rewriting them.
  - Corrected claim: The pure 'plan' modules (order-plan, margin-plan, plan, proposal-plan, publish-plan, repair-plan, media-plan, sanitize-plan, takeover-plan, missing-sizes-plan, live-check, store-edit-plan, core pricing) are tested as mock-free behaviour specs and can be lifted with their tests as acceptance criteria. The I/O orchestrators (woo/apply.ts applySync, woo/publish.ts publishProducts, scheduler.ts) are not: their only tests are RUN_DB_TESTS-gated *.db.test.ts suites that CI never executes, and publishProducts has none. However, the un-specified surface is narrower than ~2660 lines: most decisions inside the orchestrators are delegated to those tested planners, leaving the I/O contract (audit-row lifecycle, per-variation row merge, per-row refusal handling, failure isolation, ledger-on-failure, snapshot patching, scheduler instalments) as the part that a fresh repo would have to re-specify from code, header comments and the opt-in DB tests. This is a major risk for the fresh-repo option and a moderate one for keep/extract, where the orchestrators can be retained and wrapped behind the existing API rather than rewritten.

#### S3 [contested · major] The /api/v1 programmatic layer, the piece an LLM would call, has no unit tests and only 5 end-to-end tests; the 17 Postgres-backed test files never run in CI.

- **Evidence**: src/server/api/ has 4 files (catalog-order.ts, order-sources.ts, reads.ts, v1.ts) and 0 *.test.ts; `find src/app -name '*.test.ts*'` = 0; e2e/api.spec.ts has 5 `test(`; *.db.test.ts gate on RUN_DB_TESTS=1 (src/server/vetrina/catalog.db.test.ts:15-16) and ci.yml's build job has no Postgres service; scripts/guard-snapshot-blob.mjs is not referenced in ci.yml.
- **Implication**: Growing the programmatic surface on this codebase means the surface itself is the least-tested layer; before exposing it as middleware, API-level contract tests (and running the db suite in CI) are the gap to close, regardless of keep/extract/fresh.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Every cited fact checks out in the code. (1) /home/user/woo-hub/src/server/api/ contains exactly catalog-order.ts, order-sources.ts, reads.ts, v1.ts (373 lines total) and no *.test.ts; `find src/app -name '*.test.ts*'` returns 0, so none of the 14 src/app/api/v1/**/route.ts handlers has a unit test either. The only test files whose names mention the layer are src/server/vetrina/order-sources/order-sources.test.ts:3 (imports `looksLikeHomepage` from @/lib/vetrina/order-sources, a lib helper, not src/server/api) and demo.db.test.ts:136 (imports the order-sources *service*), neither touches src/server/api/* or the route handlers. (2) e2e/api.spec.ts has exactly 5 `test(` (lines 30, 41, 74, 99, 123). The only other /api/v1 exercise is e2e/sources.spec.ts:46, a single `api(request,"POST","/operations/<id>/undo")` used as setup inside a UI test, not an API contract test, so "only 5 e2e tests" stands. (3) 17 *.db.test.ts files exist (find, excluding node_modules). src/server/vetrina/catalog.db.test.ts:15-16 gates on `process.env.RUN_DB_TESTS === "1"` plus a localhost DATABASE_URL. .github/workflows/ci.yml (the only workflow): the `build` job runs `npm test` (vitest run) with no `services:` block and no RUN_DB_TESTS env; the `e2e` job has a postgres:16 service but runs only `npm run test:e2e` (Playwright), never `npm test`. grep for RUN_DB_TESTS across yml/json/mjs/sh/md hits only docs/smart-collections.md:245, so the db suite is skipped in every CI run. (4) scripts/guard-snapshot-blob.mjs is referenced only by package.json:19 (`guard:snapshot`), not by ci.yml, which runs only `guard:tanstack`. No counterexamples found; the claim is literally true.
  - Corrected claim: The /api/v1 programmatic layer, the piece an LLM would call, has no unit tests (0 tests under src/server/api/ or src/app/api/) and only 5 end-to-end tests (e2e/api.spec.ts); the 17 Postgres-backed *.db.test.ts files are gated on RUN_DB_TESTS=1 and never run in CI (ci.yml's build job runs `npm test` with no Postgres service and no RUN_DB_TESTS; the e2e job has Postgres but runs only Playwright), and scripts/guard-snapshot-blob.mjs is not wired into ci.yml.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, medium): The facts hold (4 API files, 0 unit tests there, 5 `test(` in e2e/api.spec.ts, db tests gated by RUN_DB_TESTS=1 at src/server/vetrina/catalog.db.test.ts:16, the `build` job has no Postgres). But the implication "the programmatic surface is the least-tested layer, so API contract tests + db suite in CI are the gap to close before exposure" overstates it, and "major" is too high.

1. The /api/v1 layer is a deliberately thin adapter, not a logic layer. src/server/api/* is 373 lines total; catalog-order.ts:12-16 says it "adds nothing of its own but SKUs"; order() (catalog-order.ts:99-113) and apply() (order-sources.ts:55-67) just build an OperationInput, call parseOperationInput and operations.previewOperation/startOperation; proposals/route.ts:19-26 forwards to propose(); actions/route.ts:12-14 returns describeActions(). The route files are 1-line wrappers around serve() (v1.ts:55-71). The logic an LLM would actually exercise lives in src/server/vetrina/operations/, which has 13 test files with ~183 test cases (plan.test 29, operations.db.test 32, margin-plan 22, order-plan 13, registry.test 5 for the JSON-Schema action descriptions, proposals.db.test 6, proposal-plan 5, ...) plus order-sources.test.ts (21). So "least-tested layer" misattributes: the adapter is thin, the wrapped core is among the best-tested parts of the repo.

2. The e2e tests ARE API-level contract tests running against real Postgres in CI. ci.yml has a second `e2e` job with a postgres:16 service, builds, runs `npm run test:e2e`; playwright.config.ts:43 runs `node e2e/prepare-db.mjs && next start` (fresh migrated DB). e2e/api.spec.ts exercises the JSON contract over HTTP: GET /actions (schema shape per action), POST /catalog/order -> poll /operations/{id} -> undo and compare order, POST /proposals -> confirm -> idempotent re-confirm -> undo, 409 stale proposal returning the replacement proposal, GET /homepage, /catalog/products, /operations incl. a 400 case. The auditor's "5 tests" undercounts: each covers several endpoints and status codes, and they hit auth, dry-run/async, stale-conflict and undo semantics, which is exactly the contract an LLM caller depends on. So "17 db test files never run in CI" is partly mitigated: the Postgres-dependent behaviour the API depends on (operations repo, proposals, runner) is executed in CI through the e2e job, even though the *.db.test.ts files themselves are opt-in.

3. Residual true gap (minor, not major): e2e does not touch /catalog/terms, /order-sources (upload/get/apply) or GET /proposals/{id}; serve()'s error mapping (v1.ts:63-70: ZodError -> 400 joined issues, unknown -> 500, 503 when HUB_API_TOKEN unset) has no direct test; and the *.db.test.ts suites run only locally. A competent engineer would note these as cheap improvements (a Postgres service on the build job + RUN_DB_TESTS=1, a few more api.spec cases), not as a precondition shaping the keep/extract/fresh decision. Importantly, the thin-adapter structure is itself the finding relevant to LLM exposure: an MCP/tool layer would wrap the same tested operations/service + registry, so the existing test base transfers whichever option is chosen.
  - Corrected claim: The /api/v1 layer (src/server/api/*, 373 lines) is a thin adapter with no unit tests of its own and is covered by 5 Playwright API tests (e2e/api.spec.ts) that run in CI against a fresh Postgres (ci.yml `e2e` job) and exercise the main contract: action schemas, order/undo, propose/confirm/idempotency, 409 stale proposals, reads, and a 400 case. The logic it wraps (src/server/vetrina/operations/*, registry, order-sources) has ~200 unit/db test cases. Gaps are minor: /catalog/terms, /order-sources and GET /proposals/{id} lack e2e coverage, serve()'s error/auth mapping is untested directly, and the 17 *.db.test.ts files run only with RUN_DB_TESTS=1 locally (the build job has no Postgres). These are cheap hardening items, not a blocker on exposing the middleware to an LLM nor a factor in keep-vs-fresh-repo. Severity: minor.

#### S4 [confirmed · strength] The action registry is already validated as a machine-readable contract: every action has a JSON Schema input with a const kind, a parsable example, and descriptions longer than 40 chars, enforced by a test.

- **Evidence**: src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.test.ts:19-49 (`describeActions()`, `parseAction(ACTIONS[kind].example)`, `properties.kind.const`, description length > 40, field descriptions 'a model reads').
- **Implication**: This is the part of the codebase most directly reusable as the tool manifest for an LLM; it favours keep or extract over fresh, since a rewrite would need to reproduce exactly this registry and its tests.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Checked the cited test and the code it exercises. src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.test.ts:19-24 asserts ACTION_KINDS equals the union of OperationInputSchema's kinds plus PAGE_ACTION_KINDS (so no server-run kind escapes the registry); :26-31 runs parseAction(ACTIONS[kind].example) for every kind and expects ok:true with the same kind; :33-42 calls describeActions(), checks input.type === "object", properties.kind.const === kind, and description.length > 40 for every action; :44-49 spot-checks field descriptions for two actions (sale, sectionAdd). registry.ts:41-220 defines all 21 ActionSpecs with description/undo/example; :248-266 builds JSON Schema via z.toJSONSchema(schema, { io: "input" }) and throws if a kind has no schema. I ran `npx vitest run src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.test.ts`: 5/5 pass. CI runs `npm test` (.github/workflows/ci.yml:31-32). The registry is actually served to clients at src/app/api/v1/actions/route.ts:12-14 (GET /api/v1/actions), which strengthens the implication. Only nuance: field-level descriptions ("a model reads") are only spot-checked for two actions, not enforced for every field of every action (input.ts has 8 .describe() calls, page-input.ts 4); the claim's "descriptions longer than 40 chars" refers to the per-action description, which is enforced for all. That nuance does not overturn the claim.
  - Corrected claim: The action registry is already validated as a machine-readable contract: a test (src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.test.ts) enforces that every server-run action kind is in the registry, that each example parses via parseAction with its own kind, and that describeActions() yields for each action a JSON Schema object input whose `kind` property is a const equal to the action kind and an action description longer than 40 chars; field-level descriptions are only spot-checked for two actions (sale, sectionAdd). The test passes and runs in CI, and the registry is served at GET /api/v1/actions.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Facts confirmed: src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.test.ts:19-49 does exactly what the auditor says (kinds complete vs both zod unions, examples parse, JSON Schema object with properties.kind.const === kind, description.length > 40, field descriptions present), and `npm test` (vitest run, vitest.config.ts includes src/**/*.test.ts) runs in CI (.github/workflows/ci.yml "Unit tests" step), so it is enforced, not just written.

The implication is a genuine consequence, and if anything understated: the registry is not only testable, it is already served and consumed as a tool manifest. registry.ts:247-266 `describeActions()` builds JSON Schema via `z.toJSONSchema(schema, { io: "input" })` from the same zod unions the server validates with (input.ts:115-162, page-input.ts), and the header comment (registry.ts:9-19) states its purpose is "an assistant acting for the owner" / "what an API or MCP client lists". It is exposed at GET /api/v1/actions (src/app/api/v1/actions/route.ts:12-14) and the execution path exists: POST /api/v1/proposals -> `propose()` -> `parseAction()` (proposals.ts:105-110) -> preview -> POST /proposals/{id}/confirm -> operation with undo (docs/api.md:181-257, docs/operations.md:1-30 describes propose/confirm as the designed path for anything "outside the screens"). Field descriptions are written in English "for programs" (input.ts:32-60). A competent engineer would agree this is the most LLM-ready part of the codebase and that it argues against a from-scratch rewrite.

Caveats that do not overturn it: (1) the registry covers only write actions; the read endpoints an agent needs first (/catalog/products, /catalog/terms, /homepage) have query params documented in docs/api.md but no JSON Schema or machine-readable manifest; (2) `unrepresentable: "any"` drops zod refinements (wholeTerm termKey check, endsOn >= today, max ids, input.ts:59,69-71,87,116) from the published schema — mitigated by prose descriptions and by server-side parseAction rejecting bad input with plain-words errors; (3) minor drift: pin/margin undo text hardcodes "7 days" while others interpolate OPERATION_UNDO_DAYS (registry.ts:111,120 vs 35-36); (4) the registry + schemas are ~550 lines depending only on lib types and words.ts, so they are cheap to lift — this favours keep-or-extract roughly equally, which is what the auditor said. Severity "strength" is right.
  - Corrected claim: The action registry is already validated as a machine-readable contract: every action (13 catalog + 8 homepage kinds) has a JSON Schema input derived from the same zod schema the server validates with, with a const kind, a parsable example, and a description longer than 40 chars, enforced by registry.test.ts which runs in CI. It is already served as a manifest at GET /api/v1/actions and executed through the propose/confirm/undo API, so it is the most directly reusable piece for LLM tool exposure and favours keep or extract over a fresh repo. Gaps remain outside it: read endpoints have no machine-readable schema, and zod refinements (term/target consistency, date-not-past, id count) are not expressed in the published JSON Schema, only in prose and server-side validation.

- **Evidence**: README.md:86 vs src/server/catalog/repo.ts:27,54; docs/operations.md:67 vs src/lib/vetrina/proposals.ts:12 (15); docs/operations.md:41 vs src/lib/vetrina/catalog-ops.ts:31 (7); docs/operations.md:33 vs src/server/vetrina/operations/repo.ts:36 (KEEP_DAYS = 30); docs/api.md:54-60 vs order-plan.ts:5. (3) docs/operations.md:41 "Annulla, within 7 days" vs src/lib/vetrina/catalog-ops.ts:31 `OPERATION_UNDO_DAYS = 7`. (4) docs/operations.md:33 "kept 30 days" vs src/server/vetrina/operations/repo.ts:36 `const KEEP_DAYS = 30`. (5) docs/api.md:54-60 menu_order asc then name, one number per product, fewest writes vs src/server/vetrina/operations/order-plan.ts:5-12 which states exactly that. Extra spot checks also hold: `via` values app/api/assistant/scheduler at src/lib/vetrina/catalog-ops.ts:247; 50 000 ceiling (docs/vetrina-catalog.md:447 vs catalog-ops.ts:28 MAX_TERM_PRODUCTS = 50_000); wc-gh/v1 endpoints used in src/server/vetrina/wp-source.ts; README's "(hidden) /preview" route exists at src/app/(admin)/preview/page.tsx; app/manifest.ts exists at src/app/manifest.ts. Woo" while :792 `export class WooStoreAdapter implements StorePort`. Searched docs for stale markers (not yet/TODO/planned/superseded/legacy/deprecated) and found no further contradictions with code; the only additional drift is docs/catalog-centric-redesign.md:67 citing `core/core-spine.ts:396-609` for WooStoreAdapter (now at :792), inside the doc already flagged. The one numeric slip: with HEAD at 2026-10-31, docs touched since 2026-10-10 are api, auth, deploy, hub-and-plugin, operations, order-sources, smart-collections, vetrina-catalog, vetrina-customer-hub and README = 10 of 11, not 9; this understates rather than overstates the claim, so it is a trivial correction. 111 *.test.ts files exist, consistent with "doc discipline in place". The claim stands.
  - Corrected claim: Docs are current decision records: all five behaviour claims checked are backed by named constants in code, with only two stale self-descriptions found (plus one stale line-range inside the already-flagged Sept doc). 10 of 11 docs (not 9) were touched in the last 3 weeks of history; only docs/catalog-centric-redesign.md (2026-09-15) was not.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Re-checked the evidence and tried to find cases where the "docs are a trustworthy behaviour spec" implication breaks. It holds. (1) The five constants match: README.md:84-87 "kicksdb > goldensneakers > woo ... never a price source — getFreshBySkus/getAnyBySkus exclude it" vs src/server/catalog/repo.ts:44 `ne(catalogProducts.source, "woo")` with the same rationale comment; docs/operations.md:33 "kept 30 days" vs src/server/vetrina/operations/repo.ts:36 `KEEP_DAYS = 30`; docs/operations.md:41 "Annulla, within 7 days" vs src/lib/vetrina/catalog-ops.ts:31 `OPERATION_UNDO_DAYS = 7`; docs/operations.md:67 "15 minutes → expired" vs src/lib/vetrina/proposals.ts:12 `PROPOSAL_MINUTES = 15`. (src/lib/publish-batching.ts)" vs src/lib/publish-batching.ts:13-18 `FIRST_BATCH = 6`, `MIN_BATCH = 3`, `MAX_BATCH = 25`, `TARGET_BATCH_MS = 45_000`, with the header comment (:7) explaining why 25 fixed failed — the docs track code there too. (4) Doc discipline evidence beyond the auditor's: .github/workflows/ci.yml exists, 118 test files, `OPERATION_VIAS` includes "assistant" (src/lib/vetrina/catalog-ops.ts:247) exactly as docs/operations.md:35-36 describes. Two small corrections that do not change the verdict: docs/catalog-centric-redesign.md:3 carries an explicit `Status:` line, so it is a labelled design record rather than a silent behavioural claim (still worth marking superseded, as the auditor says); and core/core-spine.ts:5 "knows nothing about ... Woo" is contradicted in the same header at :8-10 ("The Woo REST adapter at the bottom implements StorePort"), so it is an internal inconsistency rather than drift. The only scoping caveat a competent engineer would add: the docs are accurate for what they describe, but the API docs cover only the Vetrina/operations slice; the Hub's other write paths (24 "use server" modules, 4356 lines in src/server/actions/) are documented in README as UI behaviour, not as a callable interface. The docs are a trustworthy behaviour spec for the Vetrina/operations slice that /api/v1 exposes; note that the Hub's other write paths (src/server/actions/*, 24 modules) are described only as UI behaviour, not as an interface.

#### S6 [refuted · major] The code labelled 'core' is the legacy KicksDB repricing kernel, while the real middleware domain (Vetrina operations, proposals, catalog, homepage) lives under src/server/vetrina coupled to Next/Drizzle, so 'extract the core' would mean extracting src/server/vetrina, not core/.

- **Evidence**: core/core-spine.ts:1-11 header ('KicksDB -> store repricing tool'), 30 exports incl. WooStoreAdapter; core/ is 2222 lines in 3 files imported by 79 src files. src/server/vetrina has 36 source files (operations/ alone 29 files incl. registry.ts, plan.ts, proposals.ts, writes.ts 788 lines, repo.ts with raw SQL at :458-480); src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts mixes ~20 pure functions with ~12 async DB readers in 896 lines.
- **Implication**: Extraction is feasible because the pure planners are already separated, but the package boundary is not where the directory names suggest; an extract/fresh plan has to budget for untangling vetrina/* from Drizzle and server-only imports.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): Checked the cited evidence and looked for counterexamples.

What holds: core/core-spine.ts:1-11 header does say "KicksDB -> store repricing tool"; core/ is 2222 lines in 3 files (core-spine 930, config 610, collections 682) with 30 exports incl. `WooStoreAdapter` (core-spine.ts:792); src/server/vetrina has 36 non-test source files; writes.ts is 788 lines, catalog.ts 896; operations/repo.ts:458-480 (`trimOperations`) uses `sql.raw`/`sql` templates; vetrina files do import `@/server/db/*` and `drizzle-orm`.

Where the claim falls:
1. "core is the legacy KicksDB repricing kernel" — core/collections.ts (682 lines, ~31% of core/) is the pure smart-collections engine for WooCommerce categories (core/collections.ts:1-12; docs/smart-collections.md:223 "pure"), a current Woo-native middleware domain, not KicksDB repricing. And the repricing engine is not legacy: src/server/scheduler.ts:97 runs a daily "kicksdb" step; `buildPlan` is used by 8 non-test modules (src/server/woo/live-check.ts, woo/rebuild.ts, sync/store-edit-plan.ts, store-json/rebuild-plan.ts, vetrina/operations/margin-plan.ts, ...); core had 8 commits since 2026-08, last 2026-10-14.
2. "extract src/server/vetrina, not core/" — vetrina itself depends on core: 9 vetrina files import `@core/*`, e.g. margin-plan.ts:2-12 imports `computePrice`, `resolveEffectiveRule`, `declaresMarkup`, `marginKindOf` and calls them at :328-336; catalog.ts:2 imports `withAncestors` from `@core/collections`; proposal-plan/plan/words import `SourceProduct`, `ScopedPricingRule` types. Vetrina cannot be extracted without core; the extraction unit is core + vetrina (+ their deps).
3. "the real middleware domain lives under src/server/vetrina" — vetrina is 10.7k lines but src/server/woo is 5.1k (live REST client, pull, apply), store-json 2.2k, collections 1.8k, catalog 1.4k, feeds 1.1k, pricing 0.5k; README.md:68 names the catalog (`src/server/catalog/`) the "core domain", README.md:423-431 names core/ + woo/ + store-json/ as "Core". Vetrina's catalog.ts is itself a façade over src/server/collections/{repo,service,store} (catalog.ts:29-35).
4. Drizzle coupling overstated: of 36 vetrina source files only 5 import `drizzle-orm`/`@/server/db/client` at runtime (operations/repo.ts, operations/margin.ts, operations/proposals.ts, order-sources/repo.ts, sources.ts); ~11 import only row/table types from `@/server/db/schema`; 20 (incl. plan.ts, registry.ts, margin-plan.ts, order-plan.ts, sections.ts, words.ts, layout.ts, powers.ts, schemas.ts, wp-source.ts) import no DB module at all. catalog.ts imports no Drizzle (catalog.ts:1-38); its 15 async functions (catalog.ts:554-871) read via collections/repo and the Woo `storeClient`, so "~12 async DB readers" mischaracterizes them, though `import "server-only"` at :1 is real.
5. Count nits: operations/ has 22 non-test files (34 with tests), not 29; 78 src files import core, not 79.

Net: the directional point (pure planners are separable; persistence is concentrated in a few repo files) survives, but the headline — core is legacy, vetrina is the thing to extract instead — is wrong on both halves.
  - Corrected claim: core/ is not a legacy kernel: core-spine.ts/config.ts are the live pricing/plan engine (used daily by scheduler.ts:97 and by 8 planners incl. vetrina/operations/margin-plan.ts:328-336), and core/collections.ts (682 lines) is the pure Woo smart-collections engine. The Vetrina operations domain (src/server/vetrina, 36 source files) sits on top of core (9 vetrina files import @core/*) and on src/server/collections and src/server/woo, so "extracting the core" means extracting core/ plus src/server/vetrina (and the woo/collections/store-json modules they call), not vetrina alone. Vetrina's Drizzle coupling is concentrated: 5 files use drizzle-orm/db client at runtime (operations/repo.ts incl. raw SQL at :458-480, margin.ts, proposals.ts, order-sources/repo.ts, sources.ts), ~11 import only schema types, 20 import no DB module; catalog.ts imports no Drizzle but is `server-only` and reads through collections/repo and the Woo store client. An extract/fresh plan must budget for a core+vetrina unit with a thin persistence port, not for untangling vetrina from core.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The factual half mostly holds (core/core-spine.ts:1-11 is the KicksDB repricing kernel; src/server/vetrina/operations/repo.ts:2,6 imports drizzle-orm and @/server/db/client; writes.ts is 788 lines), but the implication and severity do not survive for the LLM-exposure question.

1. Mitigation the auditor missed: the middleware is ALREADY exposed without the UI. There is a versioned, bearer-authenticated HTTP API at src/app/api/v1/** (14 route files: actions, operations, operations/[id]/undo, proposals, proposals/[id]/confirm, catalog/products|terms|order, order-sources, homepage), documented in docs/api.md:1-3 ("for a script, another service or an assistant"). src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.ts:9-18 is an action registry written explicitly for "an assistant acting for the owner", and registry.ts:262 emits each action's input as JSON Schema via z.toJSONSchema; src/app/api/v1/actions/route.ts:13 serves it (GET /api/v1/actions). docs/api.md:187 says outright "An assistant's tools are these", and operations carry `via: app | api | assistant` (docs/api.md:240). The routes call the same service functions the UI calls (e.g. operations/route.ts:4 -> operations/service.ts listOperations). So LLM/MCP exposure is a thin tool adapter over an existing API; no extraction of src/server/vetrina from Drizzle or Next is required. The claim's premise that one must "extract the core" to expose the middleware is false, which collapses the implication.

2. "core/ is legacy" is overstated: only core-spine.ts is the repricing kernel. core/config and core/collections are live dependencies of the vetrina domain: margin-plan.ts:2-3 uses resolveEffectiveRule/declaresMarkup from @core/config, repo.ts:3 uses ScopedPricingRule, catalog.ts:2 uses withAncestors from @core/collections; 46 import sites for @core/config and 15 for @core/collections across src. A fresh-repo plan would carry core/config and core/collections forward, not discard core/.

3. "server-only imports" are not an untangling cost: `import "server-only"` is a one-line Next marker already stubbed for tests by vitest.config.ts (alias to src/test/server-only-stub.ts). The pure planners (plan.ts:1-4, margin-plan.ts:1-16, order-plan.ts:1, proposal-plan.ts:1-6) import no Drizzle/db, only types and @core/config. Additionally src/lib/vetrina (15 files, 2195 lines: catalog-ops, page-ops, page-plan, sections, order, proposals, types) has zero server-only/drizzle/@/server imports — a framework-free domain layer already exists beside core/.

Severity: at most minor/informational. The directory-naming observation is a correct note for a fresh-repo plan's reading of the code, but it is not a blocker and not "major" for exposing the middleware to an LLM, because the exposure surface (api/v1 + registry with JSON Schema) is already built and does not depend on where the package boundary sits. A competent engineer would conclude the keep-and-wrap path is already in place and that any extraction would start from src/lib/vetrina + the pure planners + core/config, with only repo.ts/writes.ts/catalog.ts readers needing a port.
  - Corrected claim: core/core-spine.ts is the legacy KicksDB repricing kernel, but core/config and core/collections remain live dependencies of the Vetrina domain (margin-plan.ts, repo.ts, catalog.ts), and the Vetrina domain itself spans a pure layer (src/lib/vetrina, ~2.2k lines, no server/Drizzle imports; the pure planners in operations/*-plan.ts) plus Drizzle-coupled readers/writers (operations/repo.ts, writes.ts, catalog.ts). This boundary note matters only for a fresh-repo plan; for LLM exposure it is not a blocker (severity: minor), because the middleware is already exposed independently of the UI through the bearer-authenticated /api/v1 routes and the action registry (registry.ts, GET /api/v1/actions) that emits per-action JSON Schema for an assistant's tools — an MCP/tool adapter can wrap that API without extracting or untangling src/server/vetrina.

#### S7 [refuted · minor] Identifiers and server-side user strings are an Italian/English mix with 68 hardcoded Italian strings in server code, including API error messages, outside the ~6400-line i18n dictionaries.

- **Evidence**: grep counts in src/server, core, src/lib: vetrina 245, vetrinaOperations 58, tagliaAttributeId 22, ordine 18, taglia 13; 68 Italian string literals e.g. src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts:615,793,876, service.ts:210,264,267, wp-source.ts:63; registry errors 'Azione non valida.' asserted in registry.test.ts:52,58; dictionaries it.ts 1698 + en.ts 1641 + catalog/ 3134 lines.
- **Implication**: For an LLM-facing API, error and refusal strings returned by the server are Italian-only and not routed through i18n; a rewrite could normalise naming, but on keep this is a bounded cleanup (68 sites) rather than a structural problem.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): Confirmed parts: every cited hardcoded line is literally Italian and bypasses i18n — src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts:615 ("Il collegamento al negozio non è configurato…"), :793 ("Questa categoria non c'è più nel negozio."), :876 ("Questo prodotto non è nel catalogo…"); src/server/vetrina/service.ts:210, :264, :267 (`new VetrinaError("stale", "La homepage è stata modificata nel frattempo: ricarica…", 409)`); src/server/vetrina/wp-source.ts:63 ("Il sito non risponde alla Vetrina: aggiorna il plugin…"). Also src/server/actions/vetrina.ts:58,66,72,94 return `{ ok:false, error: "Sezione non valida." }` etc., and src/server/vetrina/fixture-source.ts:236,319,381 and layout.ts carry more. Identifier counts are in the right ballpark (my grep -w over src/server, core, src/lib: vetrina 275, vetrinaOperations 86, tagliaAttributeId 25, ordine 18, taglia 20). Dictionary line counts match exactly (it.ts 1698, en.ts 1641, catalog/ 3134). Of 109 non-test `new VetrinaError(` sites in src/server, 80 are hardcoded literals (nearly all Italian), so the "~68 sites, bounded cleanup" order of magnitude is fair.

Refuted part: the registry example is wrong and the implication is overstated. 'Azione non valida.' is NOT a hardcoded server string: src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.ts:271 returns `words().input.operation`, which resolves via src/server/vetrina/operations/words.ts:36 to `getDictionary(locale).vetrinaCatalog.ops` — src/i18n/dictionaries/catalog/ops.it.ts:359 ("Azione non valida.") / ops.en.ts:285 ("Invalid action."). registry.test.ts:52 and proposals.db.test.ts:147 assert Italian only because tests run outside a request and the AsyncLocalStorage default is Italian (words.ts:33, `language.getStore() ?? defaultLocale`). Moreover `inLanguage()` wraps src/server/api/v1.ts, actions/vetrina-operations.ts, actions/vetrina-order-sources.ts, actions/vetrina.ts and operations/proposals.ts, and 29 VetrinaError sites across operations/{context,margin,order,page,preview,proposals,service,writes}.ts and order-sources/service.ts are routed through words(). So the newer operations/API layer — the one most relevant to an LLM-facing surface — already has request-scoped i18n error strings; the Italian-only leakage is concentrated in the legacy vetrina catalog/layout/service/actions path. The claim's statement that "error and refusal strings returned by the server are Italian-only and not routed through i18n" is false as a general statement and its headline example is a counterexample.
  - Corrected claim: Identifiers are an Italian/English mix (vetrina ~275, vetrinaOperations ~86, tagliaAttributeId ~25, taglia ~20, ordine 18 occurrences in src/server, core, src/lib), and roughly 60–80 server-side error/refusal strings are hardcoded Italian literals outside the ~6400-line i18n dictionaries — concentrated in the legacy vetrina path (src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts:615,793,876; service.ts:210,264,267; wp-source.ts:63; fixture-source.ts; layout.ts; src/server/actions/vetrina.ts:58,66,72,94), where 80 of 109 `new VetrinaError(...)` sites use literal strings. However, the operations layer and the v1 API already route refusals through request-scoped i18n: `parseAction` returns `words().input.operation` (ops.it.ts:359 "Azione non valida." / ops.en.ts:285 "Invalid action."), `inLanguage()` wraps src/server/api/v1.ts and the vetrina server actions, and 29 VetrinaError sites in operations/ and order-sources/ use words(). The registry.test.ts:52 assertion shows Italian only because tests run outside a request where the default locale is Italian. On keep, this is a bounded cleanup of the legacy sites onto the existing words()/inLanguage() mechanism, not a structural problem.
- **Skeptic** (refuted, high): The implication ("error and refusal strings returned by the server are Italian-only and not routed through i18n") is contradicted by the code around the auditor's own evidence.

1. The headline example is i18n-routed. src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.ts:269-273 parseAction returns `words().input.operation`, not a literal. words() (src/server/vetrina/operations/words.ts:36-37) reads `getDictionary(locale).vetrinaCatalog.ops`, backed by an AsyncLocalStorage language context (`inLanguage`, `currentLanguage`, words.ts:22-33). The string "Azione non valida." lives in src/i18n/dictionaries/catalog/ops.it.ts:359 and has an English counterpart "Invalid action." at ops.en.ts:285. The tests at registry.test.ts:52 see Italian only because outside a request the default locale is Italian (words.ts:14-16 doc comment). So refusals from the action/operations layer are already localised.

2. The public API already negotiates language. src/server/api/v1.ts:7,63 wraps every handler in `inLanguage(languageOf(req), ...)` and the module comment (v1.ts:17-20) states answers are Italian or English with `Accept-Language: en`. The API surface an LLM/MCP client would call is thus not Italian-only by design; the same `inLanguage` plumbing is used by the server actions (src/server/actions/vetrina*.ts) and proposals (proposals.ts:83,153 store and replay the requester's locale for background runs).

3. Error responses are structured, so message language is not what a tool caller keys on. VetrinaError carries a machine-readable `code` from a closed union (src/lib/vetrina/types.ts:267-276: not_configured, plugin_missing, plugin_outdated, unauthorized, stale, not_found, invalid, failed) plus HTTP status and optional `data` (source.ts:45-55). v1.ts:33,66 emits `{ ok:false, code, error, data }` with the code; service.ts:37,58-61 does the same for server actions and even replaces non-"said" messages with `plain(code)` so raw store text never leaks. An LLM-driven middleware branches on `code`, so the residual Italian `message` is cosmetic.

What survives of the claim: the VetrinaError sites cited in catalog.ts:615,793,876, service.ts:210,264,267, wp-source.ts:63 do hardcode Italian messages instead of calling words(); and the API route itself mixes in an English literal (src/app/api/v1/proposals/route.ts:20). That is a real but small inconsistency, and because the i18n infrastructure (words()/inLanguage) and the error-code taxonomy already exist, it is an even more bounded cleanup than the auditor's "68 sites" framing suggests (fewer than that, since the registry/ops refusals are already routed). The severity "minor" is right or slightly overstated; the implication as stated (Italian-only, not routed through i18n, a problem for LLM exposure) is wrong.
  - Corrected claim: Identifiers are an Italian/English mix (vetrina, taglia, ordine) and a subset of server-side VetrinaError messages (e.g. src/server/vetrina/catalog.ts:615,793,876, service.ts:210,264,267, wp-source.ts:63) hardcode Italian text instead of using the existing server-side i18n (words()/inLanguage in src/server/vetrina/operations/words.ts). However, the operations/registry refusals such as "Azione non valida." are already dictionary-routed (ops.it.ts:359 / ops.en.ts:285 "Invalid action."), the /api/v1 layer selects the language from Accept-Language (src/server/api/v1.ts:63), and every error carries a machine-readable `code` from the VetrinaErrorCode union plus HTTP status, so an LLM/MCP consumer does not depend on the message language. For LLM exposure this is a cosmetic consistency cleanup of the remaining hardcoded message sites, not a structural problem; severity: trivial-to-minor.


<a id="intent"></a>
## Design intent already recorded, and its drift

### Summary

The repo's docs record a coherent, deliberate intent for exposing the core to programs: one writer of product data (the Hub), the storefront plugin as the only WordPress-side surface, every change as a typed "action" described once in a registry, anything from outside the screens going through a proposal the owner confirms, a log with `via: app|api|assistant|scheduler`, and an MCP server as "the next step" built in-process from that registry (docs/operations.md:84-106). The code follows the programmatic half faithfully: `describeActions()` makes JSON Schema with Zod 4's `z.toJSONSchema` from the very discriminated unions the server validates (src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.ts:225-266), `GET /api/v1/actions` serves it, 21 action kinds are described, and every function the MCP table names (`readHomepage`, `readProducts`, `listTerms`, `listOperations`, `propose(action, via)`, `confirmProposal`, `undoOperation`) exists with the documented signature. The drift is in three places. First, `via` is a declared vocabulary, not a lived one: only `"app"` and `"api"` are ever passed; `"assistant"` and `"scheduler"` appear in the type, the DB default and the Italian/English labels but nowhere in a call, and the scheduler's automatic-category writes go to `collection_changes`, not `vetrina_operations`, so the "same log" promise is not yet true for unattended writes. Second, "proposals as the only outside write path" is contradicted by two one-call shortcuts the API itself documents (`POST /catalog/order`, `POST /order-sources/{id}/apply`) that call `startOperation(..., "api")` directly. Third, and most consequential for the owner's example ("change the navigation menu with these product categories"), the recorded decision explicitly places navigation menus, media hygiene and email on the WordPress/plugin side (docs/hub-and-plugin.md:121,129-131), and the code confirms the Hub has zero `wp/v2` calls: the Woo client touches only `products/*` endpoints and `wp-source.ts` calls seven `wc-gh/v1` routes about the homepage. Several docs are also stale as specs (vetrina-customer-hub.md says "Not merged" and "No new database tables" while three `vetrina_*` tables exist; catalog-centric-redesign.md says "not yet implemented" while the README documents it done), so the code, not the docs, is the accurate spec today. The §6 product-sync roadmap in hub-and-plugin.md has one of six items done; the other five (generic feeds, oversell guard, webhooks-in, rules-as-code, KicksDB discover) have no code. `hub.config.ts` is code-as-config for UI visibility and editability only (54 lines, one committed file, one Hub container per shop), not a per-shop rules/config export. Net: the recorded intent is a sound, mostly-implemented base for a tool-driven middleware over WooCommerce and the homepage, but it would have to be amended to admit a WordPress-core port (menus, pages, media), to close the shortcut write paths or declare them, and to make the log universal before "everything is in Attività, undoable" holds for an LLM caller.
