This has no bearing on readiness to expose the codebase as LLM middleware; it is informational housekeeping (mark the two design docs as superseded by the implementation), relevant only to a hypothetical rewrite-from-docs path.

#### C7 [confirmed · strength] The order-sources feature genuinely parses free-text LLM output (fenced JSON, joined multi-part answers, truncated answers repaired to the last whole item, alias keys, CSV, numbered lines) and matches it to the shop with an explicit ambiguity refusal, and the LLM prompt itself ships in the i18n dictionaries.

- **Evidence**: src/server/vetrina/order-sources/format.ts: fences :112, multi-value scan :116-149, trailing-comma/comment relax :152, parseCut :189-206, ITEMS/SKU/NAME/RANK alias keys :75-80, CSV :256-279, per-line :280-294; match.ts :73-84 SKU incl. code-inside-name, :87-104 name match returning 'ambiguous'; order-sources.test.ts 240 lines; prompt at src/i18n/dictionaries/catalog/sources.en.ts:72; docs/order-sources.md:100-128 matches the code's behaviour.
- **Implication**: This is the one place where the recorded intent ('an LLM's answer as it came') is fully honoured, but it is bespoke to one feature: an LLM-driven middleware would want structured tool inputs (which the registry provides) rather than tolerant free-text parsing, so this module is a reusable utility, not a pattern to generalise. Its upload+apply path is also one of the two proposal-bypassing shortcuts (C4).
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Every cited location is literally true of the code. /home/user/woo-hub/src/server/vetrina/order-sources/format.ts: fence extraction at :112 (regex over ```…``` or EOF); top-level multi-value bracket scan at :116-149 (valuesIn, pushes each closed value and a final {cut:true} fragment at :147); trailing-comma / `//` comment relax at :152; parseCut at :189-206 walks back to the last `}`/`]`, closes open brackets via closers() and JSON.parses the prefix, i.e. repair to the last whole element; alias key lists ITEMS_KEYS/NAME_KEYS/ORIGIN_KEYS/SKU_KEYS/ITEM_NAME_KEYS/RANK_KEYS at :75-80 (case-insensitive via pick :66-73); CSV with header regexes :252-254 and header-driven field parsing :263-279; per-line numbered/bulleted parsing :280-294 (strips "1. ", "- ", "•", splits a leading code). match.ts: bySkuOf :73-84 matches by skuKey and then by any digit-bearing token >=6 chars inside the name (code-inside-name); byNameOf :87-104 returns 'none' | 'ambiguous' | ids, with 'ambiguous' when several candidates have every word and none is the exact-word-count one (:102-103); the ambiguous flag is propagated to unmatched at :116,:121, so the refusal is explicit. order-sources.test.ts is exactly 240 lines (wc) with 21 tests, all passing (vitest run). The prompt is in the i18n dictionaries: src/i18n/dictionaries/catalog/sources.en.ts:72 and sources.it.ts:90 (`llmPrompt`), rendered/copied from the UI in src/components/vetrina/catalog/sources/OrderSourcesSheet.tsx:383,492. The test at order-sources.test.ts:209-222 asserts docs/order-sources.md contains the Italian prompt and that the prompt's example parses; docs/order-sources.md:100-128 lists fences/trailing commas, joined answers, truncated answers, bare lists, alias keys, rank, CSV, per-line, and SKU/name/ambiguous matching, matching the code. Minor nuance only: the CSV code begins at :252/:257 rather than :256, and the prompt reaches users only via the UI dictionary (not via the API/tool surface), which is consistent with the claim's wording. The apply route (src/app/api/v1/order-sources/[id]/apply/route.ts:9-13) does start an operation directly (202, undo via /operations) rather than a proposal, consistent with the implication, though C4 itself was not in scope.
  - Corrected claim: The order-sources feature genuinely parses free-text LLM output (fenced JSON, joined multi-part answers, truncated answers repaired to the last whole item, alias keys, CSV, numbered lines) and matches it to the shop with an explicit ambiguity refusal, and the LLM prompt itself ships in the i18n dictionaries (sources.en.ts:72, sources.it.ts:90), surfaced only through the UI sheet, with a test pinning the docs to the Italian prompt.
- **Skeptic** (stands, high): Factual evidence checked and holds: src/server/vetrina/order-sources/format.ts:112 (fence regex), :189-206 (parseCut repairs to last whole element); match.ts:73-84 (SKU incl. code inside a name), :87-104 (byNameOf returns "ambiguous" when several candidates share every word and none is word-count exact); prompt at src/i18n/dictionaries/catalog/sources.en.ts:72 (llmPrompt); docs/order-sources.md:100-128 describes the same behaviour. The implication also stands, and the code strengthens it rather than undermining it: (1) the tolerant parsing sits at the DATA layer (the ranking text an external LLM produced for the owner), not at the tool-call layer. The action that changes the shop, `sourceOrder`, is already a registered kind with a strict zod input (`sourceId: z.uuid()`, `term`) at src/server/vetrina/operations/input.ts:156-160 and is described with a JSON-Schema example in registry.ts:132-140, so an LLM middleware already has the structured path the auditor says it would want; nothing in the parser needs generalising. (2) The upload endpoint itself accepts the agreed clean JSON first (src/server/api/order-sources.ts:20-46, docs/order-sources.md:91-100) and only falls back to tolerant reading, so it is already compatible with a structured caller. (3) The C4 remark is accurate: docs/api.md:17-19 explicitly lists `POST /order-sources/{id}/apply` as one of the two one-call "shortcuts that predate proposals", and src/server/api/order-sources.ts:62-66 calls operations.startOperation directly with no proposal. One nuance the auditor left out, which softens but does not reverse C4: the same `sourceOrder` kind is reachable through /proposals (operations.md:14-16 lists it among catalog kinds; proposals.ts handles any non-page kind), apply supports dryRun (route.ts:17, api/order-sources.ts:65), and the started operation is logged and undoable like any other (route.ts:11-12). So the shortcut is a redundant bypass, not the only route. Severity "strength" is right: the module is a genuine, well-tested (240-line test file) data-cleaning utility whose scope is correctly bounded; a competent engineer would reach the same "reusable utility, not a pattern to generalise" conclusion.
  - Corrected claim: The order-sources feature genuinely parses free-text LLM output (fenced JSON, joined multi-part answers, truncated answers repaired to the last whole item, alias keys, CSV, numbered lines) and matches it to the shop with an explicit ambiguity refusal, and the LLM prompt ships in the i18n dictionaries. This tolerance lives at the data layer (the ranking content) only: the action that changes the shop, `sourceOrder`, already has a strict typed input in the registry (input.ts:156-160, registry.ts:132-140) and the upload endpoint accepts clean JSON first, so the parser is a bounded reusable utility, not a pattern to generalise for an LLM-driven middleware. Its `POST /order-sources/{id}/apply` route is indeed one of the two documented proposal-bypassing shortcuts (docs/api.md:17-19), though the same `sourceOrder` action is also available through /proposals, and apply offers dryRun and lands in the undoable log.


<a id="advocates"></a>
## Advocates

### A. Keep this codebase and grow the programmatic layer in place (MCP/tool endpoints over the existing registry, expose admin capabilities through /api/v1, keep the UI)

## The case for A

### 1. The hard half is already built, tested, and shaped for an LLM caller

The dossier's strongest, uncontested findings all point the same way:

- **CONFIRMED (strength):** "All 21 Vetrina action kinds are reachable through /api/v1 with JSON Schema self-description, proposal/confirm, log and undo, and the API calls the same service functions the UI's server actions call." The UI and the API already share one path (`src/server/api/*.ts` -> `src/server/vetrina/operations/service.ts`).
- **CONFIRMED (strength):** "The registry really generates draft-2020-12 JSON Schema from the same Zod discriminated unions the server validates ... every function the docs' MCP table names already exists with the documented signature." I verified this in `src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.ts:245-266` (`describeActions()` with `z.toJSONSchema(schema, { io: "input", unrepresentable: "any" })`) and `docs/operations.md:84-106`, which names `readHomepage`, `readProducts`, `listTerms`, `listOperations`, `propose(action, "assistant")`, `confirmProposal`, `undoOperation` — all present with those signatures (`proposals.ts:105 propose(input, via)`, `:122 confirmProposal(id)`).
- **CONFIRMED (strength):** "The action registry is already validated as a machine-readable contract ... enforced by a test" (`registry.test.ts`, runs in CI).
- **CONTESTED but stands at high confidence for both verifiers on mechanics:** the proposal/confirm model "is correctly implemented for atomicity, drift and idempotency" (conditional UPDATE claim, sha256 re-preview, idempotent second confirm). The dissenting verifier's complaints (same token proposes and confirms; stuck `confirming`; digest samples 20 items) are additive hardening items, and that verifier itself says the model "should be kept in any keep/extract/fresh option".
- **CONFIRMED (strength):** write mechanics are already safe for unattended use: every Woo write re-reads live before writing, homepage writes are compare-and-swap to the plugin, catalog operations are serialized, undo is filed for 7 days.

An MCP layer over this is literally what `docs/operations.md:84-106` planned: "a thin layer over these pieces, in the same process (so its actions are filed with `via: "assistant"`)". `via: "assistant"` is already a legal `OperationVia` (`src/lib/vetrina/catalog-ops.ts:247`, `schema.ts:681/755`). A fresh repo would have to re-derive exactly this — registry, schema generation, proposals table, undo items, i18n-aware previews, the 29-rule `plan.test.ts` — before it reached parity with what exists today.

### 2. The "blocker" claims against keep were refuted or downgraded on inspection

Every claim the skeptics tried to raise to blocker severity was either refuted or shown to be identical across options:

- **Admin coverage gap** (CONTESTED): one verifier refuted "blocker" explicitly: the admin actions "are thin zod-validated wrappers over a Next-independent core (src/server/*), and the repo already ships the LLM-facing framework ... Exposing the admin capabilities is therefore route/registry plumbing on an existing template rather than the bulk of the work, and it is substantially cheaper in this codebase than in a fresh one." The other verifier agrees the gap exists but that it is "scoped", not structural. I confirmed `src/server/actions/collections.ts` is exactly `safeParse` -> `service.X` per export; `sync.ts`, `publish.ts`, `taxonomy.ts` follow the same shape.
- **"Five action files hold real domain logic"** (REFUTED): both verifiers refuted the implication. "Non-UI server code already consumes these action exports (sync/price-sync.ts:2, scheduler.ts:14, the cron route), so exposing them to an LLM/MCP caller does not strictly require moving them first"; "a layering/organisation nit, not a blocker ... use no request-scoped Next APIs". Only `preview.ts` and `config.ts` carry orchestration worth lifting, and that is "the same cost under keep or extract".
- **Next.js coupling** (REFUTED as stated, but the finding helps A): only 3 files import `next/*`; the real friction is `import "server-only"` in ~85 files, which "an MCP endpoint inside the Next process needs nothing" to deal with. Option A is the one option where this cost is zero.
- **globalThis runner / single process** (CONTESTED): both verifiers agree "Option (a)/(a+MCP-in-process) is free of this problem"; the refuting verifier adds it "is not a reason to prefer (a) over (b)" only because extraction need not force a second process — but A is the only option that cannot accidentally create one. The documented constraint ("a single app container per shop", `docs/deploy.md:309`) is preserved untouched.
- **Single-tenant** (CONTESTED): the refuting verifier: multi-shop "is a client-side configuration, not a schema or runtime rewrite" (one `{hub URL, HUB_API_TOKEN}` per shop). The owner runs one Hub per shop today; nothing in the request asks for in-process tenancy.
- **Codebase health** (CONTESTED, stands): typecheck 0 errors, lint clean, 1003 unit tests in 7.6s, CI with Playwright against real Postgres, docs as decision records with named constants. "Nothing in the health metrics argues for a fresh repo." The one caveat (DB suites not in CI) is a one-line CI job.

### 3. The WordPress gap is identical in every option, and A has the cheapest seam for it

**CONFIRMED (major):** "The Hub has no WordPress-side capability beyond the plugin's homepage routes ... no wp/v2 usage at all." This is true, it is the owner's own example (menus + categories), and it is **new scope in all three options** — the dossier says so repeatedly ("Neutral for keep/extract/fresh: the gap is identical"). What differs is the cost of adding it:

- A already has `VetrinaSource` (`src/server/vetrina/source.ts:24-38`), **CONFIRMED** as "a clean port ... with two implementations" and "the right shape to add further WP adapters (e.g. a MenuSource with a fixture)". `wp-source.ts` already does Basic auth over `adapters/http.ts` with error mapping to `VetrinaError` codes; a `wp/v2` sibling reusing that transport is a ~200-line module plus two env vars.
- A already has the plugin seam (`wc-gh/v1`) and the deploy path for new plugin routes, if the owner prefers menus to go through the plugin with the existing Woo-key auth — one verifier notes the decision record "already decides that WordPress-side features ... are reached by the Hub through wc-gh/v1 endpoints".
- A already has the action-registry pattern to make `menuSet`/`pageUpdate` undoable, proposal-gated kinds alongside the 21 existing ones, so the owner's mixed request ("move these categories and update the menu") becomes two or three proposals in one log, confirmable from the phone.

A fresh repo gets none of this for free; extraction gets it only after the server-only/alias/runner work is done.

### 4. Concrete plan

**Phase 0 — close the known safety gaps at the one chokepoint (week 1)**
1. `src/server/api/v1.ts` `serve()`: add a principal model. Minimal version: `HUB_API_TOKENS` as `name:token:scopes` list (keep `HUB_API_TOKEN` as the legacy single entry); resolve to `{ principal, scopes: read|propose|confirm|undo|admin }`; put it on `AsyncLocalStorage` next to `words.ts`'s language store.
2. `src/server/db/schema.ts`: add nullable `actor text` to `vetrina_operations` (:681) and `vetrina_proposals` (:755); migration; write it from `propose`/`startOperation`/`confirmProposal`/`undoOperation`.
3. Gate confirm: `src/app/api/v1/proposals/[id]/confirm/route.ts` requires the `confirm` scope; the assistant token gets `read|propose` only, so the "owner's yes" can only come from a human-held credential (or from the Authelia-signed Vetrina sheet, which already exists). Add `confirmedBy` to the proposal row.
4. Redirect the two one-call shortcuts (`src/server/api/catalog-order.ts:113`, `order-sources.ts:66`) through `propose(...)` unless the caller has the `admin` scope; the `order`/`sourceOrder` kinds already exist in the registry (`registry.ts:123,132`). Make `POST /operations/{id}/undo` require `undo` scope.
5. Backpressure in `serve()`: per-principal token bucket (Redis is already there, `cache/redis.ts`), cap of N open proposals per principal, refuse with `429`/`busy` when `isQueued()` depth exceeds a limit. Add `expires`/`confirming` recovery: a sweep that reopens rows stuck in `confirming` older than 5 minutes with no operation id.
6. Tests: `src/server/api/v1.test.ts` (auth/scope/status mapping, pure), extend `e2e/api.spec.ts` to one proposal round-trip per kind (data-driven over `describeActions()`), enable `RUN_DB_TESTS=1` in the CI e2e job which already has Postgres.

**Phase 1 — MCP endpoint in-process (weeks 1-2)**
7. `src/app/api/mcp/route.ts`: Streamable HTTP MCP server (add `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk`) wrapped by the same `serve()`-style auth (bearer first; OAuth later, as `docs/operations.md:104-106` plans). Register tools generated from `describeActions()`: one `propose_<kind>` tool per kind with `inputSchema = description.input`, plus `read_homepage`, `find_products`, `list_terms`, `list_operations`, `get_proposal`, `confirm_proposal`, `undo_operation`. Calls go straight to `propose(action, "assistant")`, which makes `via: "assistant"` lived rather than declared. Add `src/proxy.ts:117-118` exemption for `/api/mcp/` alongside `/api/v1/`.
8. Surface freshness: add `asOf`/`lastSeen` to `/catalog/*` responses from `collections/repo.ts indexInfo` (already computed).
9. Move the ~25 hardcoded Italian `VetrinaError` messages in `vetrina/service.ts`, `catalog.ts`, `wp-source.ts`, `fixture-source.ts` behind `words()` so an English MCP client gets English refusals (bounded cleanup; codes are already machine-readable).

**Phase 2 — expose the admin half through the same registry (weeks 3-6)**
10. Hoist private Zod schemas out of `"use server"` files into `src/server/<domain>/input.ts` (collections, overrides, orders, taxonomy, publish, sync, repair, store-edits, import, feeds): mechanical, ~14 files.
11. Add a second registry scope, `ops`, to `registry.ts`, with kinds for the owner-facing admin writes: `collectionRuleSave/Delete/Run`, `pricingRulesSave`, `taxonomyRulesSave`, `publishProducts`, `syncApply`, `storePullStart`, `importSkus`, `feedUpload`, `overridesSet`, `duplicateTrash`, `orderWorkflowSet`. Each gets `title/description/undo/example` and a preview; those that are dry-run-capable already (apply, publish, rebuild, repair) use their existing `dryRun` as the proposal preview. Blast-radius kinds (rebuild, resets) require the `admin` scope and a confirm.
12. Lift the orchestration that actually lives in action files into services: `actions/preview.ts` -> `src/server/sync/preview-service.ts` (planChunk/fetchAndPreview/previewFromStore/start+advanceStoreSync), `actions/config.ts` -> `src/server/pricing/rules-service.ts`; fix the two upward imports (`scheduler.ts:14`, `sync/price-sync.ts:2`) and move `src/components/catalog/drawer-data.ts` to `src/server/catalog/drawer-data.ts`. Same work every option would do; here it is done against a green test suite.
13. Add reads the UI has and the API lacks: term margin, standing term actions, section history, pricing/taxonomy/collection rules, publish delta, feeds and orders state — as `GET /api/v1/*` on existing service functions.
14. Persist the sync apply guard into `apply_audit` (status `running` row + advisory lock inside `applySync`) so an MCP-started apply and a scheduler apply cannot overlap.

**Phase 3 — the WordPress port (weeks 5-8, can overlap Phase 2)**
15. Amend `docs/hub-and-plugin.md:121` ("Navigation menus | Plugin") to "Hub, via wp/v2 or a wc-gh/v1 route": a one-row conscious amendment.
16. Add `WP_APP_USER`/`WP_APP_PASSWORD` to `src/lib/env.ts` (optional); `src/server/wordpress/client.ts` over `adapters/http.ts requestJson` for `wp/v2/menus`, `menu-items`, `pages`, `media`; a `MenuSource`/`PageSource` port with fixture implementations mirroring `VetrinaSource`.
17. Registry kinds `menuItemAdd/Move/Remove`, `pageUpdate`, `mediaAttach`, each with preview, CAS on `modified_gmt`, before/after items for undo. The owner's example becomes: `propose(termMove)` + `propose(menuItemAdd)` -> two summaries -> one yes each.

### 5. What it costs

- Roughly 6-8 weeks of one engineer plus the owner's confirmations, mostly plumbing on an existing template. No migration of users, deploy, Authelia, Caddy, or plugin; the UI keeps working throughout and serves as the human confirm channel.
- Added dependencies: `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk`; later an OAuth provider for remote MCP clients.
- The repo keeps carrying its known debts: `preview.ts`/`config.ts` orchestration in action files until step 12, Italian/English identifier mix, whole-blob snapshot readers in operator paths, in-process runner/scheduler. None blocks exposure; all are tractable in place.

### 6. Risks

- **Scope creep in the admin half**: 73 exports is a long list; the mitigation is to register only owner-facing writes and leave debug/export/health as UI.
- **The in-process runner**: the MCP endpoint must live in the Hub's Next process (or call `/api/v1`), never import the domain from a second process. This is a rule to document and lint for (an ESLint `no-restricted-imports` on `@/server/**` from any new `mcp-server/` package), not a code change.
- **Human-confirm enforcement**: Phase 0 step 3 is the one piece that is genuinely new design; if skipped, an LLM holding the token confirms its own proposals indistinguishably from a human (both verifiers flag this).
- **Test coverage of the API glue**: thin today; Phase 0 step 6 must land before the MCP endpoint is handed to a real model.

### 7. When A is the wrong choice

- **In-process multi-tenancy is required** (one middleware service for many shops with shared runner/scheduler): tenancy is cross-cutting across 24 tables and the globalThis coordinators; this is "the single largest argument for extract" and A would be fighting the one-Hub-per-shop design. One-Hub-per-shop with one MCP token per shop is fine; shared-process multi-shop is not.
- **The UI must be retired or the middleware must scale horizontally**: then the runner/scheduler/collections coordination must move to Postgres leases (the `media_jobs` pattern) and the server-only markers must go, and extraction (B) is the honest frame for that work.
- **The owner wants a WordPress-wide product first and Woo second**: if menus/pages/posts/media/settings are the center and products the periphery, the Hub's product-sync kernel (apply, publish, snapshot, ledger) becomes ballast and a WP-first core with a thin Woo adapter is a better shape.
- **The team will not do Phase 0**: exposing the current single-token API to an autonomous model without scopes, confirm gating and backpressure is unsafe in any option, but A is where it is most tempting to skip because "it already works".

**First milestone.** Weeks 1-4 deliver: (1) scoped principals and an `actor` column on proposals/operations, with `confirm`/`undo` scopes the assistant token does not hold, the two one-call shortcuts routed through `propose`, a per-principal rate limit and open-proposal cap in `serve()`, and the DB test suites running in CI; (2) `POST /api/mcp` (Streamable HTTP, in the Next process, behind the proxy exemption) exposing one `propose_<kind>` tool per registry kind plus read/confirm/undo tools, all filing `via: "assistant"`; (3) `asOf` on catalog reads and English-routed error messages; (4) the first two admin kinds registered end to end on the hoisted-schema pattern (`collectionRuleSave` with its existing preview, `publishProducts` using its dry run as the proposal preview); (5) a `MenuSource` port with fixture plus a `wp/v2` client behind optional `WP_APP_*` env, and a `menuItemAdd` kind with CAS and undo. Acceptance: an LLM connected to the MCP endpoint handles "create category X from these products, put it on the homepage rail, and add it to the main menu" as three proposals the owner confirms from the Vetrina sheet, each visible and undoable in Attività with `via: assistant` and the confirming human recorded — with the UI untouched and the demo-shop e2e suite still green.

### B. Extract the core from this repo into a package plus a separate headless service (shared Postgres), keep the Next UI as a client of it; same repo as an npm-workspaces monorepo

## The thesis

The dossier says, repeatedly and with verifier agreement, that the domain is *already* framework-free in substance and only framework-bound in packaging: "exactly 3 files import from `next`", "core/ is fully framework-free", "vitest already proves the domain runs under plain Node by aliasing server-only to a stub", "all 23 tables ... are plain Postgres ... none of it is tied to Next". Option B is the option that makes that fact *enforced* instead of *incidental*. Every structural defect the dossier lists for the admin half exists because no boundary stops it: Zod schemas trapped as private consts in `"use server"` files ("a route cannot reuse them until they are moved into a shared module"), 898 lines of sync planning in `src/server/actions/preview.ts` imported *upward* by `src/server/sync/price-sync.ts:2` and `src/server/scheduler.ts:14`, a server module misfiled under `src/components/catalog/drawer-data.ts` and imported by two actions, 87 `import "server-only"` headers on code that has no React in it. Under option A each of these is a hygiene chore someone may or may not do; under B each becomes a compile error in `packages/hub-core`, and the work the dossier says must happen anyway ("hoisting schemas, unifying the error model, and adding server-side orchestration — not adding routes over the existing actions") gets done once, by the type checker, in the direction that the LLM surface needs.

The case does **not** rest on the 21 Vetrina kinds. The dossier is right that those are done: "an MCP layer can be one thin adapter per kind over propose/confirm/undo". If that were the whole target, option A wins and this document should lose. The case rests on three things the dossier confirms and A cannot fix cheaply.

### 1. The 82 UI-only capabilities are the bulk of the work, and Next is the wrong host for them

CONTESTED-but-stands: "73 of its server-action exports across 19 files have zero /api/v1 counterpart ... store pull (start/advance/cancel), sync preview/plan and apply, publish, SKU import, feeds upload, pricing rules/margins config, taxonomy rules, smart collections CRUD, duplicates trash, orders workflow, overrides/locks, store variation edits, repair/rebuild". These are long-running, chunked, resumable jobs. Look at why they are shaped the way they are: `src/server/sync/apply-job.ts` header: "behind Cloudflare a request that takes over 100 seconds gets a 524 ... The apply now runs here, in the server process, and the tab asks how it is going every few seconds". `src/app/api/cron/pull-store/route.ts`: `export const maxDuration = 800`. The dossier: "a 2000-product sale takes an hour or two", pull/import/rebuild/publish are "chunked, run-id/audit-id-threaded loops driven from client components". The whole admin half is a set of workarounds for hosting hours-long store writes inside an HTTP-request runtime. An LLM caller needs these as *file-a-job-then-poll* operations with server-side orchestration (the dossier says exactly this: "the relevant shape is file-a-job-then-poll, which the operations service already has and the blocking 800-second cron routes do not"). A long-lived plain-Node service has no request deadline, no `maxDuration`, no Cloudflare 524 to design around, and can own the loop the client components drive today. Under A you would be writing new Next route handlers that re-create the apply-job pattern for pull, import, rebuild, publish and repair — five more process-local job maps.

### 2. One process per shop is a real constraint; the only question is *which* process owns it

CONFIRMED: "the operations runner ... is a per-process in-memory promise chain ... recover() in any newly started process marks every row with status 'running'/'undoing' as failed regardless of which process owns it"; "Run a single app container per shop" (docs/deploy.md:309). Both verifiers agree the single-writer constraint is independent of keep-vs-extract — "extraction does not force a separate process". True. But A has a cost here the dossier states and then under-weights: *every deploy of the UI kills in-flight operations*. `runner.ts:19-22`: "A restart loses the queue: what it left running is marked interrupted (recover()), never resumed." Today a CSS fix shipped while a 2000-product sale is 40 minutes in marks that sale interrupted. With 158 commits in six weeks and an LLM about to drive long operations, this is the operational hazard that matters. Under B the UI (which changes often) and the writer (which should change rarely) are different containers; a UI deploy cannot interrupt anything. The runner's in-memory queue becomes acceptable *because* it lives in the one process that is never redeployed casually, and the dossier's proposed Postgres-lease rewrite ("a claimable job table with lockedUntil + FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, as media_jobs already does") can be deferred instead of being a prerequisite.

The honest architectural cost B forces, and A does not: the Next app must stop calling `startOperation`/`queueRun`/`startScheduler` in-process. That is the one "genuinely architectural change extraction would force". It is bounded: the UI already reads progress by polling rows ("writes its progress to its row as it goes, which is what the screens poll", runner.ts), so the UI can keep reading Postgres directly through the package and send *writes* over HTTP to the service. The service's HTTP surface is the existing `/api/v1` grown to cover the admin kinds, which is the work that has to happen for the LLM anyway. The UI becomes the first client of the API the LLM will use, which is also the cheapest way to get the "contract tests per kind" the dossier says the API lacks.

### 3. The WordPress half is new scope, and B gives it a home that A does not

CONFIRMED: "there is no wp/v2 usage at all, so menus, non-homepage pages, posts and the media library are not Hub capabilities in any class"; the owner's own example ("change the navigation menu alongside Woo categories") requires "a `wp/v2` client with a separate credential, menu/page/post/media/settings adapters, a generic capabilities/version negotiation". Also CONFIRMED: the right pattern exists — "VetrinaSource ... is a clean port with a fixture implementation, which is a sound pattern to replicate". A `MenuSource`/`PageSource`/`MediaSource` with fixtures is a package concern: it should be testable in plain Node with the fixture, as `src/server/vetrina/fixture-source.ts` already is for the homepage. In A, each new adapter inherits `server-only`, the `@/` alias, and lives next to React code; in B it lands in `packages/hub-core/src/wp/` with its fixture and its tests and no framework. Also note the authentication fork the dossier identifies (Woo keys authenticate only `wc-*`; wp/v2 needs an Application Password or plugin proxy): `src/lib/env.ts` is where that credential goes, and env.ts is one of the files that must lose `server-only` for any non-Next host — B does that on day one.

### Secondary arguments

- **The dev loop for domain work gets 100x lighter.** README measures `next dev` climbing to the 4 GB heap limit (dossier: "root-caused to `next dev` retaining module graphs"). Domain tests already run under vitest in plain Node in 7.6s. A package means the domain never loads under `next dev` at all when you are not touching UI.
- **The DB test suite finally runs in CI.** "17 files / 188 tests ... skipped unless RUN_DB_TESTS=1 ... CI never runs them". Those suites cover "the operations queue, undo, restart, sync ledger and concurrency locks" — precisely the mutation layer an LLM wraps. A `packages/hub-core` CI job with a Postgres service is a 15-line workflow addition and has no Next build in its way.
- **The MCP host is a plain Node process anyway.** The docs plan "the MCP endpoint itself (Streamable HTTP, in the Next app)". Long-lived streaming connections in Next route handlers behind Cloudflare are the same 100-second problem as apply-job. I am not designing the LLM side; I am noting that whatever hosts MCP wants to be the long-lived process that owns the runner, and that process should not be the UI.
- **Identity/authorization lands at one chokepoint.** The dossier's blocker ("one shared bearer token ... no per-caller identity ... the 'owner's yes' is whoever holds the token") is fixed at `serve()` in both A and B. But in B the UI is itself an HTTP client that forwards Authelia's `Remote-User` (which `src/proxy.ts` already carries) as the actor, so "confirm must come from a human channel" becomes "confirm is accepted only with a forwarded Remote-User, never with the LLM's token" — natural once UI writes go over the wire, awkward while the UI calls `startOperation` directly with no request context.

## The plan (same repo, npm workspaces)

**Step 0 — scaffolding (days 1-2).** Root `package.json` gains `"workspaces": ["apps/*", "packages/*"]`. Create `packages/hub-core/` and `apps/service/`. The Next app stays at the root for now (moving `src/app`, `src/components` to `apps/web` is cosmetic and can wait). Add `HUB_ROLE=web|service` to `src/lib/env.ts` on day one: `startScheduler()`, `queueRun()`, `collections/runner.ts` and `apply-job.ts` throw if `HUB_ROLE !== "service"`. This makes a second writer impossible by construction before any file moves, closing the dual-writer risk the dossier flags for the whole transition.

**Step 1 — move the domain (week 1).** `git mv core packages/hub-core/src/core`, `git mv src/server packages/hub-core/src/server` *except* `src/server/actions/`, which stays with the UI; `git mv src/lib/{env,auth,vetrina,schedule,sizes,skus,gtin,match,plan,publish-batching,site}.ts` into the package; `git mv src/i18n/dictionaries/catalog/ops.*.ts` and `src/i18n/config.ts`/`dictionary.ts` into `packages/hub-core/src/i18n/` (the dossier: "the coupling is to data files, not to Next"; `provider.tsx` and `server.ts` stay in the UI). `sed -i '/^import "server-only";$/d'` across the 87 files; delete `src/test/server-only-stub.ts` and the vitest alias. Keep the UI compiling without touching its 96 import sites by pointing root `tsconfig.json` paths `@/server/*` → `packages/hub-core/src/server/*` and `@core/*` → `packages/hub-core/src/core/*`; later replace with `@woo-hub/core` imports via codemod. Fix the two upward imports as the compiler reports them: `scheduler.ts:14` → import `refreshStaleCatalog` from `catalog/refresh.ts` directly (the dossier confirms `runKicksdbRefresh` is a thin wrapper over it); `sync/price-sync.ts:2` → see step 2. Move `src/components/catalog/drawer-data.ts` to `packages/hub-core/src/server/catalog/drawer.ts`. Add `.github/workflows/ci.yml` job `core-db-tests` with a Postgres service running `RUN_DB_TESTS=1 vitest run` inside the package.

**Step 2 — hoist what the LLM needs out of `"use server"` (week 2).** `src/server/actions/preview.ts` → `packages/hub-core/src/server/sync/store-sync.ts` (planChunk, fetchAndPreview, previewStoreChunk, previewFromStore, startStoreSync/advanceStoreSync); `actions/config.ts` → `pricing/rules-edit.ts` (updatePricing, savePricingRules); `actions/store-edit.ts` and `actions/duplicates.ts` Woo calls → `woo/store-edit.ts`, `woo/duplicates.ts` with the `assertSnapshotIsThisStore` guard kept. Every private Zod schema in the 19 admin action files moves to `packages/hub-core/src/contracts/<area>.ts` as a named export. The action files in the UI shrink to `parse → call → map error`, which is what the dossier says ~14 of 19 already are.

**Step 3 — the headless service (week 2-3).** `apps/service/src/main.ts`: plain Node HTTP (node:http or Hono — anything that speaks Web `Request`/`Response`). Port `src/server/api/v1.ts` `serve()` to take a Web `Request` and return a Web `Response` (Next route handlers accept exactly that signature, so the same handler modules serve the Next routes during the transition). Mount the 14 existing `/api/v1` handlers, the 4 `/api/cron` handlers, `/health`. Call `startScheduler()`, `recover()`, and the collections runner on boot. `apps/web` runs with `HUB_ROLE=web`, `src/instrumentation.ts` stops starting the scheduler. `docker-compose.yml` and `deploy/` gain the `service` container; `docs/deploy.md:309` is rewritten from "a single app container" to "a single *service* container; the web container is stateless and may be redeployed freely".

**Step 4 — UI writes through the service (week 3-4).** `src/server/actions/vetrina-operations.ts` and `vetrina.ts` (the 21 kinds) and then the admin actions call `apps/service` over an internal URL with an internal secret and `X-Actor: <Remote-User>`. Reads keep going straight to Postgres through the package (shared Postgres is the point). `serve()` grows `principal` (token id or forwarded actor) and `vetrina_proposals`/`vetrina_operations` gain `actor` columns (the dossier: "the schema change is small (two columns) and the gating point is one function"). Confirm is refused without a human actor. Route `POST /catalog/order` and `/order-sources/{id}/apply` through `propose` while touching them.

**Step 5 — first WP adapter (week 4).** `packages/hub-core/src/server/wp/menus.ts` implementing a `MenuSource` port (`list`, `items`, `addItem`, `moveItem`, `removeItem`) with `fixture-menus.ts`, authenticated by new `WP_APP_USER`/`WP_APP_PASSWORD` in env.ts (Woo keys cannot reach `wp/v2`; the dossier confirms this). Register `menuItemAdd`/`menuItemMove`/`menuItemRemove` kinds in `registry.ts` with proposal/confirm/undo like the homepage kinds. Amend `docs/hub-and-plugin.md:121` (the "Navigation menus | Plugin" row) consciously, as the dossier says must happen under any option.

## What it costs

- Roughly 3-4 weeks before any *new* capability ships, versus days for an in-process MCP over the 21 Vetrina kinds under A.
- A second container per shop, one more internal secret, an HTTP hop on every UI write (reads unaffected), and compose/Caddy/Authelia changes in `deploy/` and `docs/deploy.md`.
- Moving ~150 files and rewriting the `@/` alias story; a period where two tsconfigs and two vitest configs coexist.
- The i18n split: the ops dictionaries (663 lines) travel with the core; `page.ts:90` reads UI-section dictionaries beyond the ops slice and must be narrowed or those slices move too. ~25 hardcoded Italian `VetrinaError` messages in `service.ts`/`catalog.ts`/`wp-source.ts` should go behind `words()` while files are being touched.
- `next/cache` `refresh()` in two actions stays in the web app; UI freshness after a write now depends on polling the row the service updates, which the screens already do.

## What it risks

- **Dual writer during transition** — mitigated by the `HUB_ROLE` guard in step 0, before any move.
- **Alias and build churn** eating the schedule; mitigated by keeping the UI's import paths unchanged via tsconfig paths until the end.
- **Scope creep into a rewrite.** The rule must be `git mv` plus compiler-driven fixes only; no rewriting of `apply.ts`/`publish.ts`/`scheduler.ts`, whose orchestration "would have to be re-specified from code and DB tests" and whose planners are the trusted spec.
- **The runner's in-memory queue remains a single point of loss** on a *service* restart. Acceptable because service deploys become rare; the media_jobs-style lease remains available as a later step with no change of plan.

## When B is the wrong choice

- If the target scope is the 21 Vetrina kinds plus reads, and admin capabilities and WordPress-core features are out of scope. Then A (in-process MCP, `propose(action, "assistant")`) is days of work and B is a month of plumbing for nothing. Both verifiers of the Vetrina claim effectively say this: "an MCP/tool adapter can wrap that API without extracting or untangling src/server/vetrina".
- If the team is one owner plus Claude and operating a second container (and debugging two processes' logs) is a real burden. B trades code coupling for deployment coupling; that is only a win if someone will run it.
- If UI deploy frequency drops and long operations are rare, the "deploy kills the queue" argument loses most of its weight.
- If the owner is not willing to amend `docs/hub-and-plugin.md` and take on WP-core writes in the Hub at all. Then the WP adapter home is irrelevant and the remaining case (long jobs, CI for DB tests) is weaker than its cost.
- If multi-shop in one process ever becomes the goal, B is *also* insufficient: tenancy is "cross-cutting rather than additive" and would argue for the dossier's option C style persistence rebuild. B assumes the one-stack-per-shop model stays, which is the current documented deployment.

**First milestone.** Weeks 1-4 deliver: (1) `packages/hub-core` holding core/, src/server (minus actions/), the shared src/lib modules, the ops i18n slice and the hoisted preview/config/store-edit/duplicates orchestration, with `server-only` removed, Zod schemas exported from `packages/hub-core/src/contracts/*`, the two upward imports fixed, and the 17 *.db.test.ts suites running in a CI job against Postgres; (2) `apps/service`, a plain-Node process that is the sole owner of the scheduler, operations runner and collections runner (`HUB_ROLE` guard makes the web process refuse to run them), serving the existing /api/v1 and /api/cron handlers via a Web-Request `serve()` with a `principal` on proposals/operations and confirm refused without a forwarded human actor; (3) the Next UI running `HUB_ROLE=web`, reading Postgres through the package and sending the 21 Vetrina writes to the service over HTTP with `Remote-User` as actor; (4) one new WordPress-core adapter, `packages/hub-core/src/server/wp/menus.ts` behind a `MenuSource` port with a fixture, authenticated by `WP_APP_USER`/`WP_APP_PASSWORD`, exposing `menuItemAdd`/`menuItemMove`/`menuItemRemove` kinds in `registry.ts` with JSON Schema, proposal/confirm and undo. End-to-end demonstration: a bearer-token client (standing in for the LLM) calls GET /api/v1/actions, proposes `categoryAdd`/`move` for a set of products and `menuItemAdd` pointing the navigation menu at that category, a human confirms both via the UI (actor recorded), the service runs them in order through the single runner, both appear in the operations log and both are undoable — one mixed WP+Woo request driven headlessly, with the UI as a client of the same service.

### C. Start a fresh repo for the middleware, using this codebase and its docs/tests as the specification; port features deliberately

## Thesis

The dossier is mostly a brief for "keep", and I will not pretend otherwise: the Vetrina action layer is genuinely good and the planners are already a mock-free executable spec. But read the same findings from the owner's stated goal — *an LLM drives the middleware; customer needs mix WordPress and WooCommerce* — and a different picture appears. Almost every CONFIRMED finding says the thing the owner wants does not exist yet, and the things that do exist are either framework-free (and therefore cheap to port) or process-bound (and therefore have to be redesigned anyway). Option C is the honest acknowledgement that the middleware the owner describes is a *new product* that happens to share a domain with Store Hub, not an exposure of Store Hub.

The case rests on four verified facts.

### 1. The WordPress half is new scope in every option — so the "spec" covers half the target

CONFIRMED: "The Hub has no WordPress-side capability beyond the plugin's homepage routes: it uses wc/v3 and wc-gh/v1 only, never wp/v2, so navigation menus, non-homepage pages/posts and the media library are not in any class." CONFIRMED: the VetrinaSource port "models exactly one configured page's blocks and rails, not WordPress pages, posts, menus or media in general." Verifiers on both sides agree the owner's own example ("change the navigation menu with these product categories") needs "new adapters and either new auth or new plugin routes" — the Woo consumer keys "only authenticate wc-* namespaces" (src/server/vetrina/wp-source.ts:19-22).

That is not a gap to fill around the edges; it is the second pillar of the product, and the current code has zero pillars under it: no wp/v2 client, no Application Password credential path in src/lib/env.ts (only WOO_BASE_URL/WOO_CONSUMER_KEY/WOO_CONSUMER_SECRET at :43-45), and a decision record (docs/hub-and-plugin.md:121,129-133) that explicitly says menus, media and WP-CLI are *never* the Hub's job. In a fresh repo the WordPress port is designed in from day one with its own credential, its own `WordPressSource` interface beside the ported `VetrinaSource`, and its own fixture. In the existing repo it would be bolted onto a codebase whose own README, decision record and `site-guard` all assume the opposite.

### 2. The runtime model is exactly what a middleware must not have, and it is cross-cutting, not additive

CONFIRMED (stands, high): "The operations runner that executes confirmed proposals is a per-process in-memory promise chain on globalThis with no persisted queue or lease ... recover() in any newly started process marks every row with status 'running'/'undoing' as failed regardless of which process owns it." I checked src/server/vetrina/operations/runner.ts:11-25 — the header says so in plain words: "A restart loses the queue: what it left running is marked interrupted, never resumed." The same pattern covers the scheduler (scheduler.ts:170, started from src/instrumentation.ts inside the Next server), the collections runner, the apply-job Map, the publishing Set; 27 files in src/server touch globalThis. Docs require "a single app container per shop" (docs/deploy.md:309).

The "keep" camp's answer is "host the MCP endpoint in the same Next process". That is a real option, but notice what it commits the owner to: the LLM-facing middleware is forever a side-door of a React app, scaled and deployed with the UI, restarted when the UI restarts, with its confirm-then-run guarantee resting on a Node promise chain. An LLM caller that confirms a 2,000-product sale (writes.ts:63-68: "an hour or two") and then sees the container redeployed gets an "interrupted" row and no resumption. The fix the dossier itself names — "a claimable job table with lockedUntil + FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, as media_jobs already does" — is cheap to *design in* and awkward to *retrofit* across five runners plus `service.ts:64`, which derives the API's `queued` flag from in-memory `isQueued()`. A fresh repo makes the job table the primitive and every runner a consumer of it.

### 3. The authorization model is absent, and absence is easier to fill in a design than in a retrofit

CONFIRMED (stands, high): "Authentication for the programmatic API is a single shared bearer token (env.HUB_API_TOKEN, src/server/api/v1.ts:57-61) with no per-caller identity, no scopes, and no read-only mode ... a token holder can propose and confirm its own proposals indistinguishably from any other API caller." Both verifiers, including the one who downgraded it from blocker, agree on the remedy: "gate confirm on a human channel ... per-token scopes alone would not stop an LLM from confirming its own proposals." Add: `via` "assistant"/"scheduler" are declared but never written (CONFIRMED), the scheduler logs to `collection_changes`/`apply_audit` rather than the operations log, and two API writes plus undo bypass proposals (catalog-order.ts:113, order-sources.ts:66, operations/[id]/undo/route.ts:14). The codebase is single-tenant with "no shop column in any table" (src/server/woo/site-guard.ts:18).

For a human-driven app behind Authelia this was all fine. For an LLM-driven middleware, principal identity, scopes, a human-only confirm channel, and one universal log are the *first* design decisions, and they touch every table that records a write. A fresh schema puts `principal_id`, `tenant_id` (even if one tenant at first) and `via` on proposals, operations and audit rows from migration 0001, instead of adding nullable columns to 24 tables and auditing 107 server-action exports for which ones still write unlogged.

### 4. What is actually reusable is small, pure, and ports verbatim — which is the definition of "use the codebase as the spec"

CONFIRMED: "The pure 'plan' modules are tested as behaviour specs with zero mocks and store-rule oracles ... so they are a checkable spec for a rewrite." CONFIRMED: the registry "generates draft-2020-12 JSON Schema from the same Zod discriminated unions the server validates." CONFIRMED: core/ is framework-free ("core/ is fully framework-free"). The dossier also records that the 85-87 `import "server-only"` lines, the `@/`/`@core/` aliases, the 68 hardcoded Italian error strings, and the i18n AsyncLocalStorage are all "mechanical" — and mechanical friction is precisely what you shed by copying files into a new tree rather than inheriting a Next build.

Concretely portable with their tests (I counted): core/core-spine.ts, core/config.ts, core/collections.ts (2,222 lines, 12 test files, 2,343 test lines); src/server/vetrina/operations/{input.ts, page-input.ts, registry.ts, plan.ts, order-plan.ts, margin-plan.ts, proposal-plan.ts} and their *.test.ts; src/server/woo/{publish-plan, repair-plan, media-plan, live-check, sanitize, takeover-plan, missing-sizes-plan}.ts and tests; src/lib/vetrina/*; src/server/vetrina/order-sources/{format,match}.ts (CONFIRMED robust LLM-output parser, 240 test lines); src/server/adapters/http.ts (retry/backoff). The Woo client (695 lines) "depends on env, the http adapter, ./paginate and zod, so it is extractable into a package without a rewrite." The 23-table Drizzle schema is "plain Postgres, keyed by stable ids ... none of it is tied to Next" — copy src/server/db/schema.ts and drizzle/ wholesale, then add the identity/tenant columns.

What is *not* portable and must be re-derived: the I/O orchestrators (woo/apply.ts 1,029 lines, woo/publish.ts 761, scheduler.ts 870 — ~2,660 lines covered only by RUN_DB_TESTS-gated suites CI never runs), and the orchestration inside the "use server" files (actions/preview.ts 898, actions/config.ts 394). The dossier is right that this is where the rewrite risk concentrates — but note that under "keep" these same files must be *moved* anyway (two domain modules import them upward: scheduler.ts:14, sync/price-sync.ts:2), and their business rules are already in tested planners; what is re-specified is sequencing, audit writes and failure isolation.

## The plan

**Phase 0 — spec consolidation (3-4 days).** The dossier confirms "the code, not the docs, is the accurate spec today" (vetrina-customer-hub.md says "Not merged", "No new database tables"; catalog-centric-redesign.md says "not yet implemented"). Write `SPEC.md` in the new repo from the two current contract docs (docs/api.md, docs/operations.md, both 2026-10-30) plus docs/vetrina-catalog.md §3, and amend the one-writer table from docs/hub-and-plugin.md with rows for navigation, pages, media. Record the reversed decisions explicitly.

**Phase 1 — skeleton (week 1).** New repo `store-middleware`: plain Node 22 + TypeScript, Hono (or Fastify) for HTTP, Drizzle + pg, Zod 4.4.3 (same version, so `z.toJSONSchema` output is identical). No `server-only`, no path aliases beyond `#core/*`. Copy drizzle/ migrations and src/server/db/schema.ts; add migration `0001_principal_tenant.sql` (principal_id, tenant_id, via NOT NULL on vetrina_proposals, vetrina_operations, apply_audit, collection_changes). Copy core/ and the planners listed above with tests; `vitest run` green on day 3 proves the port.

**Phase 2 — the write spine (week 1-2).** Port src/server/vetrina/operations/{proposals.ts, service.ts, repo.ts, writes.ts, remove.ts, order.ts, page.ts, layout.ts, words.ts} and src/server/woo/client.ts + adapters/http.ts. Replace runner.ts with `jobs/` : a `jobs` table claimed with FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED + locked_until (lift the pattern from src/server/woo/media.ts:166-189), a worker loop with worker identity so recovery only reclaims *expired* leases. Route `order`/`sourceOrder` through `propose` (the kinds exist: registry.ts:123,132); make undo a proposal kind. Implement `serve()` with per-principal tokens, scopes (`read`, `propose`, `confirm`, `admin`), a rate limit and open-proposal cap at the one chokepoint. Port the 5 e2e/api.spec.ts tests as HTTP contract tests against the fixture source.

**Phase 3 — the WordPress port (week 2-3).** `src/wp/` with `WordPressClient` over the same http adapter, authenticated by `WP_APP_USER`/`WP_APP_PASSWORD` (Application Password) — a separate credential from the Woo keys, per the verifiers. First adapters: `menus` (wp/v2/menus, wp/v2/menu-items), `pages`, `media` (multipart upload). Each behind a port with a fixture, mirroring src/server/vetrina/source.ts:24-38. New action kinds `menuItemAdd/menuItemMove/menuItemRemove`, `pageUpdate`, `mediaUpload`, registered in the same registry so GET /actions describes them with the same JSON Schema machinery; same propose/confirm/undo, same log.

**Phase 4 — deliberate porting of the admin half (weeks 3-8).** Of the ~82 UI-only server-action exports, port by value to the LLM, not by file: pricing rules (config.ts logic → `pricing/service.ts`), taxonomy rules, smart collections CRUD (already thin: actions/collections.ts:51-145), sync preview/plan/apply (re-derive from actions/preview.ts + woo/apply.ts using sync/*.db.test.ts as acceptance), publish, pull. Each becomes an action kind or a read; nothing is ported that no LLM flow needs (debug.ts, export.ts, duplicates trash can wait).

**Phase 5 — cutover.** The fresh service becomes the *only* writer; the existing Next UI is pointed at the service's HTTP API (or frozen), eliminating the second-writer problem rather than coordinating around it.

## Costs

- Re-deriving ~2,660 lines of Woo orchestration (apply/publish/scheduler) plus ~1,300 lines of action-file orchestration from code and opt-in DB tests. This is the real price; estimate 4-6 weeks of the total.
- Two codebases alive for several months; the UI keeps shipping on the old one or stalls.
- Loss of the e2e coupling to the demo shop until contract tests are rebuilt (the dossier notes extraction "would lose even the current e2e coupling").
- Re-litigating decisions is a risk the Phase 0 spec exists to prevent, but it costs discipline.
- Velocity signal: 158 commits in seven weeks, 108 authored as Claude — the team moves fast on the existing repo, and that momentum is forfeited for the port window.

## Risks

- Scope creep: a fresh repo invites "while we're here" rewrites of the planners. Mitigation: planners port verbatim with tests, by rule.
- Hidden behaviour in the orchestrators (per-variation merge, ledger-on-failure, snapshot patching) that only the skipped *.db.test.ts suites pin. Mitigation: run RUN_DB_TESTS=1 suites against the old repo first and treat them as the acceptance oracle for the new orchestrators.
- The WP core REST surface (menus) has its own quirks (menu-items locations, auto-add); the fixture must be built from a real site's responses.

## When C is the wrong choice

Be clear-eyed: C loses under any of these conditions.

1. **The LLM scope is the Vetrina scope.** If "drive the middleware" means the 21 catalog/homepage kinds plus reads, the dossier is unambiguous: an in-process MCP adapter over `propose/confirmProposal/undoOperation` is "additive, not a refactor" (docs/operations.md:84-106 already sketches it). Keep wins by weeks.
2. **One shop, one container, forever.** The runtime constraint is documented and accepted; if nobody will ever run the middleware beside a UI or scale it, the globalThis runners are tolerable and the job table is gold-plating.
3. **Menus can live in the plugin.** The dissenting verifier is right that the recorded pattern (Hub → wc-gh/v1 plugin route) can carry menu edits with the existing Woo-key auth. If the owner controls the plugin and is happy to add routes there, the WordPress pillar shrinks to a few routes and C's strongest argument weakens.
4. **The team cannot afford a port window.** With 108 of 158 commits authored by an LLM, velocity is cheap but not free; if feature pressure on the current UI is high, option B (extract core + vetrina into a package, swap runners in place) captures most of C's architectural wins at perhaps half the cost.

If none of those four holds — if the owner truly wants a WordPress+WooCommerce middleware with principal identity, a durable job model and multi-process deployability, driven by an LLM rather than screens — then the existing repo is the best specification it could have, and a fresh repo is the honest way to build it.

**First milestone.** Weeks 1-4 deliver `store-middleware`, a plain-Node service (no Next) that an LLM can drive through one real mixed request end to end: "create the category 'Winter Boots' from these SKUs, add it to the main navigation menu, and feature it as a homepage section".

Delivered:
1. Repo skeleton with Drizzle schema and migrations copied from /home/user/woo-hub/drizzle and src/server/db/schema.ts, plus migration 0001 adding principal_id/tenant_id/via to proposals, operations and audit tables.
2. Verbatim port, tests green, of core/ (core-spine, config, collections), src/server/vetrina/operations/{input,page-input,registry,plan,order-plan,margin-plan,proposal-plan}.ts, src/lib/vetrina/*, src/server/adapters/http.ts and src/server/woo/client.ts (categories, products, batch).
3. The write spine: ported proposals.ts/service.ts/repo.ts/writes.ts/page.ts/layout.ts with a `jobs` table claimed by FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED + locked_until and a worker with identity (pattern from src/server/woo/media.ts:166-189), replacing the globalThis runner; `serve()` with per-principal tokens and scopes (read/propose/confirm), confirm restricted to a `confirm`-scoped human credential, rate limit and open-proposal cap.
4. The WordPress port: `WordPressClient` over wp/v2 with Application Password credentials, a `MenuSource` port with fixture, and three new registered action kinds (menuItemAdd/menuItemMove/menuItemRemove) described by the same `describeActions()` JSON Schema and gated by the same propose/confirm/undo.
5. GET /actions, POST /proposals, POST /proposals/{id}/confirm, GET /operations, POST /operations/{id}/undo (now proposal-gated), GET /catalog/products, GET /catalog/terms, GET /homepage, GET /menus — with HTTP contract tests ported from e2e/api.spec.ts and run in CI against Postgres plus the fixture sources.

Acceptance: an LLM holding a propose-scoped token calls GET /actions, proposes `move` (existing kind) to place the SKUs in a new category, `menuItemAdd` to add that category to the primary menu, and `sectionAdd` to feature it on the homepage; each proposal returns a preview and digest; a separate confirm-scoped credential confirms all three; the jobs run through the leased worker, survive a service restart mid-run (resumed, not "interrupted"), appear in one operations log with via="assistant" and principal recorded, and each is undoable within 7 days. SPEC.md records the ported contracts and the amended one-writer table.


<a id="judgment"></a>
## Judgment

**Recommendation:** hybrid

| Option | Score | Why |
|---|---|---|
| A. Keep and grow in place (in-process MCP over the registry, admin kinds through /api/v1) | 8 | Fit 1-3 months: 9 (docs/operations.md:85-106 already specifies the in-process MCP; every named function exists; via:"assistant" is a legal OperationVia, so the Vetrina half is days of work). Regression risk: 8 (UI, deploy, Authelia, plugin untouched; same single process preserves today's runner guarantee; the only new design is confirm gating). Maintainability: 6 (the layering leaks and server-only markers stay unless someone enforces them; A relies on discipline). Honesty: 8 (its 'when A is wrong' section is accurate; it slightly underweights that a UI deploy interrupts in-flight operations today, runner.ts:19-22). |
| B. Extract core into packages/hub-core + separate headless service, UI as HTTP client | 6 | Fit: 5 (3-4 weeks of moves before any new capability; the owner's first mixed request lands later than under A). Regression risk: 5 (150 file moves, two tsconfigs, an HTTP hop on every UI write, a second container, compose/Caddy changes for a one-owner-plus-Claude team; the dual-writer hazard is real during transition even with the HUB_ROLE guard). Maintainability: 8 (the compiler enforces the boundary the dossier says is only incidental today; DB suites run in CI naturally). Honesty: 7 (correct that the deploy-kills-queue problem is real and that the work is the same work A must do; but a separate container does not fix the in-memory queue, it only moves which restart loses it, and the 'MCP streaming behind Cloudflare' point applies equally to its own service behind the same proxy). |
| C. Fresh repo using this codebase as spec | 5 | Fit: 3 (first mixed request only after re-deriving the write spine and a WP client; the Vetrina half is rebuilt rather than reused). Regression risk: 3 (~2,660 lines of apply/publish/scheduler orchestration plus ~1,300 lines of action-file orchestration re-derived from code and from DB tests CI never runs; two codebases alive; e2e coupling to the demo shop lost; 158 commits of momentum forfeited). Maintainability: 7 (if finished, the cleanest shape: jobs table, principal/tenant from migration 0001). Honesty: 6 (its four 'when C is wrong' conditions are candid and three of them hold for this owner; but 'authorization is absent so a fresh schema is easier' is overstated: the fix is two nullable columns and one function; and the runtime constraint is bounded, not cross-cutting, because media_jobs already shows the lease pattern). |
| Hybrid A+ (recommended): keep the repo and grow in place, adopt B's boundary discipline and C's durable job lease, no process or repo split in the next 3 months | 9 | Takes A's speed and zero-regression base, B's enforced boundaries (writer-role guard, exported Zod contracts, lint-enforced layering, DB suites in CI) without the deploy coupling, and C's one genuinely better primitive (a leased jobs table replacing the globalThis runner) as a month-3 item rather than a prerequisite. Leaves extraction (B) as a reversible later option because every hoist A+ does is the same move B would make. |

### Readiness verdict

Partially, and asymmetrically. The Vetrina half (21 catalog and homepage action kinds) is already structured as LLM-driven middleware: a registry that emits JSON Schema from the same Zod unions the server validates, a proposal/confirm flow with live re-preview, digest comparison, atomic claim and idempotent confirm, a 7-day undo, live re-reads before every Woo write, compare-and-swap on homepage writes, and a documented in-process MCP plan whose every named function exists. What is missing there is identity and authorization (one shared bearer token, no actor on proposals or operations, confirm accepted from the same token that proposed, two one-call shortcuts and undo outside the proposal gate) and backpressure, all fixable at one function and two columns. The admin half (sync preview/apply, publish, pull, import, pricing and taxonomy rules, collections CRUD, orders, duplicates, repair) is not exposed at all: 82 UI-only server actions, mostly thin wrappers over framework-free services but with ~1,300 lines of orchestration still inside two "use server" files and private Zod schemas that must be hoisted before any non-UI caller can use them. The runtime is single-process by design (globalThis runner, scheduler and job maps; a restart marks in-flight operations interrupted), which is acceptable for an MCP endpoint hosted in the same Next process and a known debt otherwise. And the codebase has no WordPress-core capability of any kind: no wp/v2 client, no WordPress credential, menus and media explicitly assigned to the plugin by the project's own decision record, so the owner's mixed WP+Woo example is new integration work in every option. Net: ready today for a safe, read-propose-confirm-undo LLM surface over catalog and homepage once principals and confirm gating are added; roughly six to eight weeks of in-place plumbing from covering the admin half and the first WordPress adapter; not a candidate for a rewrite.

### Rationale

## Decision

**Hybrid A+**: keep `/home/user/woo-hub`, grow the programmatic layer in place, and bind that growth with three rules borrowed from B and C. Do **not** split the process or the repo in the next three months. Named precisely:

1. From A: the in-process MCP endpoint over `describeActions()` (`src/server/vetrina/operations/registry.ts:245-266`), calling `propose(action, "assistant")` / `confirmProposal` / `undoOperation` (`src/server/vetrina/operations/proposals.ts:105,122`), exactly as `docs/operations.md:85-106` already specifies.
2. From B: a `HUB_ROLE`-style writer guard in `src/lib/env.ts` so `startScheduler()`, `queueRun()`, the collections runner and `apply-job.ts` refuse to run outside the designated writer process; every admin Zod schema hoisted out of `"use server"` files into exported contract modules; an ESLint `no-restricted-imports` rule forbidding `src/server/**` from importing `src/server/actions/**` or `src/components/**`; the 17 `*.db.test.ts` suites run in the CI e2e job, which already has Postgres (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:50-63`).
3. From C: `vetrina_proposals`/`vetrina_operations` get `actor`/`principal` columns on day one, and in month 3 the operations runner's globalThis promise chain is replaced with a leased `jobs` table (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` + `locked_until` + worker identity, the pattern already in `src/server/woo/media.ts:177-203`), so `recover()` reclaims only expired leases instead of marking every running row interrupted.

Re-evaluate B (package + separate service) at month 3, and only if UI deploy frequency keeps interrupting long operations after the lease lands, or horizontal scaling / in-process multi-tenancy becomes a requirement.

## Decisive findings (verified in the repo)

- **The Vetrina half is already the middleware.** `docs/operations.md:85-106` names the MCP tools and the functions behind them; all exist with the documented signatures. `via: "assistant"` is a legal value never written by production code: making it lived is one argument. This alone settles keep-vs-fresh for 21 of the action kinds and makes C's rebuild of the write spine pure cost.
- **The one real safety blocker is one function and two columns.** `serve()` in `src/server/api/v1.ts:56-71` checks a single `HUB_API_TOKEN` and the confirm route (`src/app/api/v1/proposals/[id]/confirm/route.ts:16`) passes no actor to `confirmProposal`. An LLM holding the token can propose and confirm its own work. Fix: principals + scopes in `serve()`, `actor` on the two rows, and confirm accepted only from a human channel (Authelia `Remote-User`, which `src/proxy.ts:9` already forwards, or a separate human-only credential). Identical work in all three options; cheapest where the function already exists.
- **The two one-call shortcuts** (`src/server/api/catalog-order.ts:113`, `src/server/api/order-sources.ts:66`) call `startOperation(..., "api")` without a proposal; the `order`/`sourceOrder` kinds are already in the registry, so routing them through `propose` is a redirect, not a design.
- **The runtime constraint is real but bounded.** `runner.ts:19-22`: "A restart loses the queue ... marked interrupted, never resumed." `apply-job.ts:16-17` is the same pattern. B is right that a CSS deploy today can interrupt a one-hour sale; B is wrong that a second container fixes it (it moves which restart loses the queue). The fix is the lease table, which exists in `media.ts`. C is wrong that this is cross-cutting: it is five coordinators, one pattern.
- **The admin half is plumbing on an existing template, with two exceptions.** 107 server-action exports, 21 with an API twin. Most are `safeParse -> service.X`. The exceptions are `src/server/actions/preview.ts` (898 lines) and `config.ts` (394 lines), which hold sync-planning and pricing-rule orchestration and are imported upward by `src/server/scheduler.ts:14` and `src/server/sync/price-sync.ts:2`. Hoisting them into `src/server/sync/` and `src/server/pricing/` is the same move under A, B or C. The 85 `import "server-only"` files cost nothing for an in-process MCP endpoint.
- **The WordPress half is new scope in every option.** No `wp/v2` call anywhere; `docs/hub-and-plugin.md:121` assigns navigation menus to the plugin. Woo keys authenticate only `wc-*` namespaces. The owner's example (menu + categories) needs a conscious decision in week 1: either new `wc-gh/v1` routes in the plugin (keeps one credential, follows the existing `VetrinaSource` pattern in `src/server/vetrina/source.ts:24-38` and `wp-source.ts`) or a `wp/v2` client with an Application Password. Neither option's codebase shape changes this; A has the cheapest seam (`adapters/http.ts` + the port/fixture pattern).
- **Health and velocity favour the existing repo.** 0 typecheck errors, lint clean, 1003 unit tests in ~7s, 158 commits in seven weeks ending 2026-10-31. The /api/v1 glue has 5 e2e tests and no unit tests; the registry contract is pinned by `registry.test.ts` in CI.

## What is borrowed from the losing options

- From **B**: the enforcement mindset (role guard, exported contracts, lint boundary, DB suites in CI), the `Remote-User`-as-actor idea for confirm, and the observation that UI deploys interrupt long jobs, which motivates the month-3 lease.
- From **C**: principal/actor columns from the first migration; the jobs-table primitive; spec hygiene (mark `docs/vetrina-customer-hub.md` and `docs/catalog-centric-redesign.md` superseded; amend the one-writer table with rows for navigation/pages/media).
- Rejected from B: a second container and an HTTP hop on every UI write now. Rejected from C: re-deriving `woo/apply.ts`, `woo/publish.ts`, `scheduler.ts` and the action-file orchestration from code and opt-in tests.

## Ordered plan (12 weeks)

**Week 1 (safety chokepoint, no new capability yet).** Principals and scopes (`read|propose|confirm|undo|admin`) in `serve()`; `actor` columns on proposals/operations; confirm refused without a human actor; the two shortcuts routed through `propose` unless `admin`; `undo` scoped; per-principal rate limit and open-proposal cap (Redis is present); a sweep reopening rows stuck in `confirming`; `HUB_ROLE` guard; ESLint boundary rule; `RUN_DB_TESTS=1` in the CI e2e job; `src/server/api/v1.test.ts`. Decide the WP auth path (plugin route vs Application Password).

**Weeks 2-3 (Vetrina half live for an LLM).** `src/app/api/mcp/route.ts` (Streamable HTTP, `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk`, in the Next process, exempted in `src/proxy.ts:118` beside `/api/v1/`); one `propose_<kind>` tool per registry kind plus read/confirm/undo tools, filing `via: "assistant"`; `asOf`/`lastSeen` on catalog reads from `collections/repo.ts indexInfo`; the ~25 hardcoded Italian `VetrinaError` messages in `vetrina/service.ts`, `catalog.ts`, `wp-source.ts`, `fixture-source.ts` moved behind `words()`; data-driven e2e round-trip over `describeActions()`.

**Weeks 3-6 (admin half).** Hoist `actions/preview.ts` -> `src/server/sync/store-sync.ts`, `actions/config.ts` -> `src/server/pricing/rules.ts`, `store-edit`/`duplicates` Woo calls -> `src/server/woo/`; fix the two upward imports; move `src/components/catalog/drawer-data.ts` under `src/server/catalog/`; export admin Zod schemas. Register an `ops` registry scope with owner-facing kinds only (collection rule save/delete/run, pricing rules, taxonomy rules, publish, sync apply, store pull, SKU import, overrides), each with a preview (existing `dryRun` where present) and `admin` scope on blast-radius kinds (rebuild, resets). Move the sync-apply mutual exclusion into `applySync` on the `apply_audit` running row plus boot reconciliation. Add the missing reads (term margin, standing term actions, section history, rules, publish delta, feeds, orders).

**Weeks 5-8 (WordPress port, overlapping).** `MenuSource` port with fixture mirroring `VetrinaSource`; client over `adapters/http.ts` on the auth path chosen in week 1; `menuItemAdd/Move/Remove` kinds with CAS and before/after undo items; amend `docs/hub-and-plugin.md:121`. Acceptance: "create category X from these SKUs, feature it on the homepage, add it to the main menu" as three proposals confirmed by a human, all visible and undoable in Attività with `via: assistant` and actor recorded.

**Weeks 8-12 (durability).** Replace the operations runner's globalThis chain with a leased `jobs` table and worker identity; `service.ts:64`'s `queued` flag read from the table; `recover()` reclaims only expired leases. After this, a UI deploy no longer interrupts a running sale, and B becomes a free choice rather than a need.

## Risks and unknowns

- **Human-confirm enforcement is new design**, not plumbing. If the owner wants to confirm from the phone, the Authelia-signed Vetrina sheet is the channel; if from a chat client, a separate confirm-scoped credential is needed. This must not be skipped; it is the one place where "it already works" is a trap.
- **Admin scope creep**: 73 exports is a long list. Register owner-facing writes only; leave debug/export/health as UI.
- **MCP behind Cloudflare**: long-lived streams hit the same 100-second ceiling as `apply-job.ts` documents. Keep tool responses short and poll `/operations/{id}`; do not stream long operations.
- **WP menu REST quirks** (locations, auto-add) are unknown until a fixture is built from a real site; the plugin-route path avoids `wp/v2` entirely but requires plugin releases.
- **Test coverage of the API glue is thin** (5 e2e tests, 0 unit tests); the week-1 tests must land before a real model is connected.
- **Unknown**: whether the owner ever wants in-process multi-tenancy. If yes, none of A/B is sufficient (no shop column in 24 tables, `site-guard.ts`), and that is the only scenario in which C's persistence rebuild earns its cost. One Hub per shop with one MCP token per shop is adequate today.
